Classification of exceptional Jacobi polynomials
María Ángeles García-Ferrero, David Gómez-Ullate, Robert Milson

Abstract
. We provide a full classification scheme for exceptional Jacobi operators and polynomials. The classification contains six degeneracy classes according to whether α , β \alpha,\beta or α ± β \alpha\pm\beta assume integer values. Exceptional Jacobi operators are in one-to-one correspondence with spectral diagrams, a combinatorial object that describes the quasi-rational eigenfunctions of the operator and their asymptotic behaviour at the endpoints of ( − 1 , 1 ) (-1,1) . With a convenient indexing scheme for spectral diagrams, explicit Wronskian and integral construction formulas are given to build the exceptional operators and polynomials from the information encoded in the spectral diagram. In the fully degenerate class α , β ∈ ℕ 0 \alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0} there exist exceptional Jacobi operators with an arbitrary number of continuous parameters. The classification result is achieved by a careful description of all possible rational Darboux transformations that can be performed on exceptional Jacobi operators.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
42C05; 33C45; 34L10; 34A05; 34L25

1.
Introduction

Classical orthogonal polynomials are orthogonal polynomial bases of an
\rm L^{2}
space that are also eigenfunctions of a Sturm-Liouville problem
[
4
,
52
]
. Since the mid-nineteenth century until today, they appear in ubiquitous applications in mathematical physics, approximation theory, numerical analysis, or statistics, among other fields.
Relaxing the requirement that the polynomial eigenfunctions contain every possible degree has led to the discovery of
exceptional orthogonal polynomials
, which form also complete bases of
\rm L^{2}
spaces spanned by polynomial eigenfunctions of Sturm-Liouville problems, and thus constitute a very natural generalization of classical orthogonal polynomials. Although the terms might sound paradoxical,
exceptional
orthogonal polynomial systems are thus the
general
class of Sturm-Liouville problems with polynomial eigenfunctions, and they include classical polynomial systems as a particular case.

Some isolated examples existed earlier in the mathematical physics literature
[
15
]
, but the systematic study of exceptional orthogonal polynomials started in 2009 with the publication of two papers by Gómez-Ullate, Kamran and Milson
[
34
,
35
]
. Shortly after, Quesne realized that exceptional polynomials could be built via Darboux transformations,
[
66
]
, which are in fact a fundamental element of the theory. Sasaki and Odake iterated the Darboux transformation construction to derive more general families indexed by a set of integers,
[
62
,
63
]
. Durán showed an alternative construction based on perturbing measures for discrete classical orthogonal polynomials ensued by a limiting process, and he was able to extend the notion to exceptional discrete families of Charlier, Meixner and dual Hahn types,
[
17
,
18
,
20
]
. In his doctoral thesis, Bonneux performed an exhaustive enumeration of all examples known by then, showing how exceptional Hermite polynomials were indexed by a single partition
[
29
]
, and exceptional Laguerre and Jacobi polynomials were indexed by two partitions,
[
20
,
7
,
5
]
.

The mathematical properties of exceptional orthogonal polynomials are in certain ways very similar to their classical counterparts, and recent research has focused on the asymptotics and distribution of their zeros
[
39
,
50
,
68
]
, equilibrium measures and electrostatic interpretation
[
44
,
45
]
, polynomial symmetry algebras
[
56
]
, recurrence relations
[
61
,
19
,
58
,
38
]
, and Wronskian determinants
[
14
,
31
,
32
,
6
,
64
]
, among others.
The notion of exceptional polynomials, originally derived for scalar orthogonal polynomials on the line, has also been extended to discrete polynomials
[
17
,
18
,
20
,
59
]
, matrix-valued polynomials
[
9
,
49
]
, Laurent polynomials
[
54
,
55
]
, Bannai-Ito polynomials
[
53
]
or Dunkl operators
[
67
]
.
Perhaps thanks to their closeness to classical orthogonal polynomials, exceptional polynomials have been applied in diverse fields, including quantum mechanics, quantum walks
[
60
]
, bispectrality and the time-band limiting problem
[
10
,
11
]
, rational solutions to Painlevé systems
[
12
,
33
]
, superintegrable systems
[
65
,
71
,
57
,
43
]
, coherent states in quantum optics
[
13
]
, financial mathematics
[
72
]
, diffusion processes
[
51
]
and statistics of DNA mutation
[
42
]
.

The question of achieving a complete characterization and classification of exceptional orthogonal polynomials has been one of the main goals throughout the past 15 years. A key step in this task is the Bochner-type theorem that asserts that every exceptional polynomial family is necessarily connected to a classical family by a finite set of Darboux transformations,
[
24
]
, proving a conjecture previously formulated in
[
36
]
. The remaining logical step in the classification program becomes then to describe the complete catalogue of all Darboux transformations that preserve the polynomial character of the eigenfunctions (also called rational Darboux transformations), and to introduce a suitable combinatorial scheme to describe all such possible iterated transformations.
When all elements seemed to be in place to achieve a complete classification, the list of rational Darboux transformations in the desired catalogue for the Jacobi operator had to be enlarged with new additions.

It was already known to Grandati and Quesne
[
40
]
that for some particular parameter values a degenerate situation could arise that allows new rational Darboux transformations. Indeed, consider the Jacobi eigenvalue equation

(1)

(x^{2}-1)y^{\prime\prime}-[\beta-\alpha-(\alpha+\beta+2)]y^{\prime}=n(n+\alpha+\beta+1)y.

When both
\alpha
and
\beta
assume integer values, Calogero and Yi
[
8
]
showed that equation (
1
) has two linearly independent polynomial eigenfunctions at certain eigenvalues, which they called para-Jacobi polynomials. This fact can be used to introduce real parameters in Darboux transformations by considering linear combinations of the two polynomial eigenfunctions as factorizing function
[
2
]
. Grandati and Quesne have recently extended their approach in
[
40
]
to include multi-step Darboux transformations with para-Jacobi polynomial seed functions,
[
41
]
. Another way of introducing continuous parameters in Darboux transformations is via
confluent Darboux transformations
, which are two consecutive transformations at the same energy value. Confluent Darboux transformations were known in the field of mathematical physics and integrable systems
[
1
,
69
,
48
,
28
]
, but they had not been systematically applied in the context of exceptional orthogonal polynomials. In this paper we show that these two transformations are strongly related. In
[
25
]
it was shown how to build exceptional Legendre polynomials with an arbitrary number of real parameters, which are moreover interpreted as a continuous deformation from the classical Legendre class, since turning these real parameters to zero recovers the classical Legendre polynomials. In
[
27
]
the construction was extended to the Gegenbauer class, and new integral formulas were introduced that are closely related to the inverse scattering method of Gel’fand-Levitan-Marchenko in soliton theory and integrable systems (except in this polynomial setting the spectrum only contains bound states, no scattering data). Durán has also studied this new class of exceptional polynomials with continuous parameters
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
, by adapting his construction based on perturbing discrete measures to include these degenerate cases. The equivalence of Durán’s approach and the one described in this paper has not yet been established, but it will be communicated in a forthcoming paper.

In connection with their group theoretic study of deformations of Calogero–Moser operators with rational potentials, in
[
3
]
, Berest and Chalykh have noted that when the non-degeneracy condition

\alpha,\beta,\alpha\pm\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
holds, rational Darboux transformations of Jacobi operators are completely described by Wronskians indexed by 2 partitions, i.e., the class of exceptional Jacobi operators described by Bonneux
[
7
]
. They have also observed that in degenerate cases, when that condition does not hold, other Darboux transformations exist which produce new locus configurations. This is precisely the class that is fully described in this paper.

The main result of this paper is a complete classification of exceptional Jacobi operators and polynomials, which includes both the class described by Bonneux and the new degenerate families with continuous parameters. The keys to achieve a complete classification are

(i)

the characterization result
[
24
]
that states that all exceptional Jacobi operators must be connected by rational Darboux transformations to a classical Jacobi operator,

(ii)

the description of all possible rational Darboux transformations that can be successively applied at each step.

The
quasi-rational spectrum
is the set of eigenvalues for which an exceptional Jacobi operator (see Definition
2.2
) has a quasi-rational eigenfunction (an eigenfunction whose log-derivative is a rational function).
A rational Darboux transformation maps an exceptional Jacobi operator into another exceptional Jacobi operator, but the quasi-rational spectrum remains invariant under such transformation. The number and asymptotic behaviour of quasi-rational eigenfunctions at each eigenvalue can be labelled by a set of symbols, that we call
asymptotic labels
.
The collection of all those symbols at every point in the quasi-rational spectrum defines a
spectral diagram
.
Non-degenerate exceptional Jacobi operators are indexed by two Maya diagrams, but in order to describe the new degenerate exceptional families, Maya diagrams need to be generalized to spectral diagrams. To every exceptional Jacobi operator (whether it is generic or degenerate) we can associate a spectral diagram. A one-step Darboux transformation applied on it will render another exceptional Jacobi operator whose spectral diagram differs from the previous one on just a single asymptotic label. The set of all possible changes on an asymptotic label is called the
flip alphabet
, and it effectively describes point (ii) above. We can now informally state the main results of this paper:

Main results:

(1)

Exceptional Jacobi operators and spectral diagrams are in one-to-one correspondence.

(2)

Depending on the values of
\alpha,\beta
six different degeneracy classes (G,A,B,C,CB,D) can be given, and each class has its own flip alphabet and rules to construct spectral diagrams.

(3)

In the fully-degenerate class (D)
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the spectral diagram does not fully determine a single exceptional Jacobi operator, but a
k
-continuous parameter family, where
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is an arbitrary positive integer. To specify a single operator, the norms of
k
polynomial eigenfunctions need to be specified too.

(4)

For each of the six degeneracy classes, explicit Wronskian and integral formulas are given to construct both the operator and the eigenfunctions from the information encoded in the diagram.

The paper is structured as follows. Quasi-rational
spectrum and eigenfunctions, asymptotic symbols, spectral diagrams and
the six degeneracy classes are introduced Section
2
. They
are all the necessary concepts and definitions needed to state the
main theorems of the paper, which is done at the end of the
section. In Section
3
we introduce a labelling system for
spectral diagrams in each of the six classes, and construction
formulas for the exceptional Jacobi operator and polynomials. Some
explicit examples are given in Section
5
. No reference to Darboux
transformations is needed to state the main results, but of course
they are the key element that allows to understand and prove the
results of Sections
2
and
3
. For this
reason, we revise the formal theory of Darboux transformations for
second order differential operators in Section
4
, which
includes confluent Darboux transformations. In
Section
6
we study the spectral diagrams and spectral
functions of classical Jacobi operators. These simple spectral
diagrams of classical operators can be regarded as an empty canvas on which
to “paint” the richer spectral diagrams that describe exceptional
Jacobi operators. The allowed “painting operations” are the Darboux
transformations and corresponding flip alphabets, which are described
in detail for each degeneracy class in Section
7
. The remaining
proofs to complete the main results stated in Section
2

are given in Section
8
. Since this paper is already
quite long and technical, two related questions are postponed to a
forthcoming publication.

The first one concerns equivalence between different representations
to describe the same exceptional Jacobi operator. For example, for the
subclass where
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, spectral diagrams encode the set of
Darboux transformations that need to be applied on the classical
Jacobi operator with
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0),(1,0)
or
(0,1)
. It
would also be possible to start from a different classical Jacobi
operator and thus the sequence of Darboux transformations would
differ. The reason for this freedom is that there exist

primitive
Darboux transformations that connect classical
Jacobi operators with different values of
(\alpha,\beta)
among
themselves. This set of alternative constructions, which show the
equivalence between Duran’s approach and the one explained here, will
be fully explained.

The second question involves the
regularity
of the
exceptional operator, or equivalently, in which cases can we assure
that the exceptional Jacobi operator defines an orthogonal polynomial
system with a well defined and positive measure supported on

(-1,1)
. This problem has an elegant answer that is stated in Theorem

2.13
but whose proof is postponed to a forthcoming
publication.

2.
Preliminaries and main results

2.1.
Base notation and definitions.

We begin by fixing some notation. Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathbb{R}[x]
denote the ring of
univariate, real polynomials,
\mathcal{P}^{\times}
the subset of non-zero
polynomials and
\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
the subset of polynomials that do not vanish
at
\pm 1
. Let
\mathcal{Q}=\mathbb{R}(x)
denote the field of rational functions,
and
{\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}}\subset\mathcal{Q}
the subspace of rational functions that are
regular at
x=\pm 1
.
We define a
quasi-rational (qr) function
to
be a function
\phi(x)
such that its log-derivative

w(x)=\phi^{\prime}(x)/\phi(x)
is a rational function. Let

(2)

\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}=(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}{\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}},\quad\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R},

be the vector space consisting of qr-expressions of the form

q(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}
, with
q\in{\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}}
. For

q\in\mathcal{Q},\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the notation

\rho(x)=\int q(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}

will signify that

\rho(x)
is the unique quasi-rational function whose derivative is
the indicated integrand
1
1

1

Since the primitive
\rho(x)
is
assumed to be quasi-rational with

\rho^{\prime}(x)=q(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{b}
, then necessarily

q(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}
has vanishing residues away from

x=\pm 1
. Thus, the integral in question is single-valued by
assumption and does not produce logarithmic singularities.
. The
same notation is in place if one of
\alpha,\beta
is a non-negative
integer but the other isn’t. If
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the notation

\rho(x)=\int_{-1}^{x}q(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}

will signify that

\rho(x)
is the unique rational function that vanishes at
x=-1
and
whose derivative is the indicated rational integrand
2
2

2

In
the subcase where
q(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}
is integrable on

[-1,1]
with vanishing residues there, we have

\rho(x)=\int_{-1}^{x}q(u)(1-u)^{\alpha}(1+u)^{\beta}du.

This
observation motivates our particular choice of notation. However, in
general,
\int_{-1}^{x}
should be regarded as a kind of definite
integral operator, albeit one that is constrained to produce
primitives that vanish at
x=-1
.
.

The purpose of this paper is to provide a classification of exceptional
Jacobi operators and polynomials. We define the former as follows.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\operatorname{Diff}(\mathcal{Q})
denote the set of non-zero differential operator
with rational coefficients and let
\operatorname{Diff}_{n}(\mathcal{Q})
be the subset of
such operators having order
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. For
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\times}
, let

\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
denote the set of second order operators
of the form

(3)

T=pD^{2}+qD+r,\quad q,r\in\mathcal{Q}.

We define an
eigenpolynomial
of
T
to be a non-zero
polynomial solution
P\in\mathcal{P}^{\times}
of the eigenvalue equation

TP=\lambda P
where
\lambda
is a constant. We call the corresponding

\lambda
a
polynomial eigenvalue
of
T
and refer to the set

\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}(T)
of all such
\lambda
as the
polynomial spectrum
of

T
. We define the
degree set

I=I(T)
to be the set of the
degrees of the eigenpolynomials of
T
and index the latter as

\{P_{n}\}_{n\in I}
, where
P_{n}(x)
is an eigenpolynomial such that

\deg P=n
.

Definition 2.2
.

A second order differential operator

T\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
3
3

3

One can show that if
T
admits
infinitely many eigenpolynomials, then necessarily
p(x)
is a
polynomial, and
q(x),r(x)
are rational functions
[
37
]
.
Therefore no generality is lost making the above assumptions
regarding the coefficients
p,q,r
.
is an
exceptional
operator
if it has eigenpolynomials for all but finitely many
degrees, i.e. if
\mathbb{N}_{0}\setminus I
has finite cardinality.
T
is an

exceptional Jacobi operator
if
p(x)=x^{2}-1
.

The fundamental structure theorem of exceptional operators proved in

[
24
, Theorem 5.3]
imposes a very specific structure for an
exceptional Jacobi operator. The theorem below is a
version of that result. The proof can be found in Section 7.

Theorem 2.3
.

Every exceptional Jacobi operator is related by a gauge
transformation and a spectral shift to an operator of the form

(4)

{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)=(x^{2}-1)\left(D_{x}^{2}-2\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}D_{x}+\frac{\tau^{\prime\prime}}{\tau}+\eta(x;\alpha,\beta)\left(D_{x}-\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}\right)\right)+2x\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}

where

(5)

\eta(x;\alpha,\beta):=\frac{\alpha+1}{x-1}+\frac{\beta+1}{x+1},\quad\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R},

and
\tau\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
, i.e.
\tau
a polynomial such that
\tau(\pm 1)\neq 0
.
Moreover, in that case, the
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
introduced in (
4
)
is itself an exceptional Jacobi operator.

As per Definition 5.1 of
[
24
]
, an operator

{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\tau\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
will be said to be in the
natural
gauge (ng)
. In light of the above structure theorem, a classification
of exceptional Jacobi operators does not lose generality by focusing
on operators in the natural gauge.

In general, not every choice of
(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
in
(
4
) will constitute an exceptional Jacobi operator,
so the classification problem can be understood as characterizing all
of the
(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
tuples such that
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is an
exceptional operator.
So far, exceptional Jacobi operators (
4
) have been
defined as differential expressions. A subset of these expressions
will give rise to exceptional Jacobi orthogonal polynomial systems; this
happens precisely when the weight function determined by
(
4
) defines a positive definite measure with finite
moments of all orders. This requires the notion of regularity of the
weight, which we define below.

Definition 2.4
.

An exceptional Jacobi operator
T={T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is
regular

if
\tau(x)
does not vanish for
x\in[-1,1]
and if
\alpha,\beta>-1
.
If regularity holds, then we will refer to the family

\{P_{n}\}_{n\in I}
as an
exceptional Jacobi Orthogonal Polynomial system
.

The motivation for the second definition is that, in the regular case,

(6)

\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{P_{m}(x)P_{n}(x)}{\tau(x)^{2}}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}=\nu_{n}\,\delta_{mn},\quad m,n\in I(T),

with
\nu_{n}>0
for every
n
. As asserted in Theorem

2.13
, whose proof will be given elsewhere, the above
definition ensures that an exceptional Jacobi OP system is complete in
the corresponding weighted Hilbert space.

2.2.
Classical operators and polynomials.

The classical Jacobi operator

(7)

T(a,b):=(x^{2}-1)\left(D_{x}^{2}+\eta(x;a,b)\,D_{x}\right),\quad a,b\in\mathbb{R},

is a special case of definition (
4
), corresponding to

\tau=1
. The classical Jacobi polynomials are

(8)

\displaystyle P_{n}(x;a,b)

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-n}}{n!}(x-1)^{-a}(x+1)^{-b}D_{x}^{n}\left((x-1)^{n+a}(x+1)^{n+b}\right)

(9)

\displaystyle=2^{-n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n+a}{n-k}\binom{n+b}{k}(x-1)^{k}(x+1)^{n-k},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

The leading
n
th degree coefficient of
P_{n}(x;a,b)
is given by

(n+a+b+1)_{n}2^{-n}/n!
, where
(t)_{n}=t(t+1)\cdots(t+n-1)
denotes
the Pochhammer symbol
4
4

4

Also known as the rising
factorial.
. Thus, if
(n+a+b+1)_{n}\neq 0
, then we can define the
monic form of the classical polynomials

(10)

\pi_{n}(x;a,b):=\frac{2^{n}n!}{(n+a+b+1)_{n}}P_{n}(x;a,b).

These may also be defined as the monic polynomial solutions of the
classical Jacobi eigenvalue equation

(11)

T(a,b)\pi_{n}=\lambda_{n}\pi_{n},\quad\text{where }n=\deg\pi_{n},

Since

(12)

P_{n}(-1;a,b)=\frac{(-1)^{n}(b+1)_{n}}{n!},

an alternative formulation is to make the assumption that

b\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
and to define the
\pi_{n}(x;a,b)
as the unique
polynomial solutions of (
11
) subject to the initial
condition

\pi_{n}(-1;a,b)=\frac{(-2)^{n}(b+1)_{n}}{(1+a+b+n)_{n}}.

Necessarily, the allowed degrees are constrained by

(a+b+1+n)_{n}\neq 0
, while the eigenvalues have the form

\lambda_{n}=n(n+a+b+1)
.

If

a,b>-1
then we also have the classical orthogonality relation

(13)

\int_{-1}^{1}\pi_{m}(x;a,b)\pi_{n}(x;a,b)(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}dx=\nu(n;a,b)\delta_{mn},\quad m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

where

(14)

\nu(z;a,b):=2^{1+a+b+2z}\frac{\Gamma(z+1)\Gamma(a+b+z+1)\Gamma(a+z+1)\Gamma(b+z+1)}{\Gamma(a+b+2z+1)\Gamma(a+b+2z+2)}.

Using the Legendre duplication formula, the above expression may also
be given as

(15)

\nu(z;a,b)=\frac{\pi}{2^{2z+a+b}}\frac{\Gamma(z+1)\Gamma(z+1+a)\Gamma(z+1+b)\Gamma(z+1+a+b)}{\Gamma(z+(a+b+1)/2)\Gamma(z+(a+b+2)/2)^{2}\Gamma(z+(a+b+3)/2)}.

The latter expression readily gives the asymptotic behaviour of the
classical norms.

By direct inspection of (
9
), the classical operator

T(a,b)
satisfies the definition of an exceptional operator for all
values of
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, as proved in
[
24
]
,

all
exceptional operators can be obtained by a sequence of
rational Darboux transformations from a classical
T(a,b)
.

2.3.
The rational gauge

Let
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be as defined in (
4
). Let us
consider a gauge transformation of this operator with gauge factor

\tau
by setting

(16)

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta):=\mathcal{M}(\tau^{-1})\circ{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)\circ\mathcal{M}(\tau),

where
\mathcal{M}(f)
denotes the formal multiplication operator

y\mapsto fy
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be as in (
16
) and
T(\alpha,\beta)

the classical Jacobi operator as per (
7
). Then,

(17)

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)=T(\alpha,\beta)+2(x^{2}-1)u^{\prime}+2xu,\quad u=\tau^{\prime}/\tau.

Proof.

The proof follows by a straight-forward calculation. See also
(
101
) below.
∎

Definition 2.6
.

If
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is an exceptional operator in the natural gauge,
as per Definition
2.2
, we will say that
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is
an exceptional Jacobi operator in the
rational gauge (rg)
.
Moreover, we will call
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)

regular
if the corresponding
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is regular.

The operator
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}
has no longer polynomial but rational
eigenfunctions, which are polynomial up to a common factor. This
motivates to enlarge the eigenpolynomial notion introduced in
Definition
2.1
in the following manner.

Definition 2.7
.

We define a
quasi-polynomial eigenfunction
of an operator
T\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
to be a
rational solution
\pi(x)
of the eigenvalue equation

T\pi=\lambda\pi
such that
\pi(x)
has no zeros or poles at the
zeros of
p(x)
. We will refer to the corresponding
\lambda
as a

quasi-polynomial eigenvalue
. Going forward, we define the degree of
a rational function as the difference of degrees of the numerator
and denominator and let
I_{1}(T)
denote the set of the degrees of
the quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions of
T
. We will also use

\sigma_{1}(T)
to denote the set of quasi-polynomial eigenvalues and
refer to this set as the
quasi-polynomial spectrum
5
5

5

The
reason for the subscript in
\sigma_{1}(T),I_{1}(T)
will be
clear later.
of
T
.

The following result, which will be proved in Section

8.2
, characterizes the quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions of
an exceptional
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be an exceptional Jacobi operator in the
natural gauge and

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\tau\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm},\;\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
the
corresponding exceptional Jacobi operator in the rational
gauge, as shown in (
4
) and (
17
),
respectively. Every quasi-polynomial eigenfunction of
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}
has
the form

(18)

\pi_{k}(x)=\frac{P_{n}(x)}{\tau(x)},\quad k\in I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}}),\quad k=n-N,

where
P_{n}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
is an eigenpolynomial of
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}
of degree
n

and
N=\deg\tau
. Moreover, if
\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, then

\displaystyle I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})

\displaystyle=I({T_{\mathrm{ng}}})-N

(19)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}({T_{\mathrm{ng}}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})=\{k(k+\alpha+\beta+1)\colon k\in I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})\}.

In light of the above result, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 2.9
.

We refer to the quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions of an exceptional Jacobi operator in the rational gauge,

\pi_{k}(x),\;k\in I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})
, as
exceptional Jacobi
quasi-polynomials
and to the set of its degrees,
I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})
, as the
quasi-polynomial index set
.

The classification problem for exceptional Jacobi operators evidently
reduces to the classification of exceptional operators in the rational
gauge. It is in this form that we will pursue the classification
question. The reason behind this choice is that the rational gauge
has some important technical advantages over the natural gauge such as
a simplified relation between the polynomial spectrum and the degree
set exhibited in (
19
).

Another advantage of the rational
gauge is that, in the regular case, the orthogonality relation
(
6
) takes the classical form:

(20)

\int_{-1}^{1}\pi_{i}(x)\pi_{j}(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}dx=\nu_{i}\,\delta_{ij},\quad i,j\in I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}}),

with
\nu_{i}>0
for every
i
.

As specified in (
18
),

\tau(x),\{P_{n}(x)\}_{n\in I({T_{\mathrm{ng}}})},\,\{\pi_{k}(x)\}_{k\in I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})}

and the corresponding norms
\{\nu_{i}\}_{i\in I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})}
are only
defined up to scalar multiplication. In order to remedy this
ambiguity, one has to introduce some notion of normalization.

Definition 2.10
.

Given a family of exceptional Jacobi quasi-polynomials
\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}
we will refer to the sequence of constants
\pi_{i}(-1),\;i\in I_{1}
as a
choice of normalization
.

Evidently, a choice of normalization is required in order to fix the
quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions and their norms. One possibility
would be to require that
\pi_{i}(-1)=1
for all
i\in I_{1}
. However,
for various technical reasons we will opt for a normalization that
produces monic quasi-polynomials; this means that is the leading
coefficient of the numerator
P_{n}(x)
matches the leading coefficient
of the denominator
\tau(x)
. Some families of exceptional
polynomials feature a finite number of continuous deformation
parameters. In such cases, we will utilize a normalization that
results in asymptotically monic eigenfunctions; that is the
exceptional quasi-polynomials are monic in the limit where these
parameters go to infinity. The concrete details and explicit
definitions will be given in Section
3.5
.

2.4.
Generalized orthogonality.

If
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is not regular, then the norm definition

(21)

\nu_{i}:=\int_{-1}^{1}\pi_{i}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}dx,\quad i\in I_{1}(T)

is not valid because the right-hand side is a divergent integral.
There is a way to generalize the right side of (
21
) so as
to formally define
\nu_{i}
for all exceptional operators for which

\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
. The construction
relies on the following result, which is proved
6
6

6

There is an
alternative approach to generalized weights based on deformed
contours
[
47
]

[
46
]
.
in Section
8.7
.

Going forward, set

(22)

\nu(a,b):=\nu(0;a,b)=2^{1+a+b}\frac{\Gamma(a+1)\Gamma(b+1)}{\Gamma(a+b+2)}=2^{1+a+b}B(a+1,b+1),

where the latter is the usual beta function.
For
a,b>-1
, we have

\nu(a,b)=\int_{-1}^{1}(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}dx;

so
\nu(a,b)
may be
regarded as the analytic continuation of the basic weight integral.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta,\alpha+\beta+1\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
be an
exceptional operator and
\pi_{i}(x),\,i\in I_{1}
corresponding Jacobi
quasi-polynomials. Then, there exist
7
7

7

The value of these
constants depends on the choice of normalization.
constants

\nu_{i}
such that

(23)

\int\left(\pi_{i}(x)\pi_{j}(x)-\frac{\nu_{i}\,\delta_{ij}}{\nu(\alpha,\beta)}\right)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1},\quad i,j\in I_{1}(T).

If
\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, but
m:=\alpha+\beta+1\in\mathbb{Z}
then there exist
constants
\nu_{i},\;i\in I_{1}
such that

(24)

\int\left(\pi_{i}(x)\pi_{j}(x)-(1+x)^{-m}\frac{\nu_{i}\,\delta_{ij}}{\nu(\alpha,-1-\alpha)}\right)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1},\quad i,j\in I_{1}(T).

When
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}
is regular, then (
23
) implies the
usual orthogonality condition (
20
). Thus, the above
assertion extends the usual notion of a norm to the case when
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}

is not regular. For
\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, set
m:=\alpha+\beta+1
and
observe that
\nu(\alpha,\beta)=0
precisely when
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
. Thus,
relation (
23
) does not make sense in this case, and
one has to use (
24
) as the definition of
\nu_{i}
. If

\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
but
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then the
\nu_{i}
defined by
(
23
) also satisfies relation (
24
). It will
therefore be convenient to take (
24
) as the definition
of the formal norm
\nu_{i}
for all cases where
\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}

but
\alpha+\beta+1\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 2.12
.

Below, we establish explicit formulas for the norms of exceptional
Jacobi polynomials relative to monic normalization. From these
formulas, it will be seen that the constants
\nu_{i}/\nu(\alpha,\beta)

have a rational dependence on
\alpha,\beta
. Indeed, if

\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{Q}
are rational, then the norm
\nu_{i}
is a rational
number times
\nu(\alpha,\beta)
. In the subcase where
\alpha+\beta+1\in\mathbb{Z}
,
we have

\nu(\alpha,-1-\alpha)=\Gamma(-\alpha)\Gamma(1+\alpha)=-\pi\csc(\pi\alpha).

In this case also, for each
i\in I_{1}(T)
, the constants

\nu_{i}/\nu(\alpha,-1-\alpha)
are also rational in
\alpha
. Thus, if

\alpha\in\mathcal{Q}
is rational, then
\nu_{i}
is a rational number times

\pi\csc(\pi\alpha)
.

An exceptional Jacobi operator defines a well-behaved orthogonal
polynomial system (OPS) provided it is regular (in the sense of
Definition
2.4
). In order to complete the task of
classifying all exceptional Jacobi OPS we need to:

(1)

characterize the tuples

(\tau,\alpha,\beta)
that define an exceptional Jacobi operator
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
,

(2)

select those tuples
(\tau,\alpha,\beta)
for which
T
is regular.

The first step of this program is achieved in this paper. The
regularity question will be dealt with detail in a forthcoming
publication, but we state as an announcement the main criterion that
allows to determine when a given
(\tau,\alpha,\beta)
defines a regular
operator, which essentially amounts to the positivity of all the
norms.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta,\alpha+\beta+1\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
be an
exceptional Jacobi operator,
\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}
, the
corresponding exceptional quasi-polynomials defined relative to some
choice of normalization. Then, each
\nu_{i},i\in I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})
, as
defined above, is a finite, non-zero real number.
The operator
T
is regular if and only if
0<\nu_{i}<\infty
for all

i\in I_{1}(T)
. Moreover, if regularity holds then the
quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions are also a complete
\mathrm{L}^{2}
basis in
the corresponding weighted Hilbert space.

Note that even though the specific value of the
\nu_{i},\;i\in I_{1}

depends on a choice of normalization, the sign of the
\nu_{i},\;i\in I_{1}
is independent of this choice.

2.5.
Quasi-rational spectrum.

Most of the literature on the theory of exceptional polynomials and
operators on the past ten years has focused on the polynomial spectrum
and eigenfunctions. It is now clear that a complete, systematic
approach needs to consider the larger class of quasi-rational
eigenfunctions. Indeed, as we shall see, a proper characterization of
the quasi-rational eigenfunctions of a given exceptional operator and
their asymptotic behaviour essentially specifies the operator
uniquely. For this reason, we introduce in the following sections the
two key concepts of
quasi-rational spectrum
and
spectral
diagram
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
T\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
be a second-order operator. A quasi-rational
solution
\phi(x)
of the eigenvalue problem
T\phi=\lambda\phi

will be called a
qr-eigenfunction
of
T
and the corresponding

\lambda
a
qr-eigenvalue
. Let
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)
denote the set of all qr-eigenvalues of
T
. We will refer to
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)
as the

qr-spectrum
of
T
.

Definition 2.15
.

For
T=pD^{2}+qD+r\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
, let
\mathrm{R}[T]:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}
be the non-linear operator
with action

(25)

\mathrm{R}[T]w:=p(w^{\prime}+w^{2})+qw+r,\quad w\in\mathcal{Q}.

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, define
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T):=\{w\in\mathcal{Q}:\mathrm{R}[T]w=\lambda\}

to be the set of rational solutions of the indicated Ricatti-type
equation.

Observe that if
w=\phi^{\prime}/\phi
, where
\phi(x)
is quasi-rational, then

(26)

\mathrm{R}[T]w=(T\phi)/\phi.

As an immediate consequence of this, we have the following identification.

Proposition 2.16
.

The qr-spectrum of
T\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
can be characterized as

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}:\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)\neq\emptyset\}.

In the description of the qr-spectrum and eigenfunctions of an
exceptional Jacobi operator, it will be particularly important to
analyze the asymptotic behaviour of the qr-eigenfunctions at the
endpoints of the interval
[-1,1]
. Accordingly, we divide the qr-eigenfunctions of
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
into four types according to
the behaviour at
x=\pm 1
.

Definition 2.17
.

We define the
asymptotic type
of a rational function
w(x)

to be, respectively
1,2,3,4
according to whether it is regular at

x=\pm 1
, has poles at both endpoints, has a pole at
x=+1
but not
at
x=-1
, or has a pole at
x=-1
but not at
x=+1
. We define the

asymptotic type
of a qr-function
\phi(x)
to be the
asymptotic type of the corresponding log-derivative

w=\phi^{\prime}/\phi
. We define the
exponential order
of
\phi(x)
to be
the degree
of
w(x)
and the
degree
of
\phi(x)
,
\deg\phi
, to be the
residue at infinity of
w(z)
.

The four asymptotic types of qr-eigenfunctions of an exceptional
Jacobi operator are shown in Table
1

below

\mu_{\imath}
is defined in

.

eigenfunctions fit the definition of a type 1 qr-function. Indeed, as
we now claim, the type 1 eigenfunctions are precisely the
quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions of an exceptional operator. The
following result is proved in Section
8.2
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be an
exceptional Jacobi operator. Let
\phi(x)
be a type

\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
qr-eigenfunction of

and let
\lambda
be

\hat{\tau}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
such that

(27)

\phi(x)=\mu_{\imath}(x;\alpha,\beta)\frac{\hat{\tau}(x)}{\tau(x)},

.
Moreover,

(28)

\lambda=\deg\phi(\deg\phi+\alpha+\beta+1),

where

\displaystyle\deg\phi

\displaystyle=\deg\mu_{\imath}+\deg\hat{\tau}-\deg\tau,

(29)

The existence of four
families of qr-eigenfunctions is closely related to the following
parameter symmetries of the classical and exceptional Jacobi
operators.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
\mu_{\imath},\;\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
be as in Table

1
, and let
\mathcal{M}(f):y\mapsto fy
denote the formal
multiplication operator. Then,

(30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mu_{2})^{-1}\circ{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)\circ\mathcal{M}(\mu_{2})

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,-\beta)-\alpha-\beta

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mu_{3})^{-1}\circ{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)\circ\mathcal{M}(\mu_{3})

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,\beta)-\alpha(\beta+1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mu_{4})^{-1}\circ{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)\circ\mathcal{M}(\mu_{4})

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,-\beta)-\beta(\alpha+1)

Proof.

By (
17
) it suffices to verify the above identities for
the subclass of classical Jacobi operators
T=T(\alpha,\beta)
in
(
7
), which follows from a straightforward
calculation.
∎

We now introduce an indexing scheme for the qr-eigenfunctions and
extend the definition of a quasi-polynomial spectrum to other quasi-rational types
as follows.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be an exceptional Jacobi operator. Let

\phi(x)
be an eigenfunction of type
\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
let
\deg\phi
be as above.
Relying on (
27
), we define the
index

k
of
\phi
to
be the degree of the
{\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}}
factor of
\phi
. To be more precise,

(31)

k=\deg\phi-\deg\mu_{\imath}=\deg\hat{\tau}-\deg\tau=\begin{cases}\deg\phi&\text{if }\imath=1\\
\deg\phi+\alpha+\beta&\text{if }\imath=2\\
\deg\phi+\alpha&\text{if }\imath=3\\
\deg\phi+\beta&\text{if }\imath=4\end{cases}.

Definition 2.21
.

For every
\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
we define the
index sets

I_{\imath}(T)
as the set of indices of
type
\imath
eigenfunctions. We label the corresponding qr-eigenfunctions as

\phi_{\imath k},\;k\in I_{\imath}(T)
, and we extended this notation to the rational functions

\pi_{\imath k}(x)
and polynomials
\hat{\tau}_{\imath k}(x)
as per Proposition

2.18
, so that

(32)

\displaystyle\phi_{\imath k}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\imath}\pi_{\imath k}=\mu_{\imath}\frac{\hat{\tau}_{\imath k}}{\tau},\quad k\in I_{\imath}(T),\quad\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}.

In a similar fashion, we label the corresponding qr-eigenvalues and spectra according to these four types:

(33)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\imath k}

\displaystyle:=\deg\phi_{\imath k}(\deg\phi_{\imath k}+\alpha+\beta+1),\;k\in I_{\imath}(T),

(34)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\imath}(T)

\displaystyle:=\{\lambda_{\imath k}:k\in I_{\imath}(T)\},

and we refer to
\lambda_{\imath k}\in\sigma_{\imath}
as a type
\imath

eigenvalue and
\sigma_{\imath}(T)
as the type
\imath
spectrum. For
the quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions we will also employ the
simplified notation
\pi_{k}=\pi_{1k},\;k\in I_{1}(T)
.

Since, by Proposition
2.18
, every qr-eigenfunction is
one of the above asymptotic types, we have

(35)

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)=\bigcup_{\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}}\sigma_{\imath}(T).

As a direct corollary of the definition (
31
) and
Proposition
2.19
we see that

(36)

\displaystyle I_{2}(T)

\displaystyle=I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,-\beta)),

\displaystyle I_{3}(T)

\displaystyle=I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,\beta)),

\displaystyle I_{4}(T)

\displaystyle=I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,-\beta)).

The typed spectra of an exceptional
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
can be
determined directly in terms of the index sets as follows. Set

(37)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(k;\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle:=k(k+\alpha+\beta+1),

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}(k;\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=(k-\alpha-\beta)(k+1)

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}(k;\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle:=(k-\alpha)(k+\beta+1)

\displaystyle\lambda_{4}(k;\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle:=(k-\beta)(k+\alpha+1)

The following result is not surprising, but we postpone the proof to
Section
8.2
.

Proposition 2.22
.

For
\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\},\;k\in I_{\imath}(T)
, we have

\lambda_{\imath k}=\lambda_{\imath}(k;\alpha,\beta)
.

Another virtue of the above indexing scheme is that, generically,

I_{1}
and
-I_{2}-1
partition
\mathbb{Z}
and can be conveniently visualized
as a Maya diagram. The same remark holds for
I_{3}
and
I_{4}
. Thus,
for generic exceptional Jacobi operators the qr-spectrum and
eigenfunctions can be represented in terms of two Maya diagrams

[
5
]
. This way of representing the qr-eigenvalues and
eigenfunctions is described in detail in Section
2.7
in terms
of a concept called the
spectral diagram
.

Regardless of how the qr-eigenfunctions are distributed into the four
types above, their union always has the same simple form. For

\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, define
9
9

9

The notation
f(I)
denotes a set
obtained by applying a function
f
to the elements of a set
I
.

(38)

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta):=\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z};\alpha,\beta)\cup\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z}-\alpha;\alpha,\beta),

The following result is proved in Section
8.3
.

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be an exceptional Jacobi operator, then its quasi-rational spectrum is given by

(39)

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)=\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta).

By way of illustration, the quasi-rational eigenfunctions and

.

2.6.
The generic, semi-degenerate and degenerate subclasses

A key observation is to note that for some values of
(\alpha,\beta)
, an
exceptional Jacobi operator
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
might have linearly
independent qr-eigenfunctions of different types at the same
qr-eigenvalue. Otherwise speaking, the union (
35
) is not
always a disjoint union. The study of these degenerate cases motivates
to introduce the following definitions.

Definition 2.24
.

If
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
contains exactly one element, we call
\lambda
a

simple
qr-eigenvalue. If it contains multiple elements, then we
speak of a
degenerate
qr-eigenvalue.

Generically, each qr-eigenvalue of an exceptional
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)

is simple and can therefore be uniquely labelled by the asymptotic
type of the corresponding qr-eigenfunction. However, if

\alpha,\beta
,
\alpha+\beta
and/or
\alpha-\beta
take integral values, then certain qr-eigenfunctions can coalesce to produce a degenerate eigenvalue. These
types of coalescence can occur due to various symmetries in the
qr-spectrum (
37
) and motivates the introduction of six
different classes.

Definition 2.25
.

According to the values of
\alpha,\beta
, we define the following six
distinct
degeneracy classes
of exceptional Jacobi operators:

•

Type G (Generic) if
\alpha,\beta,\alpha\pm\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
,

•

Type A (Semi-degenerate) if
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
or
if
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

•

Type B (Semi-degenerate) if
\alpha,\beta,\alpha+\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
but

\alpha-\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
,

•

Type C (Semi-degenerate) if
\alpha,\beta,\alpha-\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
but

\alpha+\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
,

•

Type CB (Semi-degenerate, generalized Chebyshev) if

\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
but
2\alpha,2\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
,

•

Type D (Degenerate) if
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

We will refer to operators in class G as being
generic
, to
operators in classes A,B,C,CB as being
semi-degenerate
, and
to operators in class D as being
degenerate
.

The two type A subclasses are connected by the transformation

x\mapsto-x,\;\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta
. Therefore, by Proposition

2.19
, there is no loss of generality in considering
only type A operators where
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
.

The following result identifies the possible qr-eigenvalues, according to Definition
2.24
, for each operator type.

Proposition 2.26
.

Let
\lambda\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)
be a qr-eigenvalue of an exceptional
Jacobi operator
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
. If
T
is generic, then

\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
contains exactly one element. If
T
is
semi-degenerate, then
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
contains either one or two
elements. If
T
is degenerate, then
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
contains either one
or infinitely many elements.

The result follows directly by
Proposition
7.18
, which uses the notion of asymptotic label
introduced in Definition
2.28
.

Remark 2.27
.

Since
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is a second order differential operator, its
eigenfunctions at any given
\lambda
span a 2-dimensional linear
subspace. We are concerned only with qr-eigenfunctions, so the three
possibilities in Proposition
2.26
describe the cases
where:

(1)

Only one eigenfunction is quasi-rational.

(2)

There are two linearly independent qr-eigenfunctions but their linear combination is not quasi-rational.

(3)

There is a 2-dimensional space of rational eigenfunctions.

2.7.
The spectral diagram.

In this section we introduce the fundamental object in the
classification of exceptional Jacobi operators. We first introduce a
collection of labels to describe the number and asymptotic type of the
qr-eigenfunctions of an exceptional Jacobi operator
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
at simple
and degenerate qr-eigenvalues.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be an exceptional Jacobi operator. For a
qr-eigenvalue
\lambda\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)
we define the
\lambda
-label
to be
one of the following 8 symbols:

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus,\boxasterisk,\boxdiv,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
, according
to the asymptotic types of the elements of
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
. The
meaning of these labels is indicated by Table
3
,
with an explicit definition below.

•

The first four labels correspond to a singleton
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)

containing a rational function of type 1,2,3,4, respectively.

•

The
\boxasterisk
label, a confluence of the
\boxtimes
and

\boxplus
symbols, indicates that
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
is a two element set
containing eigenfunctions of type 2 and 3.

•

The
\boxdiv
label, a confluence of the
\boxplus
and

\boxminus
symbols, indicates that
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
is a two element
set containing eigenfunctions of type 3 and 4.

•

The
\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes
label, a confluence of the
\boxcirc
and

\boxtimes
symbols, indicates that
\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
is a 2-element
set containing eigenfunctions of type 1 and 2.

•

Finally, the
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
label, a confluence of the

\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus
symbols, indicates that

\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
is an infinite set containing eigenfunctions of types
2,3 and 4 (the third case in Remark
2.27
).

We are now ready to introduce the fundamental object employed in the
classification of exceptional Jacobi operators.

Definition 2.29
.

The spectral diagram of an exceptional Jacobi operator

T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is the assignment of the corresponding

\lambda
-label to each
\lambda\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)
.

Thus, the spectral diagram of an exceptional Jacobi operator

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
includes the information on the asymptotic types
of all of its qr-eigenfunctions.

2.8.
The main results.

In this section we state the main results of this paper.
First of all, we see that the information included in the spectral diagram suffices
to completely determine the operator in all cases except in the fully
degenerate D class. This result will be proved in Section
8.7
.

Theorem 2.30
.

Let
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be an exceptional Jacobi operator belonging
to degeneracy classes G,A,B,C or CB. Then, the spectral diagram of

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
fully determines the operator.

In the D class, exceptional Jacobi operators can have an arbitrary
number of real parameters. Thus, within the D class, a complete
classification of class D operators requires an extension of the notion
of a spectral diagram.

Definition 2.31
.

We say that two exceptional Jacobi operators are
isospectral

if they have the same quasi-polynomial spectrum. We define an

isospectral deformation
to be a family of isospectral
exceptional operators in which the corresponding quasi-polynomial
norms
\nu_{i},\;i\in I_{1}
, relative to some choice of normalization,
assume an arbitrary non-zero value for a finite number of
i\in I_{1}

and are constant for the other indices. We will refer to the former
subset of
I_{1}
as the
support
of the isospectral
deformation.

Definition 2.32
.

For a given exceptional class D operator

T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and the corresponding index
set
I_{1}=I_{1}(T)
, let
10
10

10

See Remark
3.21
for the
motivation behind these definitions.

(40)

I_{1+}=\{i\in I_{1}:i\geq(\alpha+\beta+1)/2\},\quad I_{1-}=\{i\in I_{1}:i<(\alpha+\beta+1)/2\}.

An
extended spectral diagram
of
T
is a modification of the
spectral diagram where the eigenvalues of quasi-polynomials
eigenfunctions of degree
i
are labelled by
\boxcirc
if

i\in I_{1+}
and labelled by
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
if
i\in I_{1-}
. We will
refer to
\{\boxcirc,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}},\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},\boxplus,\boxminus\}
as
the
extended
type D
\lambda
-label set.

Theorem 2.33
.

Isospectral deformations do not exist outside the degenerate D
class. Within the D class, the support of an isospectral deformation
is
I_{1-}
. The latter is finite, but can have an arbitrarily
large cardinality. There is a one-to-one correspondence between
isospectral deformations and extended spectral diagrams. Within a
particular isospectral deformation family, the value of the norms

\nu_{i},\;i\in I_{1-}
fully determines the corresponding
exceptional operator.

The proofs of these results will be given in Section

8.7
.

Thanks to Theorem
2.30
and Theorem
2.33
, the
classification methodology of exceptional Jacobi operators reduces to
two principles: (i) a combinatorial description of all possible
spectral diagrams; and (ii) a procedure for mapping a given spectral
diagram into the corresponding exceptional operator (or family of
operators in the case of an extended diagram).

The class of exceptional operators admits a natural operation called a
rational Darboux transformation (RDT) that relates pairs of
exceptional operators. We postpone the discussion of RDTs until
Section
4.1
below. At this point, it suffices to note the
following fundamental result.

Theorem 2.34

(Theorem 1.2 of
[
24
]
)
.

Every exceptional
operator is related to a classical operator by a finite chain of
rational Darboux transformations.

As a consequence of Theorem
2.34
, we have the
following result, whose proof is given in Section
8.3
.

Theorem 2.35
.

The spectral diagram of every exceptional Jacobi operator is related
to a spectral diagram of a classical Jacobi operator by a spectral
shift and by finitely many label changes.

The combinatorial part of the classification then takes the following
simple shape: describe a certain canonical set of classical spectral
diagrams, and then devise a system of parameters for describing all
finite modifications of these canonical diagrams.

The sections that follow are devoted to such constructions on a
case-by-case basis for the GABCD degeneracy classes. Each section
concludes with an explicit procedure for converting the combinatorial
data into the corresponding exceptional operator and corresponding
quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions.

3.
The GABCD classification and corresponding construction
formulas

This section is the heart of the classification results announced
above. Each of the six degeneracy classes G,A,B,C,CB,D requires a
separate treatment, but the organizational scheme is the same for each
class.

•

Each degeneracy class features a system of parameters to
describe the spectral diagram of an exceptional operator as a finite
modification of a classical operator belonging to the same class.

•

In each case, there is a constructive procedure for converting
said parameters into the triple
(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
. This procedure
always involves Wronskian differential operators, but for the (A)
and (D) classes, there is a preliminary stage of the construction
based on integral operators and determinantal representations.

•

For each degeneracy class, there is an assertion to the effect
that this construction produces an exceptional operator in that class.

•

For each degeneracy class, there is a description of a canonical choice
of parameters that gives a unique description of every exceptional
operator in that class.

In essence, the parameters describe a chain of rational Darboux
transformations (RDTs) and confluent Darboux transformations (CDTs) that
connect a classical operator
T(a,b)
with the exceptional operator

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
; see Section
4.1
for details. In each
case, there is an explicit description of how these RDTs transform the
index sets of the classical
T(a,b)
into the index sets of the
exceptional
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
, and an enumeration of the label
flips that relate the classical and the exceptional spectral diagrams.

Each of the subsections begins with a technical description of the
relevant constructions and assertions. This is done with a minimum of
explanation in order to gather the technical material in a compact and
accessible fashion. For the same reason, the proofs are postponed
until Section
8
.
Namely, the proofs of the results of Section
3.1
can be found in Section
8.4
, those of Sections
3.2
,
3.3
,
3.4
in Section
8.5
, and lastly, those of Section
3.5
in Section
8.6
.

Each technical subsection is then accompanied by a more informal
discussion with illustrations and examples. These remarks should be
viewed as a kind of “user manual” for spectral diagrams of that
class. They explain the nature of the degeneracy that gives rise to
the class in question and motivate the relevant constructions. A
useful approach for reading these sections is therefore to skim the
technical details and to pass quickly to the “user manual”. Having
perused these expositions, the reader can then return to the technical
details with an improved understanding and motivation.

Class G,B,C,CB operators do not require CDTs for their construction.
Essentially, the parameter sets for these classes consist of sets

K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},K_{4}
that enumerate the type 1,2,3,4 RDTs that connect
the classical and the exceptional operators. However, class G
operators can all be constructed using only type 1 and type 3
transformations. Class B operators require the use of 1,3,4
transformations. Class C operators require 1,2,3 type
transformations, while class CB operators require all four types of RDTs
in their construction.

The class G and class B formulas are sufficiently similar so that it
makes sense to combine them into a single statement. The same remark
holds for class C and class CB. This is the reason why the class G
section and formulas feature the
K_{4}
parameter set. The
K_{4}

parameters are superfluous for the G operators, but including them
allows the class G formulas to be reused without repetition to
construct the class B operators. The same remark applies to the C and
CB classes.

3.1.
The generic class G

Let
K_{1},K_{3},K_{4}\subset\mathbb{N}_{0}
be sets of cardinalities
p_{1},p_{3},p_{4}
,
respectively

introduce them here in order to avoid repetition.
, and

.

\displaystyle I_{1}=(\mathbb{N}_{0}\setminus K_{1})-p_{1},

Proposition 3.1
.

The type G assumptions on

\alpha,\beta,\alpha\pm\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)=\sigma_{1}\sqcup\sigma_{2}\sqcup\sigma_{3}\sqcup\sigma_{4}

Proof.

By Proposition
6.1
, the conclusions hold if

\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{2}

K_{3}

moves eigenvalues from
\sigma_{3}
to
\sigma_{4}
and
K_{4}
does just
the opposite.
∎

Let

\Lambda_{\mathrm{G}}(K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b)\colon\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)\to\{\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus\}

.

Remark 3.4
.

It is convenient to visualize the class G spectral diagram as two
Maya diagrams, one row for the type 1,2 eigenvalues, and another row
for the type
3,4
eigenvalues
12
12

12

This is very similar to
Bonneux’s system in
[
5
]
.
. Each cell in these two rows
represents a qr-eigenvalue
\lambda\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)
and is decorated
by the asymptotic label associated to
\lambda
. The type 1,2
eigenvalues are grouped together, because

\lambda_{2}(i;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{1}(\bar{i};\alpha,\beta)
, where

\bar{i}=-i-1
, and because
I_{12}=I_{1}\sqcup\bar{I}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}
, where

\bar{I}_{2}=-I_{2}-1
and where
\sqcup
denotes disjoint union. The

\boxcirc
labels are placed at positions in
I_{1}
; the
\boxtimes

labels are placed at positions in
\bar{I}_{2}
. This works, because

\alpha+\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
and hence the mapping

i\mapsto\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)=i(i+\alpha+\beta+1),\quad i\in\mathbb{Z}

defines
a bijection between the
I_{1}\sqcup\bar{I}_{2}
indices
and the
\sigma_{1}\sqcup\sigma_{2}
eigenvalues.

Likewise, the type 3,4 eigenvalues are grouped together because

\lambda_{4}(i;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{3}(\bar{i};\alpha,\beta)
. The
\boxplus

labels are placed at positions in
I_{3}
and the
\boxminus
labels
at positions in
I_{4}
. Since
\alpha-\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the mapping

i\mapsto\lambda_{3}(i;\alpha,\beta)=(i-\alpha)(i+\beta+1),\quad i\in\mathbb{Z}

defines a bijection between the
I_{3}\sqcup\bar{I}_{4}
indices and the

\sigma_{3}\sqcup\sigma_{4}
eigenvalues.

To recover the parameters
K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b
from the spectral
diagram, one places the origin
k=0
of the first array to the
leftmost
\boxcirc
label, and places the origin of the second array
to the leftmost
\boxplus
label. The elements of
K_{1}
are the
k

positions of the
\boxtimes
labels; the elements of
K_{3}
are the

k
positions of the
\boxminus
labels; while
K_{4}=\emptyset
.

13
13

13

Even though a type G diagram can be constructed without
using
\boxminus\to\boxplus
flips, such flips cannot be avoided
in the case of type B diagrams. We include
K_{4}
in our
definitions so that we can reuse the these formulas when we
discuss class B operators below.
. The values of
a,b
can be
recovered from the spectral diagram by considering the difference of
the qr-eigenvalues at
k=0
and
k=-1
in the first diagram and the
difference of the qr-eigenvalues at
k=0
and
k=-1
. This works
because

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(0;a,b)-\lambda_{1}(-1;a,b)=a+b

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}(0;a,b)-\lambda_{3}(-1;a,b)=a-b.

The diagram
\Lambda_{\mathrm{G}}(K,a,b)
is related to the classical diagram

\Lambda(a,b):=\Lambda_{\mathrm{G}}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,a,b)

by
flips
\boxcirc\mapsto\boxtimes
at the indices in
K_{1}
and by

\boxplus\leftrightarrow\boxminus
flips at the indices at

K_{3},K_{4}
, and by a spectral shift of
p_{1}(a+b+1+p_{1})
. As
described in Section
7.2
below, classical Jacobi
operators can be interrelated by RDTs. Since exceptional operators
are constructed by a chain of RDTs that begin with a classical
operator, a given exceptional operator can be connected to a
classical operator in multiple ways. In other words, the spectral
diagram
\Lambda_{\mathrm{G}}(K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b)
does not uniquely determine the
parameters
K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b
. However, by placing the origin
k=0

at the leftmost
\boxcirc
label and the leftmost
\boxplus
label,
is equivalent to the conditions that
0\notin K_{1}
and that

0\notin K_{3}
and
K_{4}=\emptyset
. This ensures the uniqueness of
the parameters. An example of a class G spectral diagram is shown

below. It is useful to compare it to the

.

3.2.
Semi-degenerate class A

Suppose that

Let

,

\displaystyle I_{1}

Proposition 3.5
.

The assumptions that
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
b\notin\mathbb{Z}
entail that

\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
also. Moreover,
\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{3}

and
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)=\sigma_{1}\sqcup\sigma_{2}\sqcup\sigma_{4}

(disjoint union).

Making use of the above decomposition, we let
\Lambda_{\mathrm{A}}(K,L,a,b)
be the
mapping

(52)

(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{4})\to(\boxcirc,\boxasterisk,\boxminus).

For type A operators, the generic Wronskian-based definitions
(
46
) fail because the eigenvalues of the type 1 and the
type 3 classical eigenfunctions may not have distinct
eigenvalues
14
14

14

In order to apply Wronskian-type formulas in the
confluent case, one has to make use of generalized eigenfunctions.
This is fully explained in
[
26
]
. We have to introduce a
new mechanism to handle such spectral transformations and divide the
construction into two stages. As the first stage of our construction,
consider the case where
K=\emptyset
. Let

\rho_{ij}(x;a,b)=\int\pi_{i}(x;a,b)\pi_{j}(x;a,b)(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b},\quad i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\;a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,b\notin\mathbb{Z}.

This
is uniquely defined, because the integrand is a polynomial times

(1+x)^{b}
, and because

(53)

\int(1+x)^{b+k}=\frac{(1+x)^{b+k+1}}{b+k+1},\quad b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;k\in\mathbb{Z}.

is the unique quasi-rational anti-derivative. Let

\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\cdots<\ell_{q}
be the increasing enumeration of

L
. Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(x;L,a,b)
be the
q\times q
matrix with entries

\mathcal{R}_{ij}=\rho_{\ell_{i}\ell_{j}}(x;a,b),\;i,j=1,\ldots,q.

Let

\mathcal{A}(n)=\mathcal{A}(x;n,L,a,b),\;n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
denote the augmented

(1+q)\times(1+q)
matrix obtained from
\mathcal{R}
as follows:

(54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n)_{00}

\displaystyle=\pi_{n}(x;a,b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n)_{0i}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\ell_{i}}(x;a,b)

(55)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n)_{i0}

\displaystyle=\rho_{\ell_{i}n}(x;a,b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\rho_{\ell_{i}\ell_{j}}(x;a,b)

where
i,j
have the range
1,\ldots,q
. Next, set

\hat{I}_{1}=(\mathbb{N}_{0}\setminus L)-q
, and define

(56)

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}(x;L,a,b)

\displaystyle:=(1+x)^{-q(q+b)}\det\mathcal{R}(x;L,a,b),

(57)

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{i}(x;L,a,b)

\displaystyle:=\hat{\chi}(i+q;L,a,b)(1+x)^{-q}\frac{\det\mathcal{A}(x;i+q,L,a,b)}{\det\mathcal{R}(x;L,a,b)},\quad i\in\hat{I}_{1},

where

(58)

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}(z;L,a,b)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k\in L}\frac{z-k^{*}}{z-k},\quad k^{*}=-k-a-b-1.

Speaking informally, the effect of the first stage of the construction
is the label transformation
\boxcirc\to\boxasterisk\to\boxminus
at
positions indexed by
L
. The idea of label transformations is fully
explained in Section
7.3
.

The second stage of the construction corresponds to label
transformations
\boxcirc\to\boxasterisk
at positions indexed by
K
.
Let
k_{1}<\cdots<k_{p}
be the the increasing enumeration of
K-q
, and
set

(59)

\displaystyle\tau(x;K,L,a,b)

\displaystyle:=\hat{\tau}\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{p}}]

(60)

\displaystyle\pi_{i}(x;K,L,a,b)

\displaystyle=\chi(i+p+q;K)\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{p}},\hat{\pi}_{i+p}]}{\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{p}}]},\quad i\in I_{1}

where

(61)

\displaystyle\chi(z;K)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k\in K}(z-k)^{-1}.

Proposition 3.6
.

The
\tau=\tau(x;K,L,a,b),\alpha,\beta
, as defined above, is a
polynomial with

(62)

\deg\tau=2\sum_{\ell\in L}\ell+\sum_{k\in K}k-\binom{p+q}{2}-\binom{q}{2}+qa.

Then,
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is a type A exceptional Jacobi operator
with spectral diagram
\Lambda_{\mathrm{A}}(K,L,a,b)
and index sets as defined
above. The corresponding exceptional Jacobi quasi-polynomials, with
the monic choice of normalization, are given by the above

\pi_{i}(x)=\pi_{i}(x;K,L,a,b),\;i\in I_{1}
. The corresponding
norms are given by

(63)

\nu_{i}=\kappa(i+p+q;K,L,a,b)\,\nu(i+p+q,a,b),\quad i\in I_{1}.

where

\kappa(z;K,L,a,b)=\hat{\chi}(z;K,a,b)\hat{\chi}(z;L,a,b)^{2}.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be a type A exceptional Jacobi operator and

\Lambda_{T}
its spectral diagram. Then,
\Lambda_{T}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{A}}(K,L,0,b)
for a
unique choice of
K,L,b
such that
0\notin K
. Moreover, up to a
scale factor,
\tau=\tau(x;K,L,0,b)
as per (
57
) and
(
59
).

Remark 3.8
.

Since the defining assumption of a type A operator is that

\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the type 1,2 and the type 3,4 eigenvalues are
commensurate; c.f., (
37
). One of three things happens
at every qr-eigenvalue as
\alpha
tends to an integer: either a type 3
eigenfunction degenerates to a type 1 eigenfunction, a type 2
eigenfunction degenerates to a type 4 eigenfunction, or a degenerate
eigenvalue with type 2 and 3 eigenfunctions appears. Consequently,
the spectral diagram of a class A operator is equivalent to a
Maya-like diagram beginning with an infinite string of
\boxminus

labels followed by a finite string consisting of

\boxminus,\boxcirc,\boxasterisk
labels followed by an infinite string
of
\boxcirc
labels. Proceeding more formally, we can assert that
the mappings
i\mapsto\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta),\;i\in\mathbb{Z}
and

j\mapsto\lambda_{3}(j;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{1}(j-\alpha;al,\beta),\;j\in\mathbb{Z}
are
both bijections of
\mathbb{Z}
and
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)
. The placement of the
asymptotic labels corresponds to the decomposition of the latter
given by Proposition
3.5
.

In order to understand class A diagrams, it is helpful to consider a
classical A operator
T(a,b),\;a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\;b\notin\mathbb{Z}
and its qr
eigenfunctions as shown in Table
2
. The first and
second kind of degeneration can be represented symbolically as

\phi_{3,k+a}(x;A,b)\dashrightarrow\phi_{1,k}(x;a,b),\;k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\qquad\phi_{2,k+a}(x;A,b)\dashrightarrow\phi_{4,k}(x;a,b),\;k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

as
A\to a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Here the we employ the notation

f\dashrightarrow g
to signify that, modulo removable
singularities,
f(x)
tends to a constant multiple of
g(x)
. At
the level of classical Jacobi polynomials this is captured by the
following classical identity
[
70
]
:

(64)

\pi_{n+a}(x;-a,b)=(x-1)^{a}\pi_{n}(x;a,b),\quad a,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

Correspondingly,

\lambda_{3}(n+a;a,b)=\lambda_{1}(n;a,b),\quad a,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

are simple eigenvalues.
Moreover, since
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have

\lambda_{2}(n;a,b)=\lambda_{3}(a-n-1;a,b),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

If

n\in{\check{I}}_{2}=\{0,1,\ldots,a-1\}
, then
\phi_{2n}
and

\phi_{3,a-n-1}
are linearly independent and thus these eigenvalues
are degenerate. For
n\geq a
, we have

\pi_{n+a}(x;-a,-b)=(x-1)^{a}\pi_{n}(x;a,-b),

and hence the
eigenvalues
\lambda_{2}(n;a,b),\;n\geq a
are simple. Consequently, a
classical type A spectral diagram features
a
degenerate eigenvalues
with a
\boxasterisk
label, indicating the presence of eigenfunctions
with both
\boxplus
and
\boxtimes
behaviour. All other qr
eigenvalues are simple and labelled by either
\boxminus
or

\boxcirc
. By way of example, the spectral diagrams for the cases

a=0
and
a=2
are shown in Figure
3

Classical type A spectral diagrams

As asserted by Proposition
3.7
, the spectral diagram of a
type A exceptional operator is a finite modification of the classical
spectral diagram

\Lambda_{\mathrm{A}}(a,b):=\Lambda_{\mathrm{A}}(\emptyset,\emptyset,a,b),\quad a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,b\notin\mathbb{Z}

by
q
label flips

\boxcirc\to\boxasterisk\to\boxminus
indexed by
L
and by
p
flips

\boxcirc\to\boxasterisk
indexed by
K
, and by a spectral shift of

(p+q)(p+q+a+b+1)
; c.f., (
166
). However,
\Lambda_{\mathrm{A}}(a,b)

is itself a finite modification of the classical diagram

\Lambda_{\mathrm{A}}(0,b-a)
by
a
consecutive
\boxcirc\mapsto\boxasterisk
flips
(see Proposition
7.7
, below). Thus, in order to assure
uniqueness of the parameters, we take
a=0
in Proposition

3.7
.

The parameters
K,L,b
and the index sets
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},I_{4}
can be
recovered from the spectral diagram as follows. One places the origin

k=0
at the leftmost
\boxcirc
or
\boxasterisk
label, and takes
K
to
be the
k
positions of the
\boxasterisk
labels and
L
the
k

positions of the
\boxminus
labels to the right of the origin. Let

p,q
be the respective cardinalities of
K
and
L
, and set

i=k-p-q
. The elements of
I_{1}
are the
i
positions of the

\boxcirc
labels. The elements of
I_{2}
are the
\bar{i}=-i-1

positions of the
\boxasterisk
labels. The elements of
I_{3}
are
i+p
,
where
i
is the position of the
\boxasterisk
label. The elements of

I_{4}
are
\bar{i}-p
, where
i
is the positions of the
\boxminus

labels. Note that
I_{3}=\bar{I}_{2}+\alpha
, because
\alpha=p
and because the
type 2 and the type 3 eigenvalues coincide:

\lambda_{2}(k;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{3}(-k-1+\alpha;\alpha,\beta).

The value of
b
is recovered by considering the difference of the qr
eigenvalues at positions
k=0
and
k-1
. Indeed,

b=\lambda_{1}(0;0,b)-\lambda_{1}(-1;0,b).

3.3.
Semi-degenerate class B

Let

such that

. Set

{\check{I}}_{1}={\check{I}}_{2}=\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(65)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{3}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:(n-a+b)_{n}\neq 0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{4}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:(n+a-b)_{n}\neq 0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{3-}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:2n-a+b<0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{3+}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:n-a+b\geq 0\}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{4-}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:2n+a-b<0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{4+}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:n+a-b\geq 0\},

and observe that

{\check{I}}_{3}={\check{I}}_{3-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{3+},\;{\check{I}}_{4}={\check{I}}_{4-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{4+}
,
and that
{\check{I}}_{3+}-a={\check{I}}_{4+}-b
. Let

K_{1}\subset\mathbb{N}_{0},K_{3}\subset{\check{I}}_{3+},K_{4}\subset{\check{I}}_{4+}
be finite
sets with respective cardinalities
p_{1},p_{3},p_{4}
, but with the
additional assumption that

(66)

\tilde{K}_{34}:=(K_{3}-a)\sqcup(K_{4}-b)

is a disjoint union.
15
15

15

The above defined
\tilde{K}_{34}
is the set
of degrees of the qr-functions present in the list

\phi_{3}(K_{3};a,b),\phi_{4}(K_{4};a,b)
. The condition that
(
66
) be a disjoint union ensures that the factorization
eigenvalues are distinct. See Section
4.1
for more
details.
Set
\bar{K}_{\imath}=-K_{\imath}-1
and

\alpha=a+p_{1}-p_{3}+p_{4},\quad\beta=b+p_{1}+p_{3}-p_{4}.

Let

\sigma_{\imath}=\lambda_{\imath}(I_{\imath};\alpha,\beta)
,

\sigma_{\imath\pm}=\lambda_{\imath}(I_{\imath\pm};\alpha,\beta)
,where

(67)

\displaystyle I_{1}=({\check{I}}_{1}\setminus K_{1})-p_{1},

\displaystyle I_{2}=({\check{I}}_{2}\cup\bar{K}_{1})+p_{1},

\displaystyle I_{3-}=({\check{I}}_{3-}\cup\bar{K}_{4})-p_{3}+p_{4}

\displaystyle I_{3+}=({\check{I}}_{3+}\setminus(\tilde{K}_{34}+a))-p_{3}+p_{4}

\displaystyle I_{4-}=({\check{I}}_{4-}\cup\bar{K}_{3})+p_{3}-p_{4}

\displaystyle I_{4+}=({\check{I}}_{4+}\setminus(\tilde{K}_{34}+b))+p_{3}-p_{4}

\displaystyle I_{3}=I_{3-}\sqcup I_{3+}

\displaystyle I_{4}=I_{4-}\sqcup I_{4+}

Proposition 3.9
.

The assumptions
a,b,a+b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a-b\in\mathbb{Z}
entail the same
conditions on
\alpha,\beta
. The qr-spectrum
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)
has the
decomposition

(68)

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)=\sigma_{1}\sqcup\sigma_{2}\sqcup\sigma_{3-}\sqcup\sigma_{4-}\sqcup\sigma_{34},

where
\sigma_{34}:=\sigma_{3+}=\sigma_{4+}
.

Proof.

We prove the last assertion; the other assertions are straightforward.
Recall that

\lambda_{3}(i;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{1}(i-\alpha;\alpha,\beta),\quad\lambda_{4}(i;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{1}(i-\beta;\alpha,\beta).

By construction,

\displaystyle I_{3+}-\alpha

\displaystyle=(({\check{I}}_{3+}-a)\setminus\tilde{K}_{34})-p_{1},

\displaystyle I_{4+}-\beta

\displaystyle=(({\check{I}}_{4+}-b)\setminus\tilde{K}_{34})-p_{1},

Hence,
I_{3+}-\alpha=I_{4+}-\beta
and hence

\sigma_{3+}=\sigma_{4+}
.
∎

Next, we to define
\Lambda_{\mathrm{B}}(K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b)
to be the mapping

(69)

(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{34},\sigma_{3-},\sigma_{4-})\to(\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxdiv,\boxplus,\boxminus)

Let
\tau
and

\pi_{i},\;i\in I_{1}
be defined as in (
46
) and
(
47
). With these definitions and with the above assumptions,
Proposition
3.2
holds verbatim, except that here

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
is a type B operator with spectral diagram

\Lambda_{\mathrm{B}}(K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b)
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The
\tau=\tau(x;K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b),\;a,b,a+b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a-b\in\mathbb{Z}
,
as defined in (
46
), is a polynomial whose degree is
given in (
48
). The operator
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),
with

\alpha,\beta
as defined in (
42
), is a type B exceptional
operator with spectral diagram
\Lambda_{T}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{G}}(K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b)
and
index sets
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},I_{4}
as defined in (
67
). The
corresponding exceptional Jacobi quasi-polynomials, with the monic
choice of normalization, are given by the above defined

\pi_{i}(x;K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b),\;i\in I_{1}
. The corresponding norms
have the form shown in (
49
).

As before, the essential complication is that
different choices of
K,a,b
can yield the same operator. To make the
choice of these parameters unique, we require the following type B
analogue of Proposition
3.3
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be a type B exceptional operator and

\Lambda_{T}
its spectral diagram. Then,

\Lambda_{T}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{B}}(K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b)
for a unique choice of

K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b
such that
a-b\in\{-1,0,1\}
and
0\notin K_{1}
.
Moreover, up to a scale factor,
\tau=\tau(x;K_{1},K_{3},K_{4},a,b)
as
defined in (
46
).

Remark 3.12
.

The type B defining assumption is that

\alpha,\beta,\alpha+\beta\notin\mathbb{Z},\alpha-\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
. From (
37
), we
see that

(70)

\lambda_{3}(i;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{3}(i^{\ddagger};\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{4}(i-\alpha+\beta;\alpha,\beta),

where

(71)

i^{\ddagger}:=-i-1+\alpha-\beta.

This symmetry is at the heart of the class B degenerations.

It is instructive to consider the consequences of the spectral
symmetry (
70
) on the qr-eigenfunctions of a classical
B operator
T(a,b),\;a-b\in\mathbb{Z}
. We consider the subcase where

a\geq b
; the case where
b\geq a
admits a similar discussion.
Consider the limit of a generic classical operator
T(A,B)
as

(A,B)\to(a,b)
. In the limit, a finite number of type 3
eigenfunctions degenerate to type 3 eigenfunctions with a lower
index. To explain this, observe that the leading coefficient of

P_{n}(x;-a,b)
has a factor of
(n-a+b+1)_{n}
. Thus,

\deg P_{n}(x;-a,b)<n
if and only if

(n-a+b+1)\cdots(2n-a+b)=(0-n^{\dagger})\cdots(n-1-n^{\dagger})=0,

where
n^{\dagger}:=-n-1+a-b,\;n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. This possibility occurs
precisely for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
n>n^{\dagger}\geq 0
. For such
indices we have

(72)

\phi_{3,n}(x;A,B)\dashrightarrow\phi_{3,n^{\dagger}}(x;a,b),\quad\text{as }(A,B)\to(a,b).

This degeneration is captured by the following
classical identity
[
70
]
:

(73)

(n^{\dagger}+1)_{j}P_{n}(x;-a,b)=(n-a)_{j}P_{n^{\dagger}}(x;-a,b),\quad j:=n-n^{\dagger}\geq 0.

Consequently, the classical type 3 index set decomposes as

{\check{I}}_{3}={\check{I}}_{3-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{3+}
, as defined in
(
65
), and is missing
\lfloor(a-b)/2\rfloor
indices.
The missing indices are precisely the
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

n>n^{\dagger}\geq 0
. At the level of the spectral diagram, as

\Lambda_{\mathrm{G}}(A,B)\to\Lambda_{\mathrm{B}}(a,b)
, this symmetry manifests as a coalescence
of a co-finite number of
\boxplus
and
\boxminus
eigenvalues into
degenerate eigenvalues with the compound label
\boxdiv
. See Figure

5
for some examples.

Since

defines a bijection of

and

. Since

\sigma_{3+}=\sigma_{4+}=\sigma_{34}
.
. Since

and since

, we use the
second bijection to represent the type 3,4 eigenvalues as a
