\boxplus,\boxminus,\boxdiv
labels.

The B class is further divided into two subclasses, according to
whether
\alpha-\beta
is even or odd. The odd subclass is characterized
by the fact that
i=(\alpha-\beta-1)/2
is the unique integer such that

i=i^{\ddagger}
, with the latter defined as in (
71
). This
index marks the vertex of the type 34 eigenvalue parabola

\{(i,\lambda_{3}(i;\alpha,\beta)):i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. We will refer to the
corresponding
\lambda_{3}(i;\alpha,\beta)
as
the vertex eigenvalue
.
In the visual representation described aboce, the vertex eigenvalue
corresponds to the leftmost cell of the type 3,4 demi-diagram. To
make this distinction visually, we will mark the vertex eigenvalue
by a doubly-lined
\boxed{\boxplus}
or
\boxed{\boxminus}
label, according to
the asymptotic type of the eigenfunction at the vertex eigenvalue.
In the even subclass, the vertex index is a half-integer, and so
does not correspond to any qr-eigenfunctions.

Every type B diagram may be canonically constructed using a finite
number of
\boxcirc\to\boxtimes
and

\boxdiv\to\{\boxplus,\boxminus\}
flips applied to a classical
diagram
\Lambda_{\mathrm{B}}(a,b)=\Lambda_{\mathrm{B}}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,a,b)
where

a,b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a-b\in\{-1,0,1\}
. The label changes

\boxcirc\mapsto\boxtimes
are indexed by
K_{1}
, while the label
changes
\boxdiv\mapsto\boxminus
and
\boxdiv\mapsto\boxplus
are
indexed by
K_{3}
and
K_{4}
, respectively. The parameter set
K_{1}

and the index sets
I_{1},I_{2}
are recovered from the doubly-infinite
diagram as follows. One takes the origin
k=0
at the leftmost

\boxcirc
symbol and lets
p_{1}
be the number of
\boxtimes
labels
to the right of this origin. The parameter set
K_{1}
consists of the

k
positions of these labels. The elements of
I_{1}
are the
k-p_{1}

positions of the
\boxcirc
labels. The elements
of
I_{2}
are the
\bar{k}:=-k-1
positions of the
\boxtimes
labels
shifted by
+p_{1}
.

The value of
\alpha-\beta
, the parameter sets
K_{3},K_{4}
, and the index
sets
I_{3},I_{4}
are recovered from the demi-diagram as follows. Let

\hat{p}_{3},\hat{p}_{4}
be the number of all
\boxminus
and
\boxplus

labels, and let
p_{3},p_{4}
be the number of non-vertex

\boxminus,\boxplus
labels. Thus,
\hat{p}_{3}=p_{3}+1
if there is a
vertex
\boxed{\boxminus}
label, and
\hat{p}_{4}=p_{4}+1
if there is a vertex

\boxed{\boxplus}
label. In the even case,
\hat{p}_{3}=p_{3}
and
\hat{p}_{4}=p_{4}
.
The value of
a-b
is given by
\hat{p}_{3}-p_{3}-(\hat{p}_{4}-p_{4})
. The value of

\alpha-\beta
is recovered as
p_{4}+\hat{p}_{4}-(p_{3}+\hat{p}_{3})
; this is the
difference between the number of
\boxplus
and
\boxminus
labels,
with non-vertex labels counted twice, and the vertex label (if any)
counted once. Next, one places the origin
k=0
of the demi-diagram
at the leftmost non-vertex cell
17
17

17

The vertex cell, if present,
is always placed at position
k=-1
.
and sets

i=k-p_{3}+\hat{p}_{4},\quad j=k+\hat{p}_{3}-p_{4}=i-\alpha+\beta.

The elements of

K_{3},K_{4}
are, respectively, the
k+\hat{p}_{4}-p_{4}
and the
k+\hat{p}_{3}-p_{3}

positions of the
\boxminus
and the
\boxplus
labels. The elements of

I_{3+},I_{4+}
are, respectively, the
i
and

positions of the

\boxdiv
labels. The elements of
I_{3-}
are the
i^{\ddagger}
positions
of the
\boxplus
labels, and the elements of
I_{4-}
are the

\bar{i}=-i-1
positions of the
\boxminus
labels. The elements
of
K_{3}
are the
k
positions
K_{4}
are the
k
positions of the

\boxplus
labels.

3.4.
Semi-degenerate classes C and CB

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

.
Set

(74)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{1}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:(n+a+b+1)_{n}\neq 0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{2}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:(n-a-b+1)_{n}\neq 0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{1-}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:2n+a+b<0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{1+}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:n+a+b+1\geq 1\}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{2-}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:2n-a-b<0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{2+}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:n-a-b+1\geq 1\},

and let

(

).

be finite

p_{\imath}
but with the additional

\tilde{K}_{12}:=K_{1}\sqcup(K_{2}-a-b),\quad\tilde{K}_{34}=(K_{3}-a)\sqcup(K_{4}-b)

be disjoint unions
18
18

18

As before, if the
factorization degrees are distinct, then the factorization
eigenvalues are also distinct.
. Set
\bar{K}_{\imath}=-K_{\imath}-1
and

(75)

\alpha=a+p_{1}-p_{2}-p_{3}+p_{4},\quad\beta=b+p_{1}-p_{2}+p_{3}-p_{4},

and let

\sigma_{\imath}=\lambda_{\imath}(I_{\imath};\alpha,\beta)
and

\sigma_{\imath\pm}=\lambda_{\imath}(I_{\imath\pm};\alpha,\beta),\;\imath=1,2,3,4
, with

(76)

\displaystyle I_{1-}

\displaystyle=({\check{I}}_{1-}\cup\bar{K}_{2})-p_{1}+p_{2}

\displaystyle I_{1+}

Proposition 3.13
.

Let

\sigma_{\imath}=\lambda_{\imath}(I_{\imath};\alpha,\beta),\;\imath=1,2,3,4
, with the latter as per (
37
). The type C and
the type type CB conditions on
a,b
entail, respectively, the same
conditions on
\alpha,\beta
. Moreover, if the type C conditions hold, then

\displaystyle\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1-}\sqcup\sigma_{2-}\sqcup\sigma_{12}\sqcup\sigma_{3}\sqcup\sigma_{4}

where
\sigma_{12}:=\sigma_{1+}=\sigma_{2+}
. If the CB
conditions hold, then

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)=\sigma_{1-}\sqcup\sigma_{2-}\sqcup\sigma_{12}\sqcup\sigma_{3-}\sqcup\sigma_{4-}\sqcup\sigma_{34},

where
\sigma_{34}:=\sigma_{3+}=\sigma_{4+}
.

For
a,b
satisfying the type C conditions, let
\Lambda_{\mathrm{C}}(K,a,b)
be the
mapping

(\sigma_{1-},\sigma_{2-},\sigma_{12},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4})\mapsto(\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus);

i.e., an
eigenvalue in
\sigma_{1-}
is labelled by
\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes
, etc.

For the CB class, let
\Lambda_{\mathrm{CB}}(K,a,b)
be the mapping

(\sigma_{1-},\sigma_{2-},\sigma_{12},\sigma_{3-},\sigma_{4-},\sigma_{34})\mapsto(\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxdiv,\boxplus,\boxminus).

Let

\phi_{1234}(K;a,b)
denote the concatenation of lists

\phi_{1}(K_{1};a,b),\phi_{2}(K_{2};a,b),\phi_{3}(K_{3};a,b),\phi_{4}(K_{4};a,b)
, and
let
\tilde{K}=\tilde{K}_{12}\sqcup\tilde{K}_{34}
be the set of degrees of the
qr-functions present in that list. With this definition in place,
define

(77)

\displaystyle\tau_{\Lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=(1-x)^{(p_{2}+p_{3})(p_{1}+p_{4}+a)}(1+x)^{(p_{2}+p_{4})(p_{1}+p_{3}+b)}\operatorname{Wr}[\phi_{1234}(K)]

(78)

\displaystyle\pi_{\Lambda,i}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{(x-1)^{p_{2}+p_{3}}(1+x)^{p_{2}+p_{4}}}{\prod_{k\in\tilde{K}}(i+p_{1}-p_{2}-k)}\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[\phi_{1234}(K),\phi_{1}(\mathfrak{u}(i+p_{1}-p_{2}))]}{\operatorname{Wr}[\phi_{1234}(K)]},\quad i\in I_{1},

where
\mathfrak{u}(j):=\max\{j,j^{*}\},\;j^{*}=-j-1-a-b
.

Proposition 3.14
.

The above defined
\tau=\tau(x;K,a,b),\;a,b\notin\mathbb{Z}
is a
polynomial with

\deg\tau=\sum_{\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}}\sum_{k\in K_{\imath}}K_{\imath}+2p_{1}p_{2}+2p_{3}p_{4}-\binom{p_{1}+p_{2}}{2}-\binom{p_{3}+p_{4}}{2}.

If
a-b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a+b\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
, with

\alpha,\beta
as defined in (
75
), is a type C exceptional
Jacobi operator with spectral diagram
\Lambda_{T}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{C}}(K,a,b)
. If

2a,2b\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
T
is a type CB exceptional operator with
spectral diagram
\Lambda_{T}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{CB}}(K,a,b)
. In both cases
T
has
the above-defined index sets. The corresponding exceptional
quasi-polynomials, with the monic choice of normalization, are given
by (
78
). The
corresponding norms are given by

(79)

\nu_{i}=\kappa(i+p_{1}-p_{2})\nu(i+p_{1}-p_{2};a,b),

with
\kappa
as defined in (
50
) but with
\tilde{K}
as
defined above. Consequently, the norms are zero if
i\in I_{1-}
,
and non-zero if
i\in I_{1+}
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let

T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta,\alpha-\beta\notin\mathbb{Z},\,\alpha+\beta\in\mathbb{Z}

be a type
C
exceptional operator and
\Lambda
its spectral
diagram. Then
\Lambda_{T}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{C}}(K,a,b)
for a unique choice of
K,a,b

such that
a+b\in\{-1,0,1\}
and (
75
) holds, and such
that
K_{4}=\emptyset
. Up to a scale factor,
\tau=\tau(x;K,a,b)
,
as defined in (
77
).

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z},\;2\alpha,2\beta\in\mathbb{Z}

be a class CB exceptional operator and
\Lambda_{T}
its spectral
diagram. Then
\Lambda_{T}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{CB}}(K,a,b)
for a unique choice of

K,a,b
such that
a,b\in\{-1/2,1/2\}
. Up to a scale
factor,
\tau=\tau(x;K,a,b)
, as defined in (
77
).

Remark 3.17
.

The class C spectral degeneracy entails a two-to-one correspondence
between type 1,2 indices and type 1,2 eigenvalues. Indeed, since

\alpha+\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{1}(i^{\star};\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{2}(\bar{i};\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{2}(i-\alpha-\beta;\alpha,\beta)

where

i^{\star}=-i-1-\alpha-\beta,\;\bar{i}=-i-1,\quad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

As for class B,
this symmetry entails two types of phenomena: the degeneration of a
finite number of quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions to
quasi-polynomials of a lower degree, and the presence of an infinite
number of degenerate eigenvalues of type
\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes
. The visual
representation of type C diagrams is similar to the B class; one
merely exchanges the 12 eigenvalues and the 34 eigenvalues and
replaces the
\boxplus,\boxminus,\boxdiv
labels with

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes
labels.

The operators in the B and C classes are related by a gauge
transformation that interchanges the asymptotic labels as follows:

\boxcirc\leftrightarrow\boxplus,\quad\boxtimes\leftrightarrow\boxminus,\quad\boxdiv\leftrightarrow\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes

Thus, the visual representation of class C spectral
diagram relies on a demi-diagram decorated with

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes
labels for the type 1,2 eigenvalue
and a doubly infinite diagram decorated with
\boxplus,\boxminus

labels for the type 3,4 eigenvalues. As with the B class, the C
class is divided into even and odd subclasses according to the
parity of
\alpha+\beta
. In the odd case, one marks the vertex
eigenvalue with either a
\boxed{\boxcirc}
or a
\boxed{\boxtimes}
label
according to the asymptotics of the eigenfunction at that
eigenvalue. The considerations are exactly the same as the ones
presented in Remark
3.12
.

However, there is a peculiarity of the C class that deserves some
further comment. One consequence of the class C norm formula
(
79
) is that the finite set of quasi-polynomial
eigenfunctions with a non-degenerate
\boxcirc
label have a zero
norm. Indeed, this can even be seen for classical type C
polynomials. An examination of the classical norm function

\nu(n;a,b)
, defined in (
14
), reveals that if

a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, then the expression

\lim_{z\to n}\frac{\Gamma(z+1+a+b)}{\Gamma(2z+1+a+b)\Gamma(2z+2+a+b)}=0

precisely when
0\leq n<n^{*}
, where
n^{*}:=-n-1-a-b
. In other
words, the condition
0\leq n<n^{*}
implies that
n\in{\check{I}}_{1-}
,
where the latter is defined in (
74
). This means that

\nu_{n}=0
when
0\leq n<n^{*}
. More generally, it is possible to
demonstrate that class C exceptional non-vertex eigenpolynomials
indexed by
I_{1-}
have zero norm. In consequence, the exceptional
and classical type C operators with a
I_{1-}
that contains
non-vertex indices can
never
be regular.

The CB class (generalized Chebyshev) is the intersection of the B
and C classes, and as such represents the case where
\alpha,\beta
are
half-integers. The CB spectral diagrams are thus represented by two
demi-diagrams: the first consists of

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes
labels, the second consists of

\boxplus,\boxminus,\boxdiv
labels. The encoding of the index
sets by the diagrams follows the same rules detailed in Remark

3.12
. Since
\alpha,\beta
are half-integers, either
\alpha+\beta
is
odd or
\alpha-\beta
is odd; the two possibilities are mutually
exclusive. That means that in the CB class, there is always exactly
one vertex eigenvalue. If
\alpha+\beta
is odd, then the vertex
eigenvalue is marked by either
\boxed{\boxcirc}
or
\boxed{\boxtimes}
,
according to whether it belongs to
\sigma_{1-}
or to

\sigma_{2-}
. If
\alpha-\beta
is even, then the vertex eigenvalue is
marked by either
\boxed{\boxplus}
or
\boxed{\boxminus}
, according to whether
it belongs to
\sigma_{3-}
or to
\sigma_{4-}
. Some examples of
CB diagrams are shown in figure
7
below.

3.5.
Degenerate class D

In order to describe and classify type D operators we introduce an
additional symbol
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
, and define an
extended
spectral diagram
to be the spectral diagram of a type D operator
with a finite number of
\boxcirc
labels replaced by
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}

as per Definition
2.32
below. As asserted by Proposition

3.20
, below, the
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
labels index the support
of an isospectral deformation.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and let

(80)

{\check{I}}_{1}=\mathbb{N}_{0},\quad{\check{I}}_{2}={\check{I}}_{2-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{2+},\quad{\check{I}}_{3}={\check{I}}_{3-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{3+},\quad{\check{I}}_{4}={\check{I}}_{4-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{4+}\quad

where

(81)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{2-}=\{0,\ldots,\min\{a,b\}-1\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{2+}=\{\max\{a,b\},\ldots,a+b-1\}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{3-}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:2n-a+b<0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{3+}=\{\max\{a-b,0\},\ldots,a-1\}={\check{I}}_{2+}-b

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{4-}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:2n+a-b<0\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\check{I}}_{4+}=\{\max\{b-a,0\},\ldots,b-1\}={\check{I}}_{2+}-a

Let
K,L_{1},L_{3},L_{4}\subset\mathbb{N}_{0}
be finite and disjoint, with

p,q_{1},q_{3},q_{4}
the respective cardinalities. Define

k^{*}=-k-a-b-1,\;K^{*}=-K-a-b-1,\;L^{*}=-L-a-b-1,

and let

(82)

\displaystyle I_{1+}=({\check{I}}_{1}\setminus(K\cup L_{1}\cup L_{3}\cup L_{4}))-p-q_{3}-q_{4},

\displaystyle I_{1-}=L^{*}_{1}-p-q_{3}-q_{4},

\displaystyle I_{2+}=({\check{I}}_{2+}\cup K)+a+b+p+q_{3}+q_{4},

\displaystyle I_{2-}=({\check{I}}_{2-}\cup K^{*})+a+b+p+q_{3}+q_{4}

\displaystyle I_{3+}=({\check{I}}_{3+}\cup K)-q_{3}+q_{4}+a,

\displaystyle I_{3-}=({\check{I}}_{3-}\cup L_{4})-q_{3}+q_{4}+a

\displaystyle I_{4+}=({\check{I}}_{4+}\cup K)+q_{3}-q_{4}+b,

\displaystyle I_{4-}=({\check{I}}_{4-}\cup L_{3})+q_{3}-q_{4}+b

Set

(83)

\alpha=a+p+2q_{4},\quad\beta=b+p+2q_{3},\\

and let
\sigma_{\imath}=\lambda_{\imath}(I_{\imath};\alpha,\beta)
and

\sigma_{\imath\pm}=\lambda_{\imath}(I_{\imath\pm};\alpha,\beta),\;\imath=1,2,3,4
.

Proposition 3.18
.

The assumptions that
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
entail the same conditions on

\alpha,\beta
. Moreover,

\sigma_{234}:=\sigma_{2-}=\sigma_{2+}=\sigma_{3+}=\sigma_{4+}
and

(84)

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)=\sigma_{1+}\sqcup\sigma_{1-}\sqcup\sigma_{234}\sqcup\sigma_{3-}\sqcup\sigma_{4-}

For
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let

\Lambda_{\mathrm{D}}(K,L,a,b)
be the mapping described by

(\sigma_{1+},\sigma_{1-},\sigma_{234},\sigma_{3-},\sigma_{4-})\mapsto(\boxcirc,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}},\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},\boxplus,\boxminus)

As before, Wronskian-type definitions (
46
) will not
suffice to define the relevant
\tau
, because such Wronskians require
that the factorization eigenvalues be distinct. Thus, just as for the
type A subclass, we have to divide our construction into two
stages.

The first stage corresponds to the case where
K=\emptyset
. Let

\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{q_{1}+q_{3}+q_{4}}
be an enumeration of

L_{134}:=L_{1}\cup L_{3}\cup L_{4}
ordered in such a way that

\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{q_{1}}
is an enumeration of
L_{1}
, so that

\ell_{q_{1}+1},\ldots,\ell_{q_{1}+q_{3}}
is an enumeration of
L_{3}
, and
so that
\ell_{q_{1}+q_{3}+1},\ldots,\ell_{q_{1}+q_{3}+q_{4}}
is an
enumeration of
L_{4}
. Set

(85)

\displaystyle\rho_{ij}(x;a,b)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{x}\pi_{i}(x;a,b)\pi_{j}(x;a,b)\,(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b},\quad i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Let
\boldsymbol{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}:=(t_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,t_{\ell_{q_{1}}})
be a finite list of indeterminates, and let

t_{\ell}=\begin{cases}\rho_{\ell\ell}(-1;a,b)=0&\text{if }\ell\in L_{3}\\
\rho_{\ell\ell}(1;a,b)=\nu(\ell;a,b)&\text{if }\ell\in L_{4}.\end{cases}

Define

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};L,a,b)
to be the square matrix with entries

\mathcal{R}_{ij}=t_{\ell_{i}}\delta_{ij}+\rho_{\ell_{i}\ell_{j}}(x;a,b),\quad i,j=1,\ldots,q_{1}+q_{3}+q_{4}

Let
\mathcal{A}(n)=\mathcal{A}(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};n,L_{1},L_{3},L_{4},a,b),\;n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be the square matrix
obtained by augmenting

\mathcal{R}
with an extra row and column as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n)_{00}

\displaystyle=\pi_{n}(x;a,b),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n)_{0j}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\ell_{j}}(x;a,b),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n)_{i0}

\displaystyle=\rho_{\ell_{i}n}(x;a,b),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{ij},

with
i,j
having the same range as above. Set

(86)

\displaystyle\hat{I}_{1}

\displaystyle:=((\mathbb{N}_{0}\setminus L_{134})\cup L_{1}^{*})-q_{3}-q_{4},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}(j)

\displaystyle:=\max\{j,j^{*}\},

\displaystyle\hat{n}_{i}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{u}(i+q_{3}+q_{4}),

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}(z)

\displaystyle:=(-1)^{q_{4}}\prod_{\ell\in L_{34}}\frac{z-\ell^{*}}{z-\ell},\quad L_{34}:=L_{3}\cup L_{4},

and define

(87)

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};L_{1},L_{3},L_{4},a,b)

\displaystyle:=(1-x)^{-q_{4}(q_{4}+a)}(1+x)^{-q_{3}(q_{3}+b)}\det\mathcal{R},

(88)

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{i}(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};L_{1},L_{3},L_{4},a,b)

\displaystyle:=(x-1)^{-q_{4}}(1+x)^{-q_{3}}\hat{\chi}(n_{i})\frac{\det\mathcal{A}(\hat{n}_{i})}{\det\mathcal{R}},\quad i\in\hat{I}_{1},\;

Next, let
k_{1},\ldots,k_{p}
be an enumeration of
K
, let

\hat{\pi}(K-q_{3}-q_{4}):=\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}-q_{3}-q_{4}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{p}-q_{3}-q_{4}},

denote the indicated list. Set

\displaystyle n_{i}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{u}(i+p+q_{3}+q_{4}),

\displaystyle\chi(z)

\displaystyle:=(-1)^{q_{4}}\prod_{k\in K}\frac{1}{z-k}\prod_{\ell\in L_{34}}\frac{z-\ell^{*}}{z-\ell},\quad L_{34}:=L_{3}\cup L_{4},

and define

(89)

\displaystyle\tau(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};K,L,a,b)

\displaystyle:=\hat{\tau}\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}(K-q_{3}-q_{4})],

(90)

\displaystyle\pi_{i}(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};K,L,a,b)

\displaystyle:=\chi(n_{i})\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}(K-q_{3}-q_{4}),\hat{\pi}_{i+p}]}{\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}(K-q_{3}-q_{4})]},\quad i\in I_{1}.

Proposition 3.19
.

The above defined

\{\tau\}_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}=\tau(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};K,L,a,b),\alpha,\beta
is a

q_{1}
-parameter family of polynomials with

(91)

\deg\tau=\sum_{k\in K}k+2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\ell\in L_{134}}\!\!\!\!\ell-\binom{p}{2}-p(q_{3}+q_{4})+q_{1}-q_{3}(q_{3}-1)-q_{4}(q_{4}-1)+a(q_{1}+q_{3})+b(q_{1}+q_{4})

The corresponding operators

\{T\}_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}}},\alpha,\beta)
are a

q_{1}
-parameter isospectral deformation with extended spectral
diagram
\Lambda_{\mathrm{D}}(K,L_{1},L_{3},L_{4},a,b)
and index sets as defined
above. The corresponding families of exceptional Jacobi
quasi-polynomials, with the asymptotically monic
normalization
19
19

19

This means that (i) the value
\pi_{i}(-1)
is
independent of
\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}}
and that (ii) in limit as
\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}}\to\infty
the resulting polynomials are monic.
, are given by

\{\pi_{i}(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}})\}_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}}},\;i\in I_{1}
. The corresponding
norms have the form

(92)

\nu_{i}=\kappa(n_{i};\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};K,L,a,b)\,\nu(n_{i};a,b),\quad i\in I_{1},

where

(93)

\kappa(z):=\prod_{\ell\in L_{1}}\left(1-\delta_{z,\ell^{*}}\frac{{\tilde{\nu}}_{\ell}}{t_{\ell}+{\tilde{\nu}}_{\ell}}\right)\prod_{k\in K}\left(\frac{z-k^{*}}{z-k}\right)\prod_{\ell\in L_{34}}\!\!\left(\frac{z-\ell^{*}}{z-\ell}\right)^{2},\quad{\tilde{\nu}}_{\ell}=\nu(\ell;a,b).

Proposition 3.20
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be a type D exceptional Jacobi operator
and
\Lambda
its extended spectral diagram. Then,

\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathrm{D}}(K,L_{1},L_{3},L_{4},a,b)
for a unique choice of

K,L_{1},L_{3},L_{4}
and
(a,b)\in\{(0,0),(1,0),(0,1)\}
. Moreover,
up to a scale factor,
\tau=\tau(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};K,L,a,b)
for some
non-zero value of
\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}}
.

Remark 3.21
.

The type D class is a confluence of the type A and B classes. As a
consequence of the defining assumptions
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there
exists degenerate eigenvalues where
all
eigenfunctions are
rational and all singular asymptotic types 2,3,4 eigenfunctions are
present. We use
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
to label such eigenvalues. As well,
class D operators admit spectral deformations supported at a finite
subset of the quasi-rational spectrum. Such eigenvalues are
labelled by
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
; the degrees of the corresponding
quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions are elements of
I_{1-}
.

This peculiarity of the D class is due to the fact that a
quasi-polynomial eigenvalue does not uniquely determine the index of
the corresponding quasi-polynomial eigenfunction. The relationship
between a type 1 index
i\in I_{1}
and type 1 eigenvalue

\lambda\in\sigma_{1}
is

\lambda=\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)=-ii^{\star},

where

(94)

i^{\star}:=-i-\alpha-\beta-1,\quad i\in\mathbb{Z}

Thus, if
\alpha+\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
, then a given
\lambda\in\sigma_{1}
admits two
possible values of
i
according to the symmetry
i\mapsto i^{\star}
,
which is the reflection across the vertex
i=(\alpha+\beta+1)/2
of the
discrete parabola
\{(i,i(i+\alpha+\beta+1)):i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. This is the
reason behind the decomposition of the type 1 quasi-polynomial index
set as
I_{1}=I_{1+}\sqcup I_{1-}
, with the latter defined as in
(
40
)
20
20

20

One can show that the vertex value

(\alpha+\beta+1)/2
can never be a quasi-polynomial eigenvalue.
. The
effect of an isospectral deformation is that it reflects the index
of a quasi-polynomial eigenfunction from
I_{1+}
to
I_{1-}
. This
fact allows us to mark the support of the isospectral deformation in
terms of degrees.

A class D exceptional operator depends on
q=|I_{1-}|
continuous
parameters
t_{\ell}
, where
i=(\ell+\gamma)^{\star},\;i\in I_{1-}

where
\gamma=p+q_{3}+q_{4}
is the constant defined above. These
parameters determine the corresponding norms
\nu_{i},\;i\in I_{1-}

in a one-to-one fashion. Thus the norms together with the extended
spectral diagram fully determine the corresponding exceptional
operator. The norms in question are subject to certain
inequalities. In the limit, as the parameters
t_{\ell}
approach
these boundary values, the isospectral deformation breaks down and
the resulting transformation corresponds to a removal of an
eigenvalue from the spectrum.

It is instructive to consider the subclass of classical type D
operators
T(a,b),\;a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and their qr-eigenfunctions as
shown in Table
2
. As a consequence of
(
64
), we have that

\phi_{3,k+a}(x;A,B),\phi_{4,k+b}(x;A,B),\phi_{2,k+a+b}(x;A,B)\dashrightarrow\phi_{1,k}(x;a,b),\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

as
(A,B)\to(a,b)
. Consequently, the qr-eigenvalues

\lambda_{1}(k;a,b),\;k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are simple with asymptotic label

\boxcirc
. If
a\geq b
, then the qr-eigenfunctions

\phi_{4,k+b}(x;a,b),\phi_{3,k+a}(x;a,b),\;k\in\{-b,\ldots,-1\}

have a common eigenvalue

\lambda_{1}(k;a,b)=\lambda_{3}(k+a;a,b)=\lambda_{4}(k+b;a,b)=\lambda_{2}(-k-1;a,b),

but are linearly independent. The eigenfunction

\phi_{2,-k-1}(x;a,b)
also occurs at this eigenvalue as a linear
combination of the other two. We signify this kind of degenerate
eigenvalue using the asymptotic label
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
. Finally, for

k\in\{-a,\ldots,-b-1\}
, the qr-functions

\phi_{2,k+a+b},\phi_{3,k+a}
are linearly dependent. The
corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(k;a,b)
are simple with a

\boxplus
label.

We visualize the spectral diagram of a class D operator as a
demi-diagram of
\boxcirc
labels with finitely of these labels
replaced by
\boxplus,\boxminus,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
and
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
labels.
A demi-diagram suffices because the condition

\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
means that the degrees of the type 1,2,3,4
eigenfunctions are all commensurate and all co-exist on the same
semi-axis.

The reflection
i\mapsto i^{\star}:=-i-1-\alpha-\beta
is
a symmetry of the eigenvalue parabola

\{(i,\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)):i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
because

\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{1}(i^{\star};\alpha,\beta)=i(i+\alpha+\beta+1)=-i\,i^{\star},\quad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

Consequently, the mapping

i\mapsto i(i+\alpha+\beta+1)
is a bijection of the paired degrees

\{(i,i^{\star}):i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and the eigenvalue set

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)
. We therefore number the cells of the demi-diagram
by pairs of integers
(i,i^{\star})
with
i\geq i^{\star}
. In effect,
the visualization replaces the curved eigenvalue parabola

\{(i,\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)):i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
by a linear demi-diagram
indexed by
(i,i^{\star})
. We decorate the demi-diagram with the
corresponding asymptotic labels, placing each label at the cell
corresponding to the degree of that eigenfunction.

If
\alpha+\beta
is odd, there is a unique integer index
i
such that

i=i^{\star}
. We then say that the corresponding eigenvalue

\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{3}(i+\alpha;\alpha,\beta)
is the vertex eigenvalue.
This eigenvalue, if present, is always located at the left-most
position of the type D demi-diagram and corresponds to either a type
3 or a type 4 eigenvalue. Correspondingly, we mark the vertex
eigenvalue by a doubly-lined
\boxed{\boxplus}
or
\boxed{\boxminus}
label,
according to the asymptotic type of the eigenfunction at the vertex
eigenvalue. The rest of the
\boxplus,\boxminus
labels are placed
in cells at positions
I_{3-}-\alpha
and
I_{4-}-\beta
, respectively.
The
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
labels are placed at positions
I_{2+}-\alpha-\beta
. The

\boxcirc
labels are placed in positions
I_{1+}
, while the

\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
labels are placed at positions
I_{1-}
.

In particular, the spectral diagram of a classical operator

T(a,b),\;a\geq b
consists of
a-b
consecutive
\boxplus
labels,
followed by
b
consecutive
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
labels, followed by an
infinity of
\boxcirc
labels. The
\boxplus
labels are attached
to the
\sigma_{3-}
eigenvalues and the corresponding type 3
eigenfunctions are indexed by
I_{3-}
. The
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
labels are
attached to
\sigma_{234}
eigenvalues. The corresponding
eigenspaces are 2-dimensional, containing type 2,3,4 quasi-rational
eigenfunctions indexed by
I_{2-},I_{2+},I_{3+},I_{4+}
. Lemma

6.7
below gives an explicit description of the
relationship between the degrees and eigenvalues of such
eigenfunctions. Figure

shows some representative
spectral diagrams of classical class D operators.

The class D construction detailed above is, essentially, an
isospectral deformation of a classical operator followed by some
state-deleting transformations. However, since the factorization
eigenvalues of the first stage are distinct from the factorization
eigenvalues of the second stage, one could interchange the order of
operations, and construct the same objects as isospectral
deformations of exceptional type D operators. This constitutes a
novel class of exceptional polynomials and operators, although
Duran’s construction in
[
23
]
should, in principle, yield the
same objects.

4.
The formal theory of second-order operators.

In this section we gather some basic results in differential algebra
related to Darboux transformations. Most of the calculations here
are formal, involving only rational or quasi-rational functions.
Many of these results are well-known and have appeared in previous
publications, but we reprise them here for the benefit of the reader.

4.1.
Rational Darboux Transformations.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
T,\hat{T}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
and let
A=b(D-w),\;b,w\in\mathcal{Q}
be a first
order operator with rational coefficients. If

(95)

A\circ T=\hat{T}\circ A,

then the quantity
\lambda=\mathrm{R}[T]w
(see (
25
) for the latter
notation) is a constant.

Proof.

Writing
T=pD^{2}+qD+r
,
\hat{T}=pD^{2}+\hat{q}D+\hat{r}
,
where
p,q,\hat{q},r,\hat{r}\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have

A\circ T-\hat{T}\circ A=b(q-\hat{q}+p^{\prime})D^{2}+b(r-\hat{r}+w(\hat{q}-q)+q^{\prime}+2pw^{\prime})D+b(w(\hat{r}-r)+r^{\prime}+\hat{q}w^{\prime}+pw^{\prime\prime}).

It follows that

\displaystyle q+p^{\prime}-\hat{q}=0,

\displaystyle r-\hat{r}+wp^{\prime}+q^{\prime}+2pw^{\prime}=0,

\displaystyle p(w^{\prime\prime}+2ww^{\prime})+p^{\prime}(w^{\prime}+w^{2})+wq^{\prime}+qw^{\prime}+r^{\prime}=0.

The left-side of the last line is precisely the derivative of

p(w^{\prime}+w^{2})+qw+r
. The latter, in turn is precisely
\mathrm{R}[T]w
.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
w(x)
be a rational function and define the qr-function

(96)

\phi(x)=\exp\left(\int w(x)dx\right).

Then, the Riccati-like relation
\lambda=\mathrm{R}[T]w
is equivalent to
the eigenvalue relation
T\phi=\lambda\phi
.

Proof.

By construction,
w=\phi^{\prime}/\phi
, and hence
A\phi=0
. Applying
both sides of (
95
) to
\phi
then gives
AT\phi=0
.
Since
\ker A
is 1-dimensional, it follows that
T\phi=\lambda\phi

for some constant
\lambda
. By (
25
), that constant is
precisely the expression
\lambda=\mathrm{R}[T]w=(T\phi)/\phi
.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

If (
95
) holds, then there exists a 1st order

\hat{A}={\hat{b}}(D-\hat{w}),\;{\hat{b}},\hat{w}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that

(97)

\hat{A}\circ\hat{T}=T\circ\hat{A}.

Writing
A=b(D-w)
,
T=pD^{2}+qD+r
,
\hat{T}=pD^{2}+\hat{q}D+\hat{r}
,
where
b,w,q,\hat{q},r,\hat{q}\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have

(98)

\displaystyle b{\hat{b}}=p,\quad w+\hat{w}=-\frac{q}{p}+\frac{b^{\prime}}{b}=-\frac{\hat{q}}{p}+\frac{{\hat{b}}^{\prime}}{{\hat{b}}},

(99)

\displaystyle p(w^{\prime}+w^{2})+qw+r=p(\hat{w}^{\prime}+\hat{w}^{2})+\hat{q}\hat{w}+\hat{r}.

Proof.

Set
{\hat{b}}=p/b
and
\hat{w}=-w-q/p-b^{\prime}/b
so that (
98
) holds
by definition. By Proposition
4.1
,

\lambda=\mathrm{R}[T]w=p(w^{\prime}+w^{2})+qw+r

is a constant. Hence, by direct
calculation,

\displaystyle\hat{A}\circ b

\displaystyle=p(D+b^{\prime}/b)+p(w+q/p-b^{\prime}/b)=p(D+w)+q,

\displaystyle\hat{A}\circ A

\displaystyle=(p(D+w)+q)\circ(D-w)

\displaystyle=pD^{2}+qD-p(w^{\prime}+w^{2})-qw

\displaystyle=pD^{2}+qD+r-\lambda=T-\lambda.

Consequently,

(\hat{A}\circ\hat{T}-T\circ\hat{A})\circ A=\hat{A}\circ A\circ T-T\circ\hat{A}\circ A=0.

The algebra of differential operators does not
have zero divisors. Therefore, (
97
) holds. A similar
calculation of
A\circ\hat{A}
yields
(
99
).
∎

Definition 4.4
.

We say that
T,\hat{T}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
are related by an RDT (short for
rational Darboux transformation) whenever there exists a first order
operator
A=b(D-w),\;b,w\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
(
95
) holds. If this is the case, we will call
b(x)

the factorization gauge, and the quasi-rational function
\phi(x)

such that
w=\phi^{\prime}/\phi
the factorization eigenfunction. The
constant
\lambda=(T\phi)/\phi=\mathrm{R}[T]w
will be called the
factorization eigenvalue, and we will write
T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\hat{T}
.

As a direct Corollary of Propositions
4.1
and

4.3
, we have the following.

Corollary 4.5
.

Every
T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\hat{T}
entails the inverse relation

\hat{T}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}T
, and dual factorizations

(100)

T=\hat{A}\circ A+\lambda,\quad\hat{T}=A\circ\hat{A}+\lambda.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
T=pD^{2}+qD+r
be an operator in
\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
, let
\mu(x)
be a
quasi-rational function. Then,

\tilde{T}:=\mathcal{M}(\mu)\circ T\circ\mathcal{M}(\mu^{-1})
is also in
\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
with

(101)

\tilde{T}=pD^{2}+(q-2p\sigma)D+(r-q\sigma-p\sigma^{\prime}+p\sigma^{2}),\quad\sigma=\mu^{\prime}/\mu.

Moreover, if
\phi(x)
is a qr-eigenfunction of
T
with eigenvalue

\lambda
, then
\tilde{\phi}(x)=\mu(x)\phi(x)
is a qr-eigenfunction of

\tilde{T}
with the same eigenvalue.

Proof.

The proof follows by a straightforward calculation.
∎

We already saw that if (
95
) holds with

A=b(D-w)
, then
w\in\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
and
\lambda=\mathrm{R}[T]w\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)
.
This condition is both necessary and sufficient.

Proposition 4.7
.

An RDT
T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\hat{T}
exists if and only if
\lambda\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)
.
For a given
\lambda\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)
, the corresponding RDT is uniquely
determined, up to a rational gauge transformation, by a choice of

w\in\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
.

Proof.

If (
95
) holds, then the corresponding
\lambda
is a qr
eigenfunction by Proposition
4.1
. We now prove the
converse. Let
T=pD^{2}+qD+r
be an operator in
\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
. Let

\lambda\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T),\;w\in\operatorname{Ric}_{\lambda}(T)
, and let
b(x)
be a non-zero
rational function. Let
{\hat{b}},\hat{w}
be as per (
98
), and set

(102)

\displaystyle A=b(D-w),\quad\hat{A}={\hat{b}}(D-\hat{w}),\quad\hat{T}:=A\circ\hat{A}+\lambda.

Relation (
95
) follows by construction. Next, consider
the case of
b(x)=1
and set

\tilde{A}=D-w,\quad\check{A}=p(D+w+q/p),\quad\check{T}=\tilde{A}\circ\check{A}+\lambda.

Observe that
b\tilde{A}=A
. The second claim now follows because

\check{A}\circ\mathcal{M}(b^{-1})=(p/b)(D+w+q/p-b^{\prime}/b)=\hat{A}

\mathcal{M}(b)\circ\check{T}\circ\mathcal{M}(b^{-1})=b\tilde{A}\circ\check{A}\circ\mathcal{M}(b^{-1})+\lambda=A\circ\hat{A}+\lambda=\hat{T}.

∎

Definition 4.8
.

We say that two operators
T,\hat{T}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
are Darboux connected if
there exists an
L\in\operatorname{Diff}(\mathcal{Q})
such that
\hat{T}L=LT
.

Observe that if, in the above relation,
L=\mathcal{M}(\mu),\;\mu\in\mathcal{Q}
is
an operator of order zero, then
T
and
\hat{T}
are related by rational
gauge transformation. In particular, if this is the case, then
quasi-rational eigenfunctions of
\hat{T}
are obtained by multiplying the
quasi-rational eigenfunctions of
T
by
\mu
.

Definition 4.9
.

We define an
n
-step RDT chain

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{2}\cdots T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n}
to be a sequence of operators
T_{0},T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})

such that each arrow is an RDT; i.e., there exist

A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{n}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})
such that

A_{k}T_{k-1}=T_{k}A_{k},\quad k=1,\ldots,n.

If this is the case, we will refer to

\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T_{0})
as
the
eigenvalue sequence
of the chain.

Theorem 4.10

(Theorem 3.10 of
[
24
]
)
.

Suppose that
T,\hat{T}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
are Darboux connected by an
n
th order
intertwiner
L\in\operatorname{Diff}_{n}(\mathcal{Q})
with
n\geq 1
. Then there
exists an
n
-step RDT chain with
T_{0}=T,\;T_{n}=\hat{T}
and

L=A_{n}\cdots A_{1}
.

The following construction characterizes
Darboux connectedness in the case where the eigenvalue sequence
consists of distinct eigenvalues.

Proposition 4.11

(Theorem 2.1 of
[
27
]
)
.

Let
T_{0}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
be as per (
3
). Let

\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
be quasi-rational eigenfunctions of
T_{0}

with
distinct
eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\ldots,\lambda_{n}
;
and let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
be non-zero rational functions. Define

(103)

T_{k}:=pD^{2}+q_{k}D+r_{k},\qquad A_{k}:=b_{k}(D-w_{k}).

where
21
21

21

Since we assume that the eigenvalues

\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
are distinct,

\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
are linearly independent. Hence, the
Wronskians in the denominator of (
104
) are non-zero,
and the functions
\upsilon_{k}
are well-defined.

(104)

\sigma_{k}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\log b_{j})^{\prime},\quad\upsilon_{k}:=\frac{\phi_{(1,2,\ldots,k)}^{\prime}}{\phi_{(1,2,\ldots,k)}},\qquad k=1,\ldots,n;

and

(105)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&q_{k}:=q_{0}+kp^{\prime}-2p\sigma_{k},\\
&r_{k}:=r_{0}+kq_{0}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}k(k-1)p^{\prime\prime}+\upsilon_{k}p^{\prime}-\sigma_{k}(q_{0}+kp^{\prime})+(\sigma_{k}^{2}-\sigma_{k}^{\prime}+2\upsilon_{k}^{\prime})p,\end{split}

(106)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&w_{1}:=\upsilon_{1},\quad w_{k}:=\sigma_{k-1}+\upsilon_{k}-\upsilon_{k-1},\quad k=2,\dots,n.\end{split}

Then

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{2}\cdots T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n}
is an RDT chain. Moreover, up to a rational gauge
transformation,
T_{n}
is invariant with respect to permutations of
the list
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
.

It is also worth noting that Proposition
4.11
is an
extension of the well-known Crum formula for Schrödinger operator.
The latter corresponds to the special case of
p=-1
and
q_{0}=0
and
gauges
b_{1}=\ldots=b_{n}=1
.

As an immediate corollary, we obtain the following.

Proposition 4.12

(Permutability)
.

Let
\lambda\neq{\tilde{\lambda}}
be qr-eigenvalues of
T_{0}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
. Then, the
corresponding RDTs
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}T_{1}
and
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{{\tilde{\lambda}}}}}\xrightharpoonup{{\tilde{\lambda}}}\tilde{T}_{1}

can be extended to a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{T}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

If
\lambda,{\tilde{\lambda}}
are simple qr-eigenfunctions of
T_{0}
, then

T_{2}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
is unique, up to a rational gauge transformation.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
T_{0}
and
T_{j},A_{j},\phi_{j}b_{j},\;j=1,\ldots,n
be as in Proposition

4.11
, and
y(x)
a sufficiently smooth function.
Then,

(A_{n}\cdots A_{1})y=(b_{1}\cdots b_{n})\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n},y]}{\operatorname{Wr}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}]}.

Proof.

The proof rests on three remarks. First, the kernel of an
n
th
order differential operator is
n
-dimensional. Thus, such an
operator is characterized by its leading coefficient and by its
kernel. Second, by inspection, the leading order coefficient of

A_{n}\cdots A_{1}
is
b_{1}\cdots b_{n}
. Third, by well know properties
of the Wronskian determinant, the differential operator

y\mapsto\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n},y]}{\operatorname{Wr}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}]}

is a monic
n
th order differential operator that
annihilates
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
.
∎

4.2.
Formal orthogonality

Proposition 4.14
.

The derivative of a quasi-rational
f(x)
is itself quasi-rational.

Proof.

Let
w=f^{\prime}/f
be the rational log-derivative of the given function.
By direct calculation,

\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime}}=\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}+w

is also rational.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
T=pD^{2}+qD+r
be an operator in
\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
. The symmetric,
Sturm-Liouville form of the eigenvalue equation
Ty=\lambda y
is

(Py)^{\prime}+Ry=\lambda Wy
,
where

(107)

W=W_{T}:=p^{-1}\exp\left(\int\frac{q}{p}\right),\quad P=pW,\quad R=rW

are the indicated quasi-rational functions. Moreover,
T
is
formally self-adjoint with respect to
W
in the sense that for
every
f,g\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have

(108)

\int(fTg-gTf)W=\operatorname{Wr}[f,g]pW,

Proof.

By definition,
(pW)^{\prime}=qW
. Hence, multiplying
Ty=\lambda y
by
W yields the symmetric form of the eigenvalue equation. To prove
the second claim, observe that

(109)

\left(pW\operatorname{Wr}[f,g]\right)^{\prime}=Wf(pg^{\prime\prime}+qg^{\prime})-Wg(pf^{\prime\prime}+qf^{\prime})=(fTg-gTf)W

∎

Definition 4.16
.

Given rational
f(x),g(x)
and a quasi-rational weight
W(x)
, we
will refer to the formal anti-derivative
\int fgW
as the

incomplete inner product
of
f
and
g
with respect to
W
.
Similarly, we will refer to
\int f^{2}W
as the
indefinite
norm
of
f
with respect to
W
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
T\in\operatorname{Diff}_{2}(p,\mathcal{Q})
and let
W=W_{T}
be as per (
107
). Let

\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
be rational eigenfunctions of
T
with eigenvalues

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
. If
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
, then

\operatorname{Wr}[\pi_{1},\pi_{2}]pW
is a constant. If
\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}
, then
the incomplete inner product of

\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
with respect to
W_{T}
is quasi-rational. More formally:

(110)

(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})\int\pi_{1}\pi_{2}W=\operatorname{Wr}[\pi_{1},\pi_{2}]pW.

Proof.

Apply (
108
) to
f=\pi_{1}
and
g=\pi_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\hat{T}
be an RDT with
T=pD^{2}+qD+r
and

\hat{T}=pD^{2}+\hat{q}D+\hat{r}
the corresponding coefficients. Let

\phi,\hat{\phi}
be the dual factorization eigenfunctions and

A=b(D-w),\;\hat{A}={\hat{b}}(D-\hat{w})
the dual intertwiners as per
(
98
). Then, the following relations hold:

(111)

\displaystyle b{\hat{b}}=p,\quad\phi\hat{\phi}=\frac{b}{pW}=\frac{{\hat{b}}}{p\hat{W}},

Proof.

This follows from (
98
) of Proposition
4.3
.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
T,\hat{T},A,\hat{A},W,\hat{W}
be as in Lemma
4.18
. Let

\pi
be a rational eigenfunction of
T
with eigenvalue

{\tilde{\lambda}}\neq\lambda
. Then,
\hat{\pi}=A\pi
is a rational eigenfunction
of
\hat{T}
with eigenvalue
{\tilde{\lambda}}
.
Moreover,
the indefinite norms of
\pi
and
\hat{\pi}
are
related as follows:

(112)

\int\hat{\pi}^{2}\hat{W}-(\lambda-{\tilde{\lambda}})\int\pi^{2}W=\pi\hat{\pi}{\hat{b}}W.

Proof.

The first assertion follows by applying both sides of the
intertwining relation (
95
) to
\pi
. To prove the
second assertion, observe that if
f,g
are rational functions, then
by (
107
), (
111
) and (
98
),

\displaystyle(Af)g\hat{W}+f(\hat{A}g)W=b(f^{\prime}g-wfg)\hat{W}+{\hat{b}}(g^{\prime}f-\hat{w}fg)W

\displaystyle\quad=(f^{\prime}g+g^{\prime}f-(w+\hat{w})fg){\hat{b}}W=\left(fg{\hat{b}}W\right)^{\prime}

Applying the above relation to
f=\pi
and
g=\hat{\pi}
and using the
fact that
\hat{A}A=T-\lambda
yields the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
f_{1}(x),\ldots,f_{j}(x),g_{1}(x),\ldots,g_{k}(x)
be quasi-rational
functions and
x_{0},a\in\mathbb{R}
constants. Define
\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}f
to be the
residue of
f^{\prime}(x)/f(x)
at
x=x_{0}
, and assume that

\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}f_{i}=a,\;i=1,\ldots,j,\quad\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}g_{i}=0,\;i=1,\ldots,k.

Then,

\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{j},g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}]=j(a-k).

Proof.

The statement is evident if either
j=0
or
k=0
. Consider the
case of
k=1
. Observe that

h:=\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{j},g]=(-1)^{j}g^{j+1}\operatorname{Wr}[(f_{1}/g)^{\prime},\ldots,(f_{j}/g)^{\prime}].

Since
\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}(f_{i}/g)^{\prime}=a-1
we must have

\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}h=j(a-1)
. To prove the general
case, we use the identity

\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{j},g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}]=\left(\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{j}]\right)^{k-1}\operatorname{Wr}[h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}],

where

h_{i}=\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{j},g_{i}],\;i=1,\ldots,k.

Since

\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}h_{i}=j(a-1)
and
\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{j}]=aj
, we have

\operatorname{ord}_{x_{0}}\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{j},g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}]=jk(a-1)-aj(k-1)=aj-jk=j(a-k).

∎

Definition 4.21
.

Let
f(x)
be a quasi-rational function of exponential order
0
and
degree
d=\deg f
. We define the leading coefficient of
f
to be

(113)

\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}(f)=\lim_{x\to\infty}x^{-d}f(x).

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
f_{1}(x),\ldots,f_{p}(x)
be qr-functions of exponential order

0
. Then,

(114)

\displaystyle\deg\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{p}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\deg f_{i}-\frac{p(p-1)}{2},

(115)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}\left(\operatorname{Wr}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{p}]\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq p}(\deg f_{j}-\deg f_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{p}\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}(f_{i}).

Proof.

The definition (
113
) is equivalent to the condition that,
asymptotically,
f(x)\sim\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}(f)x^{d}
as
x\to\infty
where

d=\deg f
. Hence, it suffices to prove (
115
) for the
case where
f_{i}(x)=x^{d_{i}},\;i=1,\ldots,p
and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{p}

constants. We leave this as an exercise.
∎

4.3.
Confluent Darboux Transformations

Definition 4.23
.

We say that 2-step
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{2}
is a CDT
(confluent Darboux transformation) if (i)
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
, and (ii)
the RDT is non-trivial in the sense that
T_{0}
and
T_{2}
are not
gauge-equivalent.

Recall that every RDT is invertible, with the inverse RDT having the
same factorization eigenvalue. It therefore makes sense to regard a
2-step factorization chain consisting of an RDT and its inverse as
being trivial. This observation motivates the use of the term
“non-trivial” in the above definition.

Proposition 4.24
.

A given RDT
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}T_{1}
can be extended to a CDT if and only
if the factorization eigenvalue
\lambda
is a degenerate qr-eigenvalue
of
T_{1}
.

Proof.

This follows directly by Proposition
4.7
and by the fact
that
T_{2}
is distinct from
T_{0}
.
∎

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\hat{T}
be an RDT. Let
\phi,\hat{\phi}
be the dual
factorization eigenfunctions and let
W=W_{T}
be the quasi-rational weight as
defined in Lemma
4.15
. Then
\lambda
is a degenerate
eigenvalue of
\hat{T}
if and only if the indefinite norm

\rho:=\int\phi^{2}\,W
defines a quasi-rational function. If
this holds, then
\rho\hat{\phi}
is another, linearly independent qr
eigenfunction of
\hat{T}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\rho
is quasi-rational. Let
\hat{W}=W_{\hat{T}}
be the dual
weight function. Hence, by (
98
) and (
111
),

p\hat{W}\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\phi},\rho\hat{\phi}]=p\hat{W}\hat{\phi}^{2}\rho^{\prime}=p\phi^{2}\hat{\phi}^{2}W\hat{W}=1.

Hence,
(p\hat{W}\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\phi},\rho\hat{\phi}])^{\prime}=0
. Hence, by
(
109
),

(\rho\hat{\phi}\hat{T}\hat{\phi}-\hat{\phi}\hat{T}(\rho\hat{\phi}))\hat{W}=0.

Since
\hat{T}\hat{\phi}=\lambda\hat{\phi}
, we must have

\hat{T}(\rho\hat{\phi})=\lambda\rho\hat{\phi}
. The converse can be argued
using the same manipulations, but only in reverse.
∎

5.
Some examples

5.1.
Isospectral deformation of exceptional class D operators

It is instructive to include a simple example of such
a construction to show the commutativity of the two types of Darboux transformations with explicit expressions for operators and eigenfunctions.

The starting point for this construction is the classical Legendre operator
{\check{T}}
given by

(116)

\check{\tau}=1,\quad{\check{T}}=(x^{2}-1)D_{xx}+2xD_{x},\quad{\check{\pi}}_{n}=\pi(x;0,0),

whose eigenfunctions are the classical Legendre polynomials
{\check{\pi}}_{n}=\pi(x;0,0)
given by (
10
).
We would like to construct a 1-parameter family of class D exceptional operators
T
, indexed by the spectral diagram

\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathrm{D}}(K,L_{1},L_{3},L_{4},a,b)=\Lambda_{\mathrm{D}}(\{1\},\{0\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0,0),

depicted in Figure
10
below.

-1
0
1
2
3
4
i=k-1
\alpha=\beta=1,\;K=\{1\},\;L_{1}=\{0\},\;L_{3}=L_{4}=\emptyset
\triangledown
\sqbullet
…
I_{1+}=\{1,2,3,4,\ldots\},I_{2+}=\{2\},I_{3}=I_{4}=\{1\}
i^{\star}
-7
-6
