-4
-3
-2
I_{1-}=\{-2\},\;I_{2-}=\{-1\}

Figure 10.
Spectral diagram for a type
D
exceptional operator
T

In order to construct
T
we have two possible routes which produce
the same operator in the end via different intermediate operators:

(1)

As explained above in this section, we can perform an
isospectral CDT on the classical Legendre operator
{\check{T}}

corresponding to
L_{1}=\{0\}
to arrive at
\hat{T}
, and then apply a
state-deleting transformation corresponding to
K=\{1\}
.

(2)

Alternatively, we could perform first a state-deleting
transformation corresponding to
K=\{1\}
on the classical Legendre
operator to arrive at
\tilde{T}
, and then an isospectral CDT introducing
a real parameter corresponding to
L_{1}=\{0\}
.

The two constructions can be visualized as the following commutative
diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{T}+2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T+2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{0}\lambda_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{0}\lambda_{0}}

The arrows with the
\lambda_{1}
label represent a rational Darboux
transformation (RDT), while the arrows with label
\lambda_{0}\lambda_{0}

indicates a confluent Darboux transformation (CDT) performed at the
indicated eigenvalue. RDTs are formally defined and discussed in
Section
4.1
below. A CDT is a non-trivial 2-step RDT
performed consecutively a the same eigenvalue. This is discussed in
detail in Section
4.3
below. We also visualize this
commutative diagram in terms of the corresponding spectral diagrams.

\ldots
\leftharpoondown\rightharpoonup
\triangledown
\ldots
\upharpoonleft
\downharpoonright
\upharpoonleft
\downharpoonright
\sqbullet
\ldots
\leftharpoondown\rightharpoonup
\triangledown
\sqbullet
\ldots

In the first route
{\check{T}}\to\hat{T}\to T
, the intermediate operator
\hat{T}

and eigenfunctions are given by formulas
(
85
)– (
88
). Since the matrix
\mathcal{R}
in this case
is just a scalar

\mathcal{R}(x,\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}};L,a,b))=\mathcal{R}(x,(t_{0});\{0\},0,0)=t_{0}+\check{\rho}_{00},\qquad\check{\rho}_{ij}=\int_{-1}^{x}\!\!{\check{\pi}}_{i}{\check{\pi}}_{j},

we have the 1-parameter
\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(t_{0})
family of operators
\hat{T}
given by

\hat{T}=(x^{2}-1)D_{xx}+2xD_{x}+\left(\frac{2x}{1+x+t_{0}}+\frac{2(1-x^{2})}{(1+x+t_{0})^{2}}\right),\qquad t_{0}\in(-\infty,-2)\cup(0,\infty),

which follows directly from (
17
), since

\hat{\tau}=t_{0}+\check{\rho}_{00}=1+x+t_{0}
. The quasi-polynomial
eigenfunctions
\hat{\pi}_{i}
of
\hat{T}
are

\hat{\pi}_{i}={\check{\pi}}_{n_{i}}-\frac{\check{\rho}_{0n_{i}}}{\hat{\tau}},\qquad i\in\hat{I}_{1}=\{-1,1,2,\ldots\},

as given by (
88
), where

n_{i}=\max\{i,-i-1\}.

The first few quasi-polynomial
eigenfunctions of the intermediate operator
\hat{T}
are:

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{-1}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{x+1}{x+t_{0}+1}=\frac{t_{0}}{1+t_{0}+x}

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{1}

\displaystyle=x-\frac{1}{2}\frac{x^{2}-1}{x+t_{0}+1}

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{2}

\displaystyle=x^{2}-\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{3}\frac{-x^{3}+x}{x+t_{0}+1}.

Note that as
t_{0}\to\infty
, we recover the classical Chebyshev
polynomials in their monic normalization. Thus, these exceptional
polynomials can be justly regarded as deformations of classical
polynomials. Their normalization isn’t monic, but rather
asymptotically monic.

The final operator
T
is obtained by performing a state-deleting transformation in
\hat{T}
with
K=\{1\}
, so expressions (
89
) and (
17
) lead to

(117)

\displaystyle\tau(x;t_{0})

\displaystyle=\hat{\pi}_{1}\hat{\tau}=(x+1)^{2}+2xt_{0},\qquad t_{0}\in(-2,0),

(118)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)D_{xx}+4xD_{x}-(x^{2}-1)\left(\frac{8t_{0}(2+t_{0})}{\tau(x;t_{0})^{2}}+\frac{2}{\tau(x;t_{0})^{2}}\right)+2

The first few quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions of
T
, following (
90
) are:

(119)

\displaystyle\pi_{-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{x}-\frac{(x+1)^{2}}{\tau(x;t_{0})},

\displaystyle\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=x+\frac{1}{3x}-\frac{1}{3}\frac{(x^{2}-1)^{2}}{\tau(x;t_{0})},

\displaystyle\pi_{2}

\displaystyle=x^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\frac{(x^{2}-1)^{2}}{\tau(x;t_{0})},

\displaystyle\pi_{3}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-\frac{1}{7}x-\frac{1}{35x}-\frac{1}{35}\frac{(x^{2}-1)^{2}(7x^{2}-1)}{\tau(x;t_{0})}.

The general formula is

\pi_{i}=\frac{1}{n_{i+1}-1}\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{1},\hat{\pi}_{i+1}]}{\hat{\pi}_{1}},\quad i\in\{-2,1,2,\ldots\},

where
n_{-1}=0
and
n_{i}=i
otherwise.

We observe that as
t_{0}\to\infty
, we recover monic polynomials,
suggesting that the above family may also be regarded as an
isospectral deformation, but this time a deformation of an exceptional
family. This observation may be realized by following the second
route described above. It involves first applying a standard
state-deleting transformation with
K=\{1\}
on the classical Legendre
operator
{\check{T}}\to\tilde{T}
, followed by a CDT
\tilde{T}\to T
. The
intermediate operator
\tilde{T}
can be simply written in terms of the
Wronskian formulas (
89
)– (
90
) where all “hat”
variables
(\hat{\tau},\hat{\pi}_{i})
are substituted by “check” variables

(\check{\tau},{\check{\pi}}_{i})
. More specifically, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle={\check{\pi}}_{1}\check{\tau}=x

\displaystyle\tilde{T}

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)D_{xx}+4xD_{x}+\frac{2}{x^{2}},

\displaystyle{\tilde{\pi}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}(i+1)\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[{\check{\pi}}_{1},{\check{\pi}}_{i+1}]}{{\check{\pi}}_{1}},\qquad i\in\{-1,1,2,\ldots\},\qquad\tilde{\chi}(n)=\frac{1}{n-1}.

The first few quasi-rational eigenfunctions of
\tilde{T}
are:

{\tilde{\pi}}_{-1}=\frac{1}{x},\qquad{\tilde{\pi}}_{1}=x+\frac{1}{3x},\qquad{\tilde{\pi}}_{2}=x^{2},\qquad{\tilde{\pi}}_{3}=x^{3}-\frac{2}{7}x-\frac{1}{35x};

we recognize these as the
t_{0}
independent terms in (
119
).
We now need to apply a CDT
\tilde{T}\to T
, following the procedure described above albeit with “check” variables
(\check{\tau},{\check{\pi}}_{i})
replaced by “tilde” variables
(\tilde{\tau},{\tilde{\pi}}_{i})
.
Following (
85
), define (note the shift
\alpha=a+1,\beta=b+1
after the first DT)

{\tilde{\rho}}_{ij}=\int_{-1}^{x}{\tilde{\pi}}_{i}{\tilde{\pi}}_{j}(1-x)(1+x).

The expressions for
\tau
in (
117
) can also be written as

\tau=(2t_{0}-{\tilde{\rho}}_{-1,-1})\tilde{\tau}=(x+1)^{2}+2xt_{0},\qquad t_{0}\in(-2,0),

and likewise the quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions
\pi_{i}
of
T
can be expressed as:

\pi_{i}={\tilde{\pi}}_{n}-\frac{{\tilde{\rho}}_{-1,n}}{-2t_{0}+{\tilde{\rho}}_{-1,-1}},\qquad i=1,2,\ldots

Remark 5.1
.

It is not obvious at all that
{\tilde{\rho}}_{ij}
are rational functions of
x
. This requires the vanishing of the residues at all poles of the integrand. While this can be seen to happen in the general case, a detailed proof will be given elsewhere. This is the reason why, despite the commutativity of both types of Darboux transformations, we chose to apply first the set of CDTs to the classical operator, where it is evident that
\rho_{ij}
in (
85
) are rational (and therefore, also
\hat{\tau}
in (
87
) is rational.

Remark 5.2
.

Observe that in the second route the intermediate operator
\tilde{T}
is not regular, but the CDT
\tilde{T}\to T
produces a regular operator
T
for a certain range of values of the deformation parameter
t_{0}
. The whole study of the regularity of exceptional Jacobi operators is postponed to a forthcoming publication.

5.2.
Norm calculations.

In this section we collect some calculations to illustrate the ideas
behind generalized orthogonality and norms. The basic idea is that if

\alpha,\beta,\alpha+\beta+1\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, then for every quasi-polynomial eigenfunction

\pi_{i},\;i\in I_{1}
of an exceptional operator
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)

there exists a unique constant
\kappa_{i}
such that the quasi-rational
expression

\left(\pi_{i}(x)^{2}-\kappa_{i}\right)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}

possesses a
quasi-rational anti-derivative. If we accept this is true, then for
the regular cases where
\alpha,\beta>-1
and
\tau(x)
does not vanish in

[-1,1]
, we have

\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\pi_{i}(x)^{2}-\kappa_{i}\right)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}dx=0.

Consequently, the corresponding
\mathrm{L}^{2}
norm is given by

(120)

\displaystyle\nu_{i}=\kappa_{i}\nu(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\pi_{i}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}dx,\;i\in I_{1},\quad\text{ where }

\displaystyle\nu(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}dx,

As asserted in Proposition
2.11
, such constants exists
for every quasi-polynomial eigenfunction
\pi_{i},\;i\in I_{1}
of an
exceptional
T
. The interesting and useful fact is that this
approach allows us to define norms even in the non-regular case.

By way of example, let’s consider the norms of the classical Chebyshev
polynomials relative to a monic normalization. Let

\pi_{i}(x):=\pi_{i}(x;1/2,1/2),\;i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be the indicated classical
Jacobi polynomials. According to the above prescription,

\kappa_{i}=\frac{\nu(i;1/2,1/2)}{\nu(1/2,1/2)}=4^{-i}.

Consequently, as asserted by Proposition

6.13
below, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exists
a polynomial
\Pi_{i}(x)
such that

\int\left(\pi_{i}(x)^{2}-4^{-i}\right)(1-x)^{1/2}(1+x)^{1/2}=\Pi_{i}(x)(1-x)^{3/2}(1+x)^{3/2}.

Therefore,

\int_{-1}^{1}\pi_{i}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{1/2}(1+x)^{-3/2}=4^{-i}\nu(1/2,1/2)=2^{-2i-1}\pi.

Next, let us consider the exceptional operator obtained from the
classical
T(1/2,1/2)
by a 1-step type 3 transformation with factorization eigenfunction
\pi_{1}(x;1/2,1/2)
. Applying (
78
) with
K_{1}=K_{2}=K_{4}=\emptyset
and
K_{3}=\{1\}
we obtain

\displaystyle\tau(x)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(x;1/2,1/2)=x-1/2,\quad\alpha=-1/2,\;\beta=3/2,\;I_{1}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}

\displaystyle{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)D_{x}^{2}+(3x-2)D_{x}+\frac{2-x}{(x-1/2)^{2}}

\displaystyle\pi_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{x-1}{i-1/2}\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[\phi(x),{\check{\pi}}_{i}(x)]}{\phi(x)},\;i=0,1,2,\ldots,\quad\text{where }

\displaystyle\phi(x)

\displaystyle=(1-x)^{-1/2}(x-1/2),\quad{\check{\pi}}_{i}(x)=\pi_{i}(x;1/2,1/2)

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{x-1/2}

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=x-1+\frac{1/2}{x-1/2}

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=x^{2}-x+\frac{1}{4}

\displaystyle\ldots

Since
\tau(x)
vanishes at
x=1/2
, the norm integrals

\nu_{i}=\int_{-1}^{1}\pi_{i}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{-1/2}(1+x)^{3/2}dx

diverge and cannot be used to define the constants
\nu_{i}
. The

\nu_{i}
can be meaningfully defined by deforming the contour of
integration to avoid the singularity at
x=1/2
. This works because
the integrand has vanishing residue at the singularity.
By (
79
), the value of these norms is given by

\displaystyle\nu_{i}

\displaystyle=4^{-i}\frac{i+5/2}{i-1/2}\nu(1/2,1/2)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{i}\nu(-1/2,3/2),

where

\displaystyle\kappa_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{4^{-i}}{3}\frac{2i+5}{2i-1}.

Thus, the assertion is that the following anti-derivatives
quasi-rational:

\int\left(\pi_{i}(x)^{2}-\kappa_{i}\right)(1-x)^{-1/2}(1+x)^{3/2},\;i=0,1,2,\ldots

This implies that

\int_{I}\pi_{i}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{-1/2}(1+x)^{3/2}=\kappa_{i}\nu(-1/2,3/2)=\frac{2i+5}{2i-1}2^{-2i-1}\pi,\;i=0,1,2,\ldots

where
I
is
any contour from
x=-1
to
x=1
that avoids the singularity at

x=1/2
. Moreover, observe that asserted by Theorem
2.13
,
the lack of regularity in this example is signalled by the fact that

\kappa_{0}
is negative.

6.
Classical Jacobi operators

In this section, we describe the index sets and the spectral diagrams
of the classical Jacobi operators. The quasi-rational eigenfunctions
of a classical
T(a,b),\;a,b\in\mathbb{R}
are shown in Table

2
. However, if
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
or
a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
is a
negative integer, then the monic version
\pi_{n}(x;a,b)
of the
classical polynomials
P_{n}(x;a,b)
may no longer be well-defined.
This happens in one of two ways. If
a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, then the leading
coefficient of
P_{n}(x;a,b)
will vanish for a finite number of

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
(n+a+b+1)_{n}=0
and consequently
\pi_{n}(x;a,b)

will be undefined. The other possibility occurs if
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
.
Then, for
n\geq a
or
n\geq b
, respectively, the polynomial

\pi_{n}(x;a,b)
gains a zero of order
|a|,|b|
respectively and can
no longer be classed as a type 1 eigenfunction, but rather must be
classed as a type 3 or type 4 eigenfunction with a prefactor of

(1-x)^{-a}
or
(1+x)^{-b}
, respectively.

6.1.
Classical index sets and spectral diagrams.

The following Propositions describe the index sets of classical
operators, and the corresponding spectral diagrams. The proofs
are all elementary and are left to the reader. After this
case-by-case analysis, we confirm that the qr-spectrum of a classical
operator has the expected form (
39
). This is done in
Proposition
6.8
, which is a restriction of
Proposition
2.23
to the case of classical operators.

Throughout, we let
{\check{I}}_{1},{\check{I}}_{2},{\check{I}}_{3},{\check{I}}_{4}
denote the index sets
of a classical operator
T(a,b)
, let

{\check{\sigma}}_{\imath}:=\lambda_{\imath}({\check{I}}_{\imath};a,b),\;\imath=1,2,3,4

denote the corresponding spectral sets, and let
\Lambda(a,b)
denote the
spectral diagram of
T(a,b)
. In those cases where the index sets are
decomposed into lower and upper degrees as

{\check{I}}_{\imath}={\check{I}}_{\imath-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{\imath+}
, we write

{\check{\sigma}}_{\imath-}=\lambda_{\imath}({\check{I}}_{\imath-};a,b),\quad{\check{\sigma}}_{\imath+}=\lambda_{\imath}({\check{I}}_{\imath+};a,b).

Proposition 6.1
.

The index sets of a classical type G operator

{\check{T}}=T(a,b),\;a,b\notin\mathbb{Z}
, are given by

{\check{I}}_{1}={\check{I}}_{2}={\check{I}}_{3}={\check{I}}_{4}=\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The qr-eigenvalues of
{\check{T}}
are
all simple. Moreover,

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({\check{T}})={\check{\sigma}}_{1}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{2}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{3}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{4}
, with

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus
the corresponding labels of

\Lambda(a,b)
.

Proof.

By inspection of (
37
), since
a\pm b\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the
mappings
i\mapsto\lambda_{1}(i;a,b)
and
i\mapsto\lambda_{3}(i;a,b)
are
one-to-one when restricted to
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
a,b\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the
sets
\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z};a,b)
and
\lambda_{3}(\mathbb{Z};a,b)
are disjoint.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

The index sets of a classical type A operator

{\check{T}}=T(a,b),\;a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},b\notin\mathbb{Z}
, are given by

{\check{I}}_{1}={\check{I}}_{4}=\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and
{\check{I}}_{2},{\check{I}}_{3}
as in (
51
).
The type 1 and 4 qr-eigenvalues are simple. The type 2 and 3
eigenvalues are degenerate with
{\check{\sigma}}_{23}:={\check{\sigma}}_{2}={\check{\sigma}}_{3}
.
Moreover,

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({\check{T}})={\check{\sigma}}_{1}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{23}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{4}
, with

\Lambda(a,b):({\check{\sigma}}_{1},{\check{\sigma}}_{23},{\check{\sigma}}_{4})\mapsto(\boxcirc,\boxasterisk,\boxminus)
the corresponding spectral diagram.

Let
{\check{I}}_{3},{\check{I}}_{4},{\check{I}}_{3\pm},{\check{I}}_{4\pm}
be as in
(
65
). Suppose that
a,b,a+b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a-b\in\mathbb{Z}
. If

a>b
, then a finite range of degrees will be missing from
{\check{I}}_{3}

and the resulting index set decomposes into a finite range of lower
degrees
{\check{I}}_{3-}
and an infinite range of higher degrees

{\check{I}}_{4+}
. Similar remarks apply if
b>a
for
{\check{I}}_{4},{\check{I}}_{4\pm}
.
If
a=b
, then
{\check{I}}_{3}={\check{I}}_{4}={\check{I}}_{3+}={\check{I}}_{4+}=\mathbb{N}_{0}
. For
example, if
a-b=4
, then
{\check{I}}_{3-}=\{0,1\}
, and

{\check{I}}_{3+}=\{4,5\ldots\}
; the degrees
\{2,3\}
are missing
from
{\check{I}}_{3}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

The index sets of a classical type B operator

{\check{T}}=T(a,b),\;a,b,a+b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a-b\in\mathbb{Z}
are given by

{\check{I}}_{1}=\mathbb{N}_{0},{\check{I}}_{2}=\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
{\check{I}}_{3},{\check{I}}_{4}
as in (
65
).
The type 1 and 2 eigenvalues are all simple. The

{\check{\sigma}}_{3-},{\check{\sigma}}_{4-}
eigenvalues are simple. The other type 3
and 4 eigenvalues are degenerate with
{\check{\sigma}}_{34}:={\check{\sigma}}_{3+}={\check{\sigma}}_{4+}
.
Moreover, if
a\geq b
, then

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({\check{T}})={\check{\sigma}}_{1}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{2}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{3-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{34},

with

\Lambda(a,b):({\check{\sigma}}_{1},{\check{\sigma}}_{2},{\check{\sigma}}_{3-},{\check{\sigma}}_{34})\mapsto(\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxdiv)
the corresponding spectral
diagram. If
b\geq a
, then

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({\check{T}})={\check{\sigma}}_{1}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{2}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{4-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{34},

with

({\check{\sigma}}_{1},{\check{\sigma}}_{2},{\check{\sigma}}_{4-},{\check{\sigma}}_{34})\mapsto(\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxminus,\boxdiv)
the corresponding spectral diagram.

Proposition 6.4
.

The index sets of a classical type C operator

{\check{T}}=T(a,b),\;a,b,a-b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a+b\in\mathbb{Z}
are given by

{\check{I}}_{1},{\check{I}}_{2}
as given in (
74
) and by

{\check{I}}_{3}=\mathbb{N}_{0},{\check{I}}_{4}=\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The type 3 and 4 eigenvalues are all
simple. The
{\check{\sigma}}_{1-},{\check{\sigma}}_{2-}
eigenvalues are simple. The
other type 1 and 2 eigenvalues are degenerate with

{\check{\sigma}}_{12}:={\check{\sigma}}_{1+}={\check{\sigma}}_{2+}
. If
a+b\geq 0
, then

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({\check{T}})={\check{\sigma}}_{2-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{12}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{3}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{4},

with
\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus
the corresponding
labels.
If
a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, then

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({\check{T}})={\check{\sigma}}_{1-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{12}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{3}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{4},

with

\boxcirc,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus
the corresponding labels.

Proposition 6.5
.

The index sets of a classical CB operator

{\check{T}}=T(a,b),\;a,b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;2a,2b\in\mathbb{Z}
are given by

{\check{I}}_{\imath}={\check{I}}_{\imath-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{\imath+},\;\imath=1,2,3,4,
with the latter as defined in (
65
) and
(
74
). The

{\check{\sigma}}_{1-},{\check{\sigma}}_{2-},{\check{\sigma}}_{3-},{\check{\sigma}}_{4-}
eigenvalues are
simple with
{\check{\sigma}}_{1-}=\emptyset
if
a+b\leq 0
,

{\check{\sigma}}_{2-}=\emptyset
if
a+b\geq 0
,
{\check{\sigma}}_{3-}=\emptyset

if
a\leq b
, and
{\check{\sigma}}_{4-}=\emptyset
if
b\leq a
. All the
other eigenvalues are degenerate with

{\check{\sigma}}_{12}:={\check{\sigma}}_{1+}={\check{\sigma}}_{2+}
and

{\check{\sigma}}_{34}:={\check{\sigma}}_{3+}={\check{\sigma}}_{4+}
. The qr-eigenvalues
decompose as

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({\check{T}})={\check{\sigma}}_{1-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{2-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{12}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{3-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{4-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{34},

with

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus,\boxdiv

as the corresponding labels of the spectral diagram.

Proposition 6.6
.

The index sets of a classical type D operator

{\check{T}}=T(a,b),\;a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are given by (
80
) (
81
).
The
{\check{\sigma}}_{1},{\check{\sigma}}_{3-},{\check{\sigma}}_{4-}
eigenvalues are simple with

{\check{\sigma}}_{3-}=\emptyset
if
b\geq a
and
{\check{\sigma}}_{4-}=\emptyset
if

a\geq b
. All the other eigenvalues are degenerate with

{\check{\sigma}}_{234}:={\check{\sigma}}_{2-}={\check{\sigma}}_{2+}={\check{\sigma}}_{3+}={\check{\sigma}}_{4+}
.
The qr-spectrum decomposes as

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(a,b)={\check{\sigma}}_{1}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{3-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{4-}\sqcup{\check{\sigma}}_{234},

with
\boxcirc,\boxminus,\boxplus,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
as the corresponding
labels of the spectral diagram.

The proof is a consequence of the following Lemma. The
upshot is that all of the rational eigenfunctions corresponding to a

\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
label have the same degree, but belong to 4 distinct
asymptotic classes. The precise description is as follows.

Lemma 6.7
.

Suppose that
a\geq b\in\mathbb{N}
. As per Proposition
6.6
, let

\displaystyle{\check{I}}_{2-}

\displaystyle=\{0,\ldots,b-1\},

\displaystyle{\check{I}}_{2+}

\displaystyle=\{a,\ldots,a+b-1\},

\displaystyle{\check{I}}_{3+}

\displaystyle=\{a-b,\ldots,a-1\},

\displaystyle{\check{I}}_{4+}

\displaystyle=\{b-a,\ldots,b-1\}

be the indicated index sets, let

{\check{\sigma}}_{2-}={\check{\sigma}}_{2+}={\check{\sigma}}_{3+}={\check{\sigma}}_{4+}=\{(k+1)(k-a-b):k=0,1,\ldots,b-1\}

the corresponding
eigenvalues, and
let
E_{k},\;k=0,\ldots,b-1
denote the
corresponding eigenspace of
{\check{T}}
. Then,
E_{k}
is 2-dimensional
and consists of rational functions. Aside from the zero element,
the eigenfunctions in
E_{k}
fall into 4 categories, the first
generic, and the rest belonging to distinguished 1-dimensional
subspaces:

•

type 2 eigenfunctions having index
a+b-k-1\in{\check{I}}_{2+}
and
degree
-k-1
;

•

type 2 eigenfunctions of index
k\in{\check{I}}_{2-}
and
degree
k-a-b
;

•

type 3 eigenfunction of index
a-k-1\in{\check{I}}_{3+}
and degree
-k-1
;

•

type 4 eigenfunctions of index
b-k-1\in{\check{I}}_{4+}
and degree

-k-1
.

Proof.

Fix a
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,b-1\}
and note that the eigenvalues in
question,
(k+1)(k-a-b)
, can be expressed in 4 different was as

\lambda_{2}(k;a,b)=\lambda_{2}(a+b-k-1;a,b)=\lambda_{3}(a-k-1;a,b)=\lambda_{4}(b-k-1;a,b).

As a basis for
E_{k}
we can take

\displaystyle\phi_{3,a-k-1}(x)

\displaystyle=(1-x)^{-a}P_{a-k-1}(x;-a,b),\quad\;k=0,\ldots,b-1,

\displaystyle\phi_{4,b-k-1}(x)

\displaystyle=(1+x)^{-b}P_{b-k-1}(x,a,-b).

These constitute the type 3 and type 4 subspaces of the above list.
The linear combination

\phi_{2}(x,t;k):=2^{b}\phi_{4,b-k-1}(x)-(-2)^{a}t\,\phi_{3,a-k-1}(x),\;t\neq 0

is a
type 2 eigenfunction whose index belongs to either
I_{2-}
or to

I_{2+}
. The classical identity

(121)

2^{b}(x-1)^{a}P_{b-k-1}(x;a,-b)-2^{a}(x+1)^{b}P_{a-k-1}(x;-a,b)=2^{a+b}(-1)^{a}P_{k}(x;-a,-b),

implies that

\phi_{2}(x,1;k)=2^{a+b}(1-x)^{-a}(1+x)^{-b}P_{k}(x;-a,-b)

has
index
k
. By contrast, if
t\neq 1
, then
\deg\phi_{2}=-k-1

and hence the index of
\phi_{2}
is
l:=a+b-k-1
, which by
inspection is an element of
I_{2+}
.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

If
T=T(a,b),\;a,b\in\mathbb{R}
is a classical operator, then its qr
spectrum is given by
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)=\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(a,b)
, with the latter as per
(
38
).

Proof.

As a special case of Lemma
8.4
with
\tau=1
, every
qr-eigenfunction of
T(a,b)
has the form

\phi(x)=\mu_{\imath}(x;a,b)P(x)
for some
\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
polynomial
P(x)
. Hence, by Proposition
2.22
, we
have
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)\subseteq\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(a,b)
. The converse
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(a,b)\subseteq\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(T)
is a direct consequence of the above Propositions.

∎

6.2.
Formal norms of classical operators.

Throughout this section, let
\nu(z;a,b)
be as defined in (
14
), and let

(122)

W(x;a,b):=(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
q\in{\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}},\;a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and suppose that
q(\pm 1)\neq 0
and that

\rho(x)=\int q(x)W(x;a,b)

is a quasi-rational
function. Then, one of the following mutually exclusive
possibilities holds: (i)
\rho\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{a+1,b+1}
; (ii)

\rho\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{0,b+1},\;a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\;\rho(1)\neq 0
; (iii)

\rho\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{a+1,0},\;b\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\;\rho(-1)\neq 0
; (iv)
\rho\in{\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}},\;a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\;\rho(\pm 1)\neq 0
.

Proof.

By assumption,
w(x)=\rho^{\prime}(x)/\rho(x)
is rational. Hence,

(123)

\displaystyle\rho(x)=\frac{q(x)}{w(x)}(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}

(124)

\displaystyle w(x)+\frac{w^{\prime}(x)}{w(x)}-\frac{q^{\prime}(x)}{q(x)}=\frac{a}{x-1}+\frac{b}{x+1}.

By inspection
w(x)
has only simple poles. Hence, either
x=1
is
a simple pole of
w(x)
; in which case,
\operatorname{Res}_{x=1}w(x)=a+1
and

\operatorname{ord}_{x=1}\rho(x)=a+1
. Otherwise,
x=1
is a zero of
w(x)
;
in this case necessarily
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is the multiplicity of that
zero, and
\rho(1)\neq 0
. Similarly, if
x=-1
is a simple pole of

w(x)
, then
\operatorname{Res}_{x=-1}w(x)=b+1
and

\operatorname{ord}_{x=-1}\rho(x)=b+1
. Otherwise,
x=-1
is a zero of
w(x)

with multiplicity
b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\rho(-1)\neq 0
.
∎

Proposition 6.10
.

The first order operators
D_{x}
and

(125)

R(a,b):=(x^{2}-1)D_{x}+a(x+1)+b(x-1),\quad a,b\in\mathbb{R}

serve as a lowering and raising operators for the
classical Jacobi polynomials in
the sense that

(126)

\displaystyle D_{x}P_{n}(x;a,b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(n+a+b+1)P_{n-1}(x;a+1,b+1),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

(127)

\displaystyle R(a,b)P_{n}(x;a,b)

\displaystyle=2(n+1)P_{n+1}(x;a-1,b-1),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

Proof.

By direct calculation,

(128)

D_{x}\circ\mathcal{M}(W(x;a,b))=-\mathcal{M}(W(x;a-1,b-1))\circ R(a,b).

By the Rodrigues formula (
8
),

(129)

-D_{x}\left[W(x;a,b)P_{n}(x;a,b)\right]=2(n+1)W(x;a-1,b-1)P_{n+1}(x;a-1,b-1).

Identity (
127
) follows.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, the formal anti-derivative
\rho(x):=\int W(x;a,b)

defines a quasi-rational function if and only if one of three
mutually exclusive conditions hold (A) either
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},b\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
or

b\in\mathbb{N}_{0},a\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
; (C)
a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
and

a,b\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
; or (H)
a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with opposite signs.

Proof.

If (A) holds, then
\rho(x)
is quasi-rational by inspection.
Observe that

(130)

D_{x}W(x;a+1,b+1)=(2+a+b)W(x;a+1,b)-2(a+1)W(x;a,b)

Hence, if

a+b+1=-1,\;a\neq-1
, then
\rho(x)
is quasi-rational by (
130
). From
there, the sufficiency of (C) and (H) can be shown by induction.

Conversely, suppose that
\rho(x)
is quasi-rational. If

a,b\notin\mathbb{Z}
, then by Lemma
6.9

\rho\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{a+1,b+1}
. Hence, by (
128
) and by
(
127
), we must have
a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
. Suppose that

a\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
. Since
\rho(x)
doesn’t have a logarithmic singularity
at
x=1
, we must have

(131)

\operatorname{Res}_{x=1}(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}=(-1)^{a}2^{1+a+b}\frac{\Gamma(b+1)}{\Gamma(-a)\Gamma(2+a+b)}=-\operatorname{Res}_{z=0}\nu(z;a,b)=0.

This forces
b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, which is condition (H).
Analogous reasoning applies if
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
.
∎

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If
a,b\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
and if
n+a+b+1>0
, then

(132)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi_{n}(x;a,b)^{2}}{\nu(n;a,b)}W(x;a,b)-\frac{W(x;a+n,b+n)}{\nu(a+n,b+n)}=-D_{x}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{{\tilde{\pi}}_{j+1}(x)}{(j+1)}\frac{{\tilde{\pi}}_{j}}{{\tilde{\nu}}_{j}}W(x;a+n-j,b+n-j)\right),

where

\displaystyle{\tilde{\pi}}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle:=\pi_{j}(x;a+n-j,b+n-j),\quad j=0,1,\ldots,n-1;

\displaystyle{\tilde{\nu}}_{j}

\displaystyle:=\nu(j;a+n-j,b+n-j).

Proof.

Set
n^{*}=-n-1-a-b
, and observe that by
(
126
),
(
128
) and (
127
), we have

(133)

\displaystyle D_{x}\left[\pi_{n}(x;a,b)\pi_{n-1}(x;a+1,b+1)W(x;a+1,b+1)\right]

\displaystyle=n^{*}\,\pi_{n}(x;a,b)^{2}W(x;a,b)+n\,\pi_{n-1}(x;a+1,b+1)^{2}W(x;a+1,b+1),

Dividing through by
n\nu(n-1,a+1,b+1)
and using the identity

(n+a+b+1)\nu(n,a,b)=n\nu(n-1,a+1,b+1),

gives

\displaystyle\frac{\pi_{n}(x;a,b)^{2}}{\nu(n;a,b)}W(x;a,b)-\frac{\pi_{n-1}(x;a+1,b+1)^{2}}{\nu(n-1;a+1,b+1)}W(x;a+1,b+1)

\displaystyle\quad=D_{x}\left[\frac{\pi_{n}(x;a,b)}{\nu(n;a,b)}\frac{\pi_{n-1}(x;a+1,b+1)}{n-1}W(x;a+1,b+1)\right]

Relation (
132
) now follows by induction.
∎

Proposition 6.13
.

The formal norms corresponding to the classical Jacobi polynomials

\pi_{n}(x;a,b),\;a,b,a+b+1\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
are given by

\nu_{n}=\nu(n;a,b),\;n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
in the sense of (
23
).

Proof.

Let
W(x;a,b)
be as in (
122
). By Lemma
6.12
,

(134)

\int\frac{\pi_{n}(x;a,b)^{2}}{\nu(n;a,b)}W(x;a,b)-\frac{W(x;a+n,b+n)}{\nu(a+n,b+n)}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{a+1,b+1}.

Identity (
130
) can be restated as

\frac{W(x;a+1,b)}{\nu(a+1,b)}-\frac{W(x;a,b)}{\nu(a,b)}=D_{x}\left[\frac{W(x;a+1,b+1)}{(2+a+b)\nu(a+1,b)}\right].

By induction and by Lemma
6.9
,

(135)

\int\frac{W(x;a+n,b+n)}{\nu(a+n,b+n)}-\frac{W(x;a,b)}{\nu(a,b)}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{a+1,b+1}.

It follows that

\int\left(\pi_{n}(x;a,b)^{2}-\frac{\nu(n;a,b)}{\nu(a,b)}\right)W(x;a,b)\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{a+1,b+1}.

This proves (
23
) for the case where
i=j=n
.
For
i\neq j
, relation (
23
) follows by Lemma
4.17
.
∎

Proposition 6.14
.

Suppose that
a,b\notin\mathbb{Z},a+b+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
and let
{\check{I}}_{1}
be as
defined in (
74
). The formal norms of classical
polynomials
\pi_{n}(x;a,b),\;n\in{\check{I}}_{1}
is also given by

\nu_{n}=(-1)^{n}\nu(a+n,b+n)
, but in the sense of (
24
).
Moreover,

(136)

\nu_{n}=\begin{cases}\nu(n;a,b),&\text{ if }n\in I_{1+}\\
\frac{1}{2}\nu(n;a,b),&\text{ if }2n+a+b+1=0,\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

If
n\in{\check{I}}_{1+}
, that is if
n+a+b+1>0
, then (
134
)
holds by Lemma
6.12
. The argument used in the proof of
Proposition
6.13
applies to show

(137)

\int\frac{W(x;a+n,b+n)}{\nu(a+n,b+n)}-\frac{W(x;a,-a-1)}{\nu(a,-a-1)}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{a+1,-a}.

Hence, writing
m=a+b+1
, we have

\int\left(\pi_{n}(x)^{2}-(1+x)^{-m}\frac{\nu_{n}}{\nu(\alpha,-1-\alpha)}\right)(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{a+1,b+1}

if
n\in{\check{I}}_{1+}
. If

2n+a+b+1<0
, then repeated application of (
133
) and
Proposition
6.11
show that

\int\pi_{n}(x;a,b)^{2}W(x;a,b)
defines a quasi-rational function.
The desired conclusion now follows by Lemma
6.9
.
Finally, consider the vertex case where
2n+a+b+1=0
. Repeated
application of (
133
) shows that

\int\pi_{n}(x;a,b)^{2}W(x;a,b)-(-1)^{n}W(x;a+n,b+n)

is quasi-rational. As above, (
137
) also holds. Finally,
we have

\nu(n;a,b)=2(-1)^{n}\nu(a+n,b+n),

where the value of the
left-side has to be understood in terms of removable singularities.
Relation (
24
) follows.
∎

Proposition 6.15
.

Suppose that
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},b\notin\mathbb{Z}
, and let

(138)

\rho(x;a,b)=\int W(x;a,b)\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{0,b+1}

be the unique quasi-rational anti-derivative of
W(x;a,b)
. Then,

(139)

\rho(1;a,b)=\nu(a,b)

Proof.

If
a=0
, then by inspection,

\nu(0,b)=\frac{2^{b+1}\Gamma(b+1)}{\Gamma(b+2)}=\frac{2^{b+1}}{b+1},\quad\rho(x;0,b)=\frac{(1+x)^{b+1}}{b+1},\quad\rho(1;0,b)=\nu(0;0,b).

By (
130
),

\rho(1;a+1,b)=\frac{2(a+1)}{2+a+b}\rho(1;a,b),\quad a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\;b\notin\mathbb{Z}.

The desired conclusion now follows by induction on
a
.
∎

7.
Rational Darboux transformations of exceptional Jacobi operators

7.1.
The four types of Jacobi rational Darboux transformations.

The class of exceptional Jacobi operators enjoys four types of
rational Darboux transformations, corresponding to the four asymptotic types of the Jacobi-class
factorization eigenfunctions as detailed in Definition

2.17
. In each case, the choice of factorization gauge is
fixed by the assumption that the operators in question are in the
rational gauge. In light of this fact, we will refer to these
transformations as Jacobi RDTs of type 1,2,3,4, respectively. We also
show that all Jacobi RDTs involve a
\pm 1
shift of the parameters

(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto({\hat{\alpha}},{\hat{\beta}})
. Type 1 and type 2 transformations
also involve a spectral shift
\epsilon
. By contrast, type 3 and
type 4 transformations are isospectral.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
be an exceptional
Jacobi operator. Let
\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, and set

(140)

({\hat{\imath}},{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath},\epsilon_{\imath})=\begin{cases}(2,\alpha+1,\beta+1,\alpha+\beta+2)&\text{ if }\imath=1,\\
(1,\alpha-1,\beta-1,-\alpha-\beta)&\text{ if }\imath=2,\\
(4,\alpha-1,\beta+1,0)&\text{ if }\imath=3,\\
(3,\alpha+1,\beta-1,0)&\text{ if }\imath=4.\end{cases}

For a given
k\in I_{\imath}(T)
, let

\phi_{\imath k}(x)=\mu_{\imath}(x)\pi_{\imath k}(x)
be the corresponding
eigenfunction as per (
32
) with eigenvalue

\lambda_{\imath k}=\lambda_{\imath}(k;\alpha,\beta)
. Let
\hat{\tau}_{\imath k}
be as defined
in (
32
) and set

(141)

\hat{T}_{\imath k}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{\imath k};{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath})

Let
w_{\imath k}:=\phi_{\imath k}^{\prime}/\phi_{\imath k}
be the
corresponding log derivatives and set

(142)

\displaystyle A_{1k}

\displaystyle:=D_{x}-w_{1k},

\displaystyle A_{2k}

\displaystyle:=(x^{2}-1)(D_{x}-w_{2k})

\displaystyle A_{3k}

\displaystyle:=(x-1)(D_{x}-w_{3k})

\displaystyle A_{4k}

\displaystyle:=(x+1)(D_{x}-w_{4k})

Next, set

(143)

\hat{\phi}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}(x)=\frac{\mu_{\hat{\imath}}(x;{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath})}{\pi_{\imath k}(x)},\quad\hat{k}=-k

and let
\hat{w}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}=\hat{\phi}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}^{\prime}/\hat{\phi}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}
be the corresponding
rational log-derivatives. Let
\hat{A}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}
be defined analogously to
(
142
), but with
w_{\imath k}
replaced by
\hat{w}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}
.

Proposition 7.1
.

With the above definitions,
T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{{\imath k}}}}\xrightharpoonup{{\imath k}}\hat{T}_{\imath k}+\epsilon_{\imath}

is an RDT
22
22

22

Since the qr-eigenvalue
\lambda_{\imath k}
is fully
determined by the type
\imath
and the index
k
, we sometimes
denote the corresponding RDT by
\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{{\imath k}}}}\xrightharpoonup{{\imath k}}
instead of the more
cumbersome
\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{\imath k}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{\imath k}}
.
with intertwining relations

(144)

A_{\imath k}\circ T=(\hat{T}_{\imath k}+\epsilon_{\imath})\circ A_{\imath k},\quad\hat{A}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}\circ(\hat{T}_{\imath k}+\epsilon_{\imath})=T\circ\hat{A}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}},

and factorizations

(145)

T=\hat{A}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}\circ A_{\imath k}+\lambda_{\imath k},\quad\hat{T}_{\imath k}=A_{\imath k}\circ\hat{A}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}+\lambda_{\imath k}-\epsilon_{\imath}.

Moreover,
\hat{k}\in I_{\hat{\imath}}(\hat{T}_{\imath k})
and

\hat{\phi}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}
, as defined in (
143
), is the
corresponding eigenfunction of
\hat{T}_{\imath k}
with eigenvalue

\lambda_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}=\lambda_{\imath k}-\epsilon_{\imath}
.

Proof.

We give the proof for the case
\imath=1
. The other cases are
argued similarly. First, we establish that
\hat{\phi}_{2\hat{k}}
is an
eigenfunction of
\hat{T}_{1k}
by showing that

(146)

\mathrm{R}[T]w_{1}=\mathrm{R}[\hat{T}_{1}]\hat{w}_{2}+\sigma_{1}

By inspection,

w_{1}=\hat{u}-u,\quad\hat{w}_{2}=-w_{1}-\eta,

where

u=\tau^{\prime}/\tau,\;\hat{u}=\hat{\tau}^{\prime}/\hat{\tau}
and with
\eta=\eta(x;\alpha,\beta)

as in (
5
). Also, observe that

(147)

\mathrm{R}[T(\alpha,\beta)]w_{1}=(x^{2}-1)\left(w_{1}^{\prime}+w_{1}^{2}+\eta\,w_{1}\right)

Hence, by (
17
) (
141
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}[T]w_{1}-\mathrm{R}[\hat{T}_{1}]\hat{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{R}[T(\alpha,\beta)]w_{1}-\mathrm{R}[T(\alpha+1,\beta+1)]\hat{w}_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2(x^{2}-1)(u-\hat{u})^{\prime}+2x(u-\hat{u})

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)\left((w_{1}-\hat{w}_{2})^{\prime}+w_{1}^{2}-\hat{w}_{2}^{2}+\eta(w_{1}-\hat{w}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-2x\hat{w}_{2}-2(x^{2}-1)w_{1}^{\prime}-2xw_{1}

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)(2w^{\prime}_{1}+\eta^{\prime})-2x\hat{w}_{2}-2(x^{2}-1)w_{1}^{\prime}-2xw_{1}

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)\eta^{\prime}+2x\eta=\alpha+\beta+2=\sigma_{1}

By (
29
), we have

\deg\hat{\phi}_{2k}=-{\hat{\alpha}}_{1}-{\hat{\beta}}_{1}+\deg\tau-\deg\hat{\tau}.

Hence,

-\deg\hat{\phi}_{2k}-{\hat{\alpha}}_{1}-{\hat{\beta}}_{1}-1=\deg\hat{\tau}+\deg\tau-1=k-1=\hat{k}.

Hence,
\hat{k}\in I_{2}(\hat{T}_{2k})
by (
31
). Moreover,

\lambda_{2,\hat{k}}=\lambda_{12}(\hat{k};{\hat{\alpha}}_{2},{\hat{\beta}}_{2})=(k-1)(k-1+\alpha+\beta+3)=k(k+\alpha+\beta+1)-\alpha-\beta-2=\lambda_{1,k}-\epsilon_{1}.

Next, we prove (
145
). Applying (
102
) with

b=1,\,p=x^{2}-1,\;w=w_{1,k}
gives

{\hat{b}}=x^{2}-1,\quad\hat{w}=-w_{1}-\eta(x;\alpha,\beta)=\hat{w}_{2}.

Hence,

A=b(D_{x}-w)
and
\hat{A}={\hat{b}}(D_{x}-\hat{w})
match the definitions of

A_{1,k}
and
\hat{A}_{2,\hat{k}}
in (
142
). The intertwining
relations (
144
) now follows by Proposition

4.7
. The factorization relations follow by
Corollary
4.5
.
∎

Proposition 7.2
.

Jacobi-class RDTs are covariant with respect to the gauge
transformations (
30
). Explicitly, let

T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be an exceptional Jacobi operator and let

\hat{T}_{\imath k}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{\imath k};{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath})
a partner
operator as defined in (
141
). Then,

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,\beta)\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\tilde{\imath}k}}}\xrightharpoonup{\tilde{\imath}k}{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{\imath k};-{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath})

is also a
Jacobi-class RDT where
(\imath,\tilde{\imath})
is one of

(1,3),(3,1),(2,4),(4,2)
. Similarly,

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,-\beta)\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\tilde{\imath},-k-1}}}\xrightharpoonup{\tilde{\imath},-k-1}{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{\imath k};{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},-{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath})

is also a
Jacobi-class RDT where
(\imath,\tilde{\imath})
is one of

(1,4),(4,1),(2,3),(3,2)
. Finally,

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,-\beta)\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\tilde{\imath},-k-1}}}\xrightharpoonup{\tilde{\imath},-k-1}{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{\imath k};-{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},-{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath})

is also a
Jacobi-class RDT where
(\imath,\tilde{\imath})
is one of

(1,2),(2,1),(3,4),(4,3)
.

Proof.

This follows from the form of
\mu_{\imath}
show in Table

1
, (
30
), Proposition

4.6
, from the definition of
\hat{\tau}_{\imath k}
in
(
32
).
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

Jacobi-class RDTs are covariant with respect to the transformation

x\mapsto-x
. Explicitly, let

T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
be an exceptional Jacobi operator and let

\hat{T}_{\imath k}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{\imath k};{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath})
a partner
operator as defined in (
141
). Set

\tilde{\tau}(x)=\tau(-x),\quad\tilde{\tau}_{\imath k}(x)=\hat{\tau}_{\imath k}(-x),\quad\tilde{T}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tilde{\tau};\beta,\alpha),\quad\tilde{T}_{\imath k}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tilde{\tau}_{\imath k};{\hat{\beta}}_{\imath},{\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath}).

Then,
\tilde{T}

is also an exceptional operator and
\tilde{T}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\tilde{T}_{\imath k}
a type

\tilde{\imath}
Jacobi RDT, where
(\imath,\tilde{\imath})
is one
of
(1,1),(2,2),(3,4),(4,3)
.

7.2.
Primitive RDT

In this section, we take note of the fact that classical Jacobi
operators can be Darboux-connected to other classical operators.

Definition 7.4
.

We say that
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}T_{1}
is a
primitive
RDT if both

T_{0}
and
T_{1}
are classical operators
23
23

23

In quantum
mechanics, the presence of such primitive factorizations leads to

T_{0}
being called “shape invariant”.
.

Observe that
D_{x}=A_{1,0}
and
R(a,b)=A_{2,0}
, as defined in
(
127
) and (
142
) with

w_{1,0}=0,\quad w_{2,0}=\frac{a}{x-1}+\frac{b}{x+1}.

Thus,

D_{x}
and
R(a,b)
should be justly regarded as the intertwiners of
type 1 primitive RDTs and are the factors of the following primitive
factorizations:

T(a,b)=R(a,b)\circ D_{x},\quad T(a+1,b+1)+2+a+b=D_{x}\circ R(a,b).

There is an analogous pair of shift operators related to the
type 3,4 primitive RDT. We summarize the possibilities in the
following result. The proof is a special case of Proposition

7.1
.

Proposition 7.5
.

The primitive RDTs of a classical operator
T_{0}=T(a,b)

are
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{\imath}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{\imath}}T_{\imath},\;\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
where

\lambda_{\imath}=\lambda_{\imath}(0;a,b)
and where

\displaystyle T_{1}=T(a+1,b+1)+2+a+b,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=0,

\displaystyle T_{2}=T(a-1,b-1)-a-b,

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}=-a-b,\;a,b\neq 0

\displaystyle T_{3}=T(a-1,b+1),

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}=-a(b+1),\;a\neq 0

\displaystyle T_{4}=T(a+1,b-1),

\displaystyle\lambda_{4}=-b(a+1),\;b\neq 0

Note that there are instances where the “ground state” index 0 may
be missing from one of the index sets. In such cases, the classical

T(a,b)
will not have an RDT of that type. For example if
a=0
, the
operator
T(a,b)
will not have type 2 and type 3 qr-eigenfunctions
for the simple reason that
(1-x)^{-a}
is just the constant function.
Such operators will have
{\check{I}}_{2}={\check{I}}_{3}=\emptyset
and will not have
type 2 and 3 primitive RDTS.

The following results (Propositions
7.7
-

7.12
) are all direct consequences of Proposition

7.5
and of the results in Section
6.1
.
The details are left to the reader.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
T_{0}=T(a,b),\;a+b\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
be a classical operator and let

k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
T_{0}
is Darboux connected to

T_{k}=T(a+k,b+k)+k(a+b+k+1)
by a primitive type 1 chain whose
eigenvalue sequence is
\lambda_{j}:=\lambda_{1}(j;a,b),j=0,1,\ldots,k-1
.
The spectral diagrams of
T_{0}
and
T_{k}
differ by

\boxcirc\to\boxtimes
flips at the eigenvalues

\lambda_{j},\;j=0,\ldots,k-1
.

Proposition 7.7
.

A classical type A operator
T(a,b),\;a\in\mathbb{N},b\notin\mathbb{Z}
is
Darboux connected to the classical type A operator
T_{a}:=T(0,a+b)

by a primitive type 3 chain whose eigenvalue sequence is

\lambda_{j}:=\lambda_{3}(j;a,b),j=0,1,\ldots,a-1
. The spectral
diagrams of
T_{0}
and
T_{a}
differ by
\boxcirc\to\boxasterisk
flips
at these eigenvalues.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
T_{0}=T(a,b),\;a,b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a-b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be a classical type B
operator and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
T_{0}
is Darboux connected to

. The spectral

and

differ by

flips at
these eigenvalues.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let

flips at these

. The spectral diagrams of

and
T_{k}
differ by
\boxminus\to\boxdiv
flips at these
eigenvalues.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let

.

b\geq a
,

and

flips.

. The spectral diagrams of
