7.3.
Transformations of the spectral diagram

In the preceding section, we established that exceptional Jacobi
operators are interrelated by four types of RDTs according to the
asymptotic type of the corresponding factorization eigenfunction. In
the present section, we describe the action of these 4 types of RDTs
on the corresponding spectral diagrams. These results will allow us,
in the following section, to furnish proofs of Theorem
2.30
,
Theorem
2.33
, and Theorem
2.35
.

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
be an exceptional
operator and

T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{{\imath k}}}}\xrightharpoonup{{\imath k}}\hat{T}_{\imath k}+\epsilon_{\imath},\;\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\},\,k\in I_{\imath}(T)
a Jacobi RDT as described by (
140
) and
(
141
). Then,
\hat{T}_{\imath k}
is also an exceptional operator.
The spectral diagrams of
T
and
\hat{T}_{\imath k}+\epsilon_{\imath}
differ
only by a label change at
\lambda_{\imath k}=\lambda_{\imath}(k;\alpha,\beta)
.

The proof requires the following Lemma.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
T,\hat{T}_{\imath k}
be as above. Let

\phi_{\jmath\ell}(x),\;\jmath\in\{1,2,3,4\},\,\ell\in I_{\jmath}(T)
be another qr-eigenfunction of
T
such that

\lambda_{\imath k}\neq\lambda_{\jmath\ell}
. Then

\hat{\varphi}_{\jmath\hat{\ell}}:=A_{\imath k}\phi_{\jmath\ell}
is a non-zero
qr-eigenfunction of
\hat{T}_{\imath k}
of asymptotic type
\jmath
that
satisfies

(148)

\hat{T}_{\imath k}\hat{\varphi}_{\jmath\hat{\ell}}=(\lambda_{\jmath\ell}-\epsilon_{\imath})\hat{\varphi}_{\jmath\hat{\ell}}.

Proof.

By (
145
) and by assumption,

(149)

\hat{A}_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}\hat{\varphi}_{\jmath\hat{\ell}}=(T-\lambda_{\imath k})\phi_{\jmath\ell}=(\lambda_{\jmath\ell}-\lambda_{\imath k})\phi_{\jmath\ell}\neq 0.

Applying
A_{\imath k}
to both sides of the above equation and using the
factorization (
145
) yields (
148
).

We now prove the second assertion. By the assumption that

\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, we see that
\phi_{\jmath\ell}(x)
is singular
at
x=\pm 1
if and only if

w_{\jmath\ell}=\phi_{\jmath\ell}^{\prime}/\varphi_{\jmath\ell}
has a first
order pole at that point. By inspection of (
142
), the
application of
A_{\imath k}
or
A_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}
cannot introduce a singularity
at
x=\pm 1
. Therefore,
\phi_{\jmath\ell}(x)
is regular at

x=\pm 1
if and only if
\hat{\varphi}_{\jmath\hat{\ell}}(x)
is regular at
that point.

∎

Proof of Proposition
7.13
.

By Lemma
7.14
, if
\lambda\neq\lambda_{\imath k}
is a qr
eigenvalue of
T
, then it is also a qr-eigenvalue of
\hat{T}_{\imath k}

with the same asymptotic label. In particular, if

\lambda\neq\lambda_{\imath k}
is a quasi-polynomial eigenvalue of
T
, then it
is also a quasi-polynomial eigenvalue of
\hat{T}_{\imath k}
. Thus a 1-step
RDT can add or remove at most 1 eigenvalue from the quasi-polynomial
spectrum. Therefore
\hat{T}_{\imath k}
is also exceptional.

Consider how the RDT
T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{{\imath k}}}}\xrightharpoonup{{\imath k}}\hat{T}_{\imath k}+\epsilon_{i}
modifies the
spectral diagram at
\lambda_{\imath k}
. By construction, we have

A_{\imath k}\phi_{\imath k}=0
. Thus,
A_{\imath k}\phi_{\imath k}
cannot serve as a qr
eigenfunction of
\hat{T}_{\imath k}
at
\lambda_{\imath k}
. Instead, by
Proposition
7.1
, that role is taken up by

\hat{\phi}_{{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}}
, as defined in (
140
) and
(
143
). Hence, the RDT changes the asymptotic label at

\lambda_{\imath k}
and at no other qr-eigenvalue.
∎

In order to describe the transformation of the index sets induced by
an RDT, we set

\bar{I}_{2}=-I_{2}-1,\quad\bar{I}_{4}=-I_{4}-1,

and define the combined index sets as

(150)

I_{12}:=I_{1}\cup\bar{I}_{2},\quad I_{34}:=I_{3}\cup\bar{I}_{4}.

The combined index sets undergo the following transformations.

Proposition 7.15
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
be an exceptional
operator and

T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{{\imath k}}}}\xrightharpoonup{{\imath k}}\hat{T}_{\imath k}+\epsilon_{\imath},\;\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\},\,k\in I_{\imath}(T)
a Jacobi RDT as described by (
140
) and
(
141
). Further suppose that
the factorization eigenvalue
\lambda_{\imath k}
is a simple qr
eigenvalue of
T
.
Let
\hat{I}_{12},\hat{I}_{34}
be the combined index
sets of
\hat{T}_{\imath k}
. If
\imath=1
, we have

\hat{I}_{12}\supseteq I_{12}-1,\;\hat{I}_{34}\supseteq I_{34}
; for

\imath=2
, we have

\hat{I}_{12}\supseteq I_{12}+1,\;\hat{I}_{34}\supseteq I_{34}
; for

\imath=3
, we have

\hat{I}_{12}\supseteq I_{12},\;\hat{I}_{34}\supseteq I_{34}-1
; for

\imath=4
, we have

\hat{I}_{12}\supseteq I_{12},\;\hat{I}_{34}\supseteq I_{34}+1.

Proof.

Let

\phi_{\jmath\ell}(x),\;\jmath\in\{1,2,3,4\},\,\ell\in I_{\jmath}(T)
be another qr-eigenfunction of
T
such that

\lambda_{\imath k}\neq\lambda_{\jmath\ell}
. Let us consider the index of

\hat{\varphi}_{\jmath\hat{\ell}}:=A_{\imath k}\phi_{\jmath\ell}
. We focus on
the case of
\imath=1
; the other cases are argued similarly. By
inspection of
A_{1k}
defined in (
142
),

\deg\hat{\varphi}\leq\deg\phi-1
. Since
{\hat{\imath}}=2
, by inspection
of
\hat{A}_{2k}
defined in (
142
) and by
(
149
),
\deg\phi\leq\deg\hat{\varphi}+1
. Hence

\deg\hat{\varphi}=\deg\phi-1
. Suppose that
\jmath=1
. By the
definition of index in (
31
) and (
32
), we
see that
\hat{\varphi}
has index
\hat{\ell}=\ell-1
. Next, suppose that

\jmath=2
. Since
{\hat{\alpha}}=\alpha+1,{\hat{\beta}}=\beta+1
, it follows that

\hat{\ell}=\deg\hat{\varphi}+{\hat{\alpha}}+{\hat{\beta}}=\deg\phi-1+\alpha+\beta+2=\ell+1.

Next, consider the case of
\jmath=3
. Now

\hat{\ell}=\deg\hat{\varphi}+{\hat{\alpha}}=\deg\varphi+\alpha=\ell.

Finally, consider
the case of
\jmath=4
. Now, also,

\hat{\ell}=\deg\hat{\varphi}+{\hat{\beta}}=\deg\varphi-1+\beta+1=\ell.

Next, let us consider the transformation of the index sets

I_{\imath}\to\hat{I}_{\imath},\;\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
by the RDT in
question. If
\jmath\neq\imath
, then the index of

A_{\imath k}\phi_{j\ell},\;\ell\in I_{\jmath}
undergoes the
transformation described above. If
\imath=1
, then the RDT removes

k
from
I_{1}
and adds
-k
to
\hat{I}_{2}
. This conforms to a shift of

I_{12}
by -1. If
\imath=2
, then the RDT removes
k
from
I_{2}

and adds
-k
to
\hat{I}_{1}
. This conforms to a shift of
I_{12}
by

+1
. The case of type 3 and 4 RDTs is argued similarly.
∎

Remark 7.16
.

Remark: since the RDT relation is symmetric, the above result implies
that if the dual eigenfunction

\lambda_{{\hat{\imath}},\hat{k}}=\lambda_{\imath k}-\epsilon_{\imath}
is also a simple qr
eigenfunction of
\hat{T}_{\imath k}
then the
\supseteq
symbol above can be
replaced by equality. For example, this is the case for operators
belonging to the generic G class. The reason behind this subtlety and
the reason behind the requirement that
\lambda_{\imath k}
be simple is that
the qr-eigenfunctions associated with eigenvalues different from

\lambda_{\imath k}
retain their asymptotic type and merely undergo an index
shift by one of
-1,0,1
. At the eigenvalue
\lambda_{\imath k}
the effect of
the RDT is complicated. If
\lambda_{\imath k}
is simple, but
\lambda_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}
is
degenerate, then new qr-eigenfunctions are created and new indices are
added to
\hat{I}
. Conversely, if
\lambda_{\imath k}
is degenerate, but

\lambda_{{\hat{\imath}}\hat{k}}
is simple, then some qr-eigenfunctions are annihilated,
and some indices are removed from
\hat{I}
. The specifics of such
transformations are described by the various flip alphabets introduced
below. The main utility of Proposition
7.15
is to
describe the index shifts of the qr-eigenfunctions unrelated to the
factorization eigenvalue.

7.4.
Labels and flips

Definition 7.17
.

Let
X
be a collection of exceptional Jacobi operators. We define
the
X

label set
to be the collection of the possible

\lambda
-labels of all the qr-eigenvalues
\lambda
of all the operators
in
X
. If
X
is closed under Jacobi RDTs, we further define the

X

flip alphabet
to be the set of the possible
transformations
24
24

24

As demonstrated in Proposition

7.13
, an RDT changes exactly one label of a spectral
diagram.
of that label set engendered by the 4 types of Jacobi
RDTs.

Proposition 7.18
.

The GABCD degeneracy classes are all closed under Jacobi RDTs. The
G-class label set is
\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxplus,\boxminus
. The
G-class flip alphabet is

(151)

1:\;\boxcirc\to\boxtimes;\quad 2:\;\boxtimes\to\boxcirc;\quad 3:\;\boxplus\to\boxminus;\quad 4:\;\boxminus\to\boxplus.

The A-class label set is
\boxcirc,\boxasterisk,\boxminus
. The A-class
flip alphabet is:

(152)

1:\;\boxcirc\to\boxasterisk;\quad 2:\;\boxasterisk\to\boxcirc;\quad 3:\;\boxasterisk\to\boxminus;\quad 4:\;\boxminus\to\boxasterisk.

The B-class label set is

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\boxminus,\boxplus,\boxdiv
. The type B-class
flip alphabet is:

(153)

\displaystyle 1:\;\boxcirc\mapsto\boxtimes;

\displaystyle 2:\;\boxtimes\mapsto\boxcirc,

\displaystyle 3:\boxplus\mapsto\boxdiv,\;\boxdiv\mapsto\boxminus,\;

\displaystyle 4:\boxminus\mapsto\boxdiv,\;\boxdiv\mapsto\boxminus.

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxplus}\mapsto\boxed{\boxminus}

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxminus}\mapsto\boxed{\boxplus}

The C-class label set is

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\boxminus,\boxplus
. The C-class
flip alphabet is:

(154)

\displaystyle 1:\boxcirc\mapsto\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\;\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\mapsto\boxtimes,\;

\displaystyle 2:\boxtimes\mapsto\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\;\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\mapsto\boxcirc,

\displaystyle 3:\boxplus\mapsto\boxminus,

\displaystyle 4:\boxminus\mapsto\boxplus.

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxcirc}\mapsto\boxed{\boxtimes}

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxtimes}\mapsto\boxed{\boxcirc}

The CB-class label set is

\boxcirc,\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\boxminus,\boxplus,\boxdiv
. The CB-class flip alphabet is:

(155)

\displaystyle 1:\boxcirc\mapsto\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\;\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\mapsto\boxtimes,

\displaystyle 2:\boxtimes\mapsto\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes,\;\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\mapsto\boxcirc,

\displaystyle 3:\boxplus\mapsto\boxdiv,\;\boxdiv\mapsto\boxminus,

\displaystyle 4:\boxminus\mapsto\boxdiv,\;\boxdiv\mapsto\boxminus,

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxcirc}\mapsto\boxed{\boxtimes},

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxtimes}\mapsto\boxed{\boxcirc},

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxplus}\mapsto\boxed{\boxminus},

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxminus}\mapsto\boxed{\boxplus}.

The D-class label set is

\boxcirc,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}},\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},\boxplus,\boxminus
. Type
D-class flip alphabet is

(156)

\displaystyle 1:\;\boxcirc,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},

\displaystyle 2:\;\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\boxcirc,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}},

\displaystyle 3:\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\boxminus,\boxplus\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},

\displaystyle 4:\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\boxplus,\boxminus\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},\;

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxplus}\mapsto\boxed{\boxminus},

\displaystyle\boxed{\boxminus}\mapsto\boxed{\boxplus}

The proof is broken up into the following Lemmas
7.19

-
7.34
.

Lemma 7.19
.

The label sets of a classical GABC Jacobi operators are as given in
Proposition
7.18
. The label set of classical type D
operators is
\boxcirc,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},\boxplus,\boxminus
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Propositions
6.1

–
6.6
.
∎

Definition 7.20
.

Let
T_{0}=T(a,b),\;a,b\in\mathbb{N}
be a type D operator with non-zero
parameters and let
{\check{I}}_{2}={\check{I}}_{2-}\sqcup{\check{I}}_{2+}
be the
decomposition of the type 2 index set defined in (
81
).
For
k\in{\check{I}}_{2+}
, we call corresponding

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{2,k}}}\xrightharpoonup{2,k}T_{1},\;k\in{\check{I}}_{2+}
a
para-Jacobi RDT
.

As indicated in Table
3
and Propositions

6.6
and
7.19
, the
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
label
associated to a classical type D operator is characterized by the fact
that the corresponding 2-dimensional eigenspace consists entirely of
rational functions. It is this degenerate nature of the
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}

eigenvalues that lies at the core of the para-Jacobi phenomenon. The
terminology comes from
[
8
]
; this paper studied considered the
classical operators
T(-a,-b),\;a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. These operators are
gauge-equivalent to classical type D operators and the conjugation
(
30
) transforms the
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}
eigenvalues of
T(a,b)

into hyper-degenerate eigenvalues of
T(-a,-b)
. The characteristic
property of these hyper-degenerate eigenvalues is that the
corresponding 2-dimensional eigenspace is entirely polynomial.

Lemma 7.21
.

Let
T_{0}
be a classical operator GABCD operator, and

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}
a 1-step RDT that
is not
para-Jacobi.
Then,
T_{1}
can be Darboux-connected to a classical operator using a
chain whose eigenvalue sequence excludes
\lambda_{1}
and that consists of
operators belonging to the same degeneracy class as
T_{0}
.

Proof.

The desired conclusion for type G operators follows by Proposition

7.6
, by gauge-covariance (Proposition

7.2
), and by permutability (Proposition

4.12
).

For the case of type A operators, because of gauge-covariance, it
suffices to consider the type 1 and type 3 RDTs. In the first
instance, the conclusion follows by Proposition
7.6
. In
the second instance, the conclusion follows by Proposition

7.7
, by observing that
{\check{\sigma}}_{3}
consists of
a

distinct eigenvalues, and by applying permutability.

Consider the case of where
T_{0}
is a type B classical operator.
For type 1 and type 2 RDTs, the desired conclusion follows by
Proposition
7.6
, by permutability and by
gauge-covariance. For type 3 RDTs, the argument splits into two
subcases. If
\lambda\in{\check{\sigma}}_{3-}
, the conclusion follows by
Proposition
7.9
and permutability. If

\lambda\in{\check{\sigma}}_{3+}
, we have to also invoke Proposition

7.8
. The argument for type 4 RDTs is the same. The
proof for C and CB operators is analogous to the
proof for the B class.

Suppose that
T_{0}
is a type D classical operator where WLOG

a\geq b
. For type 1 RDTs, the conclusion follows by Proposition

7.6
. For type 3 and type 4 RDTs the conclusion follows by
Propositions
7.11
and
7.12
. For a type 2
RDT, that
is not
para-Jacobi, the conclusion follows by
Proposition
7.10
.
∎

Lemma 7.22
.

The flips admitted by classical G operators is given by
(
151
).

Proof.

Suppose that
T_{0}=T(a,b),\;a,b,a\pm b\notin\mathbb{Z}
is a class G
operator and
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{1,k}}}\xrightharpoonup{1,k}T_{1},\;k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
a type 1 RDT. By
Proposition
7.6
and by permutability, we can extend the
RDT by a chain that connects
T_{1}
to a classical
T_{k}
such that
the
\lambda_{1,k}
-label of
T_{k}
is
\boxtimes
and such that the
eigenvalue set of the chain that connects
T_{1}
to
T_{k}
excludes

\lambda_{1,k}
. Hence, the
\lambda_{1,k}
-labels of
T_{1}
and
T_{k}
are
the same. Hence the spectral diagrams of
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
differ by
the flip
\boxcirc\to\boxtimes
at
\lambda_{1,k}
. The case of type
2,3,4 RDTs in the G class now follows by gauge-covariance
Proposition
7.2
.
∎

Lemma 7.23
.

The flips admitted by classical A operators is given by (
152
).

Proof.

Suppose that
T_{0}=T(a,b),\;a\in\mathbb{N},\;b\notin\mathbb{Z}
is a class A
operator. The argument for type 1 and type 4 RDTs is the same as in
the proof of Lemma
7.22
. By Proposition

6.2
, the spectral diagram of
T_{0}
has
\boxasterisk

labels at eigenvalues

{\check{\sigma}}_{23}=\{\lambda_{j}:=\lambda_{3}(j;a,b):j=0,\ldots,a-1\}
. Suppose
then that
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{3,j}}}\xrightharpoonup{3,j}T_{1},\;j\in\{0,\ldots,a-1\}
is a type 3
RDT. By Proposition
7.7
and by permutability, the RDT
can be extended by a chain that connects
T_{1}
to the classical

T_{a}=T(0,a+b)
such that the
\lambda_{j}
label of
T_{a}
is
\boxasterisk

and such that the eigenvalue set of the chain excludes

\lambda_{j}
. Hence, the
\lambda_{j}
labels of
T_{1}
and
T_{a}
are the
same. Hence, the spectral diagrams of
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
differ by a
flip
\boxcirc\to\boxasterisk
. The subcase of a type 2 RDT follows by
gauge-covariance.
∎

Lemma 7.24
.

The flips admitted by classical B operators is given by (
153
).

Proof.

Suppose that
T_{0}=T(a,b),\;a,b\notin\mathbb{Z},a-b\in\mathbb{Z}
is a type B
classical operator. The argument for type 1 and type 2 RDTs is the
same as in Lemma
7.22
. Without loss of generality,
assume that
a-b\geq 2
. By Proposition
6.3
, the
spectral diagram of
T_{0}
has
\boxplus
labels at eigenvalues

\lambda_{3,j},\;j\in{\check{I}}_{3-}
and
\boxdiv
labels at

\lambda_{3,j},\;j\in{\check{I}}_{3+}
with

{\check{I}}_{3-}=\{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:2j<a-b\}
and

{\check{I}}_{3+}=\{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:j\geq a-b\}
. Consider the type 3 RDT

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{3,j}}}\xrightharpoonup{3,j}T_{1},\;j\in{\check{I}}_{3-}
. By Proposition

7.9
and by permutability, the RDT can be extended by a
type 3 chain that connects
T_{1}
to the classical

T_{k}=T(a-k,b+k),\;k=\lfloor(a-b)/2\rfloor|
such that the

\lambda_{3,j}
label of
T_{k}
is
\boxdiv
and such that the eigenvalue
set of the chain excludes
\lambda_{3,j}
. Hence, the
\lambda_{3,j}

labels of
T_{1}
and
T_{k}
are the same. Hence, the spectral diagrams
of
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
differ by a flip
\boxplus\to\boxdiv
.

Next consider the type 3 RDT
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{3,m}}}\xrightharpoonup{3,m}T_{1},\;m\in{\check{I}}_{3+}
.
By Proposition
7.8
, the above defined
T_{k}
can be
connected by a type 3 chain to the classical

T_{k+m}=T(a-k-m,b+k+m)
whose spectral diagram has
\boxminus

labels at the eigenvalues
\lambda_{3,j},\;j=0,\ldots,k+m
. Using
permutability, one can obtain a connection from
T_{1}
to
T_{k+m}

using a chain whose eigenvalue set excludes
\lambda_{3,m}
. Hence,
the
\lambda_{3,m}
-label of
T_{1}
matches the
\lambda_{3,m}
label of

T_{k+m}
. Hence, the spectral diagrams of
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
differ
by the flip
\boxdiv\to\boxminus
.

Finally, consider the type 4 RDT
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{4,m}}}\xrightharpoonup{4,m}T_{1},\;m\in{\check{I}}_{4}
. By assumption,
{\check{I}}_{4-}=\emptyset
, so we can reuse
the above argument to conclude that the spectral diagrams of
T_{0}

and
T_{1}
differ by the flip
\boxdiv\to\boxplus
.
∎

Lemma 7.25
.

The flips admitted by classical C and CB operators is given by
(
154
) and (
155
), respectively.

Proof.

One applies gauge-covariance Proposition
7.2

and re-uses the arguments in the proof of Lemma
7.24
.
∎

Lemma 7.26
.

The flips admitted by classical D operators is given by
(
156
).

Proof.

Let
T_{0}=T(a,b),\;a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be a classical type D operator.
Proposition
6.6
serves to prove that the spectral
diagram of
T_{0}
operator consists of the following labels:

\boxcirc,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},\boxplus,\boxminus
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
a\geq b
, in which case,
there are no
\boxminus
labels.
Thus, there are 6 possible RDTs to
consider. The claim is that the corresponding flips are as follows:

1:\;\boxcirc\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},\quad 2:\;\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\boxcirc,\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}},\quad 3:\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\boxminus,\boxplus\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}},\quad 4:\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\boxplus.

The argument for the type 1,3,4 RDTs is more-or-less the same as in
the proof to Lemma
7.24
. The argument for the type 2,
non-para-Jacobi RDT utilizes similar reasoning, but is based on
Proposition
7.10
. Finally, the para-Jacobi case is the
subject of the following Lemma
7.27
.
∎

Lemma 7.27
.

A para-Jacobi RDT corresponds to the label transformation

\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\to\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality,
a\geq b>0
. Fix a

k\in\{0,1,\ldots,b-1\}
and let
E_{k},\phi_{2}(x,t;k),\;
be as
defined to the proof of Lemma
6.7
. The para-Jacobi case
corresponds to using the generic

\phi_{2}(x,t;k)\in E_{k},\;t\notin\{0,1\}
as the factorization
eigenfunction. Set

\tau(x,t;k)=(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}\phi_{2}(x,t;k),

fix a
t\neq 0,1
and consider the para-Jacobi RDT

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{2,l}}}\xrightharpoonup{2,l}T_{1},\;l\in I_{2+}
where
l=a+b-k-1
and

T_{1}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;a-1,b-1)-a-b
. By Proposition
7.1
,

\tau(x,t;k)^{-1}
is a type 1 eigenfunction of
T_{1}
with index

-k-1
at the eigenvalue

\lambda_{2}(l;a,b)=\lambda_{1}(-k-1;a-1,b-1)-a-b=(k+1)(k-a-b).

It
remains to show that this eigenvalue is simple.

This proof requires an argument based on indefinite norm
anti-derivatives and Proposition
4.25
. Consider the
RDT
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{3,j}}}\xrightharpoonup{3,j}\tilde{T}_{1}
, where
j=a-k-1\in{\check{I}}_{3+}
. In the
proof to Lemma
7.26
we showed that the corresponding
flip is
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\to\boxminus
. Hence,
\lambda_{3,j}
is a simple
eigenvalue of
\tilde{T}_{1}
. It follows by Proposition

4.25
that

\int\phi_{3,j}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}dx=\int P_{j}(x;-a,b)^{2}(1-x)^{-a}(1+x)^{b}dx,\quad j=a-b,\ldots,a-1

is
not quasi-rational. Hence, the above integrand has a non-zero
residue at
x=1
. Since
\phi_{4,b-k-1}(x)
is regular at
x=1
,
the indefinite norm

\int\phi_{2}(x,t;k)^{2}(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}dx

is also not
quasi-rational. Therefore,
\lambda_{2}(l;a,b)
is a simple eigenvalue
of
T_{1}
.
∎

Definition 7.28
.

We say that an exceptional Jacobi operator is an
n
-step GABCD
operator if it can be Darboux-connected to a classical operator of
that class by an
n
-step RDT chain.

Note: at this point we are not yet asserting that an
n
-step GABCD
operator
belongs
that class. That will be demonstrated in
Lemma
7.34
.

Lemma 7.29
.

The flip alphabets enjoyed by 1-step GABCD operators are as given in
(
151
) - (
156
).

Proof.

Let
T_{0}
be a classical GABCD operator and

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{2}
a 2-step Jacobi RDT. Suppose
that
\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}
. Then, the
\lambda_{2}
-labels of
T_{0}
and

T_{1}
are the same. Apply permutability and consider the chain

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}\tilde{T}_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{2}
. The
\lambda_{2}
-labels of

\tilde{T}_{1}
and
T_{2}
are the same. Hence, the label transformation
engendered by
T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{2}
matches the label transformation
engendered by
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}\tilde{T}_{1}
. By Lemmas
7.22
-

7.26
, the latter flip conforms with the possibilities in
Proposition
7.18
.

Suppose then, that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
. If
\lambda_{1}
is a non-degenerate
eigenvalue of
T_{1}
, then the second step is just the inverse of the
first step,
T_{2}=T_{0}
and the label transformation engendered by

T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{2}
is the inverse of the label transformation
engendered by
T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{0}
. The flip alphabets in
Proposition
7.18
are closed under inverses, so the
conclusion follows.

It remains to consider the possibility that the 2-step chain is a
CDT. By Propositions
7.7
-
7.10
,
T_{0}
is
Darboux connected to a classical operator
\tilde{T}_{n}
of the same
degeneracy class by a chain

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}\tilde{T}_{2}\cdots\tilde{T}_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}\tilde{T}_{n}

where

\lambda_{1}\notin\{\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}
. By permutability, we can
extend this chain by
\tilde{T}_{n}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}\tilde{T}_{n+1}
and use
permutability again to obtain the chain

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{2}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{3}\cdots T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n-1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n-1}}T_{n}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n+1},

where

\tilde{T}_{n+1}=T_{n+1}
. By Proposition
7.13
, the RDTs

\tilde{T}_{n}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}\tilde{T}_{n+1}
and
T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{2}
engender
the same label transformation. Since
\tilde{T}_{n}
is classical, that
transformation belongs to one of the flip alphabets enumerated in
Proposition
7.18
.
∎

Lemma 7.30
.

Let
T_{0}
be a classical Jacobi operator and

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}T_{2}
a CDT. The corresponding label
transformations must be one of the following, or the inverse of one
of the following:

(A)

\boxcirc\mapsto\boxasterisk\mapsto\boxminus

(B,CB)

\boxplus\mapsto\boxdiv\mapsto\boxminus

(C,CB)

\boxcirc\mapsto\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\mapsto\boxtimes

(D)

\boxcirc\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}},\;\boxcirc\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\boxplus,\;\boxcirc\mapsto\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\mapsto\boxminus
.

Proof.

This follows by Lemma
7.29
and by inspection of the label
transformations displayed in Proposition
7.18
.
∎

Definition 7.31
.

We say that a factorization chain

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\cdots T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n}
is

reduced
if (i)
\lambda_{k}\neq\lambda_{k+j+1}
for all
k,j\in\mathbb{N}

where defined, and (ii) in those cases where
\lambda_{k}=\lambda_{k+1}
,
the subchain
T_{k-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{k}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{k}}T_{k}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{k+1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{k+1}}T_{k+1}
is a
CDT.

Lemma 7.32
.

If
T
is Darboux-connected to a classical operator, it can be
connected by a reduced factorization chain.

Proof.

Consider a chain

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\cdots T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n}
of
exceptional Jacobi operators with
T_{0}
classical. Suppose that one
of the above eigenvalues is repeated. By permutability, WLOG assume
that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
. If the initial 2-step subchain isn’t a CDT
then,
T_{2}=T_{0}
, and the chain can be shortened. Suppose then that
the initial 2-step subchain is a CDT, but that
\lambda_{1}
occurs at
least 3 times in the eigenvalue set. Again by permutability, WLOG

\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}
. By inspection of the list given in Lemma

7.30
,
\lambda_{2}
is a simple eigenvalue of
T_{2}
, and
hence
T_{3}=T_{1}
. Again, the chain can be shortened.
∎

Lemma 7.33
.

Let
n\geq 2
. The
n
-step GABCD operators enjoy the flip alphabets
displayed in (
151
) - (
156
).

Proof.

Consider a chain

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{2}\cdots T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n}
where
T_{0}
is a classical Jacobi operator. By Lemma

7.32
, we may assume that the chain is reduced. Hence,
either
\lambda_{n}\notin\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n-1}\}
, or

\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{j}
for a unique
j=1,\ldots,n-1
. In the first case,
apply permutability to construct the chain

(157)

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}\tilde{T}_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}\tilde{T}_{2}\cdots\tilde{T}_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n-1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n-1}}T_{n}.

By Proposition
7.13
, the
\lambda_{n}
-labels of
\tilde{T}_{1}
and

T_{n}
are the same. For the same reason, the
\lambda_{n}
-labels of

T_{0}
and
T_{n-1}
are the same. Therefore
T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}\tilde{T}_{1}

engenders the same label transformation as

T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n}
. The desired conclusion now follows by
Lemmas
7.22
-
7.27
.

Next, suppose that
\lambda_{n}
occurs twice in the eigenvalue set.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{n}
. Again,
apply permutability to construct the chain (
157
). By the
same argument as above,
\tilde{T}_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}\tilde{T}_{2}
engenders the
same label transformation as
T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n}
. The desired
conclusion now follows by Lemma
7.29
.
∎

Lemma 7.34
.

The GABCD degeneracy classes are closed with respect to Jacobi RDTs.

Proof.

Suppose that
T
is an
n
-step GABCD operator for some
n\geq 1
. We
claim that, necessarily,
T
belongs to the degeneracy class in
question. We also claim the converse, namely that every exceptional
GABCD operator can be Darboux connected to a classical operator
belonging to that class. Both assertions are evident for type
G,B,C,CB operators by inspection of (
140
) and by
Definition
2.25
. The conditions that define these
degeneracy classes are invariant with respect to the
\pm 1
shifts
of the
\alpha,\beta
parameters engendered by Jacobi RDTs.

Let
T_{0}=T(a,b),\;a\in\mathbb{N}_{0},b\notin\mathbb{Z}
be a classical type A
operator, and let

(158)

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}T_{2}\cdots T_{n-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{n}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{n}}T_{n}

be an
n
-step chain. By Lemma
7.32
, without loss of
generality that chain is reduced. By Proposition
6.2
,
the spectral diagram of
T_{0}
has exactly
a
labels of type

\boxasterisk
. Hence, by Lemma
7.33
the the total number of
type 2
\boxasterisk\mapsto\boxcirc
and type 3

\boxasterisk\mapsto\boxminus
flips engendered by this chain cannot
exceed
a
. Since these are the flips that shift
\alpha
by
-1
,

T_{n}
must have the form
T(\tau,\alpha,\beta)+\epsilon
where

0\leq\alpha\leq a
is a non-negative integer. Therefore,
T_{n}
is a
class A exceptional operator.

Conversely, consider a type A exceptional operator

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\;\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
. By Theorem

2.34
, there exists an
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\hat{T}:=T(\tau,\alpha,\beta)+\epsilon
is Darboux connected to a classical
operator
T_{0}=T(a,b)
. Since the
\alpha,\beta
shifts are
\pm 1
for
all RDTS, we must have
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\notin\mathbb{Z}
. If
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
we are done. Suppose then that
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
is a negative integer.
Hence,
T(-a,b)
is a classical type A operator, which by
gauge-covariance is Darboux connected to

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,\beta)+\epsilon
. However, we just proved that the
latter is a type A operator, with
0\leq-\alpha\leq-a
. Hence

\alpha=0
, which means that
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)+\epsilon
can be Darboux
connected to the type A classical operator
T(-a,b)
.

Analogous reasoning serves to establish the desired conclusion for
the D class.
∎

8.
Remaining Proofs

Having assembled the necessary definitions and key demonstrations we
now turn to the proofs of the propositions and theorems stated in the
introductory sections of this paper.

8.1.
Proof of Theorem

2.3

We begin by proving Theorem

2.3
. As was mentioned, a more limited version of this
result was proved in
[
24
]
[Theorem 5.3]. Theorem

2.3
is a stronger statement and our first task is to
show how this stronger statement follows from the results in
[
24
]

The demonstration is based on an operator form that generalizes both
the natural gauge form
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}
(
4
) and the rational gauge
form
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}
(
17
). For
\eta(x),\mu(x)\in\mathcal{P}
and

\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, define

(159)

{T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(\eta,\mu;\alpha,\beta)={T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\eta;\alpha,\beta)+2(x^{2}-1)u^{\prime}+2xu,\quad u=\mu^{\prime}/\mu.

The following results were proved in
[
24
]
[Lemmas 5.26 and
5.27].

Lemma 8.1
.

Every exceptional Jacobi operator is gauge equivalent to an
exceptional Jacobi operator (in the sense of Definition

2.2
) having the form
{T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(\eta,\mu;\alpha,\beta)
, where

\eta(x),\mu(x)\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
f(x)\in\mathcal{P}
. Then,

(160)

\mathcal{M}(f)\circ{T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(\eta,\mu;\alpha,\beta)\circ\mathcal{M}(f^{-1})={T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(f\tau,f^{-1}\mu;a,b).

The natural gauge and rational gauge forms shown in (
4
)
and (
17
) are extreme examples of the
general gauge (gg)

form (
159
), with

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)={T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(1,\tau^{-1};\alpha,\beta),\quad{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)={T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(\tau,1;\alpha,\beta).

However, as the following
identity demonstrates, natural gauge (
4
) is not
uniquely determined when the parameters
\alpha,\beta
are integral.

Proposition 8.3
.

We have

(161)

\displaystyle{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau(1-x)^{\alpha+1};\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;-\alpha-2,\beta)+\beta(\alpha+1),

(162)

\displaystyle{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau(1+x)^{\beta+1};\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,-\beta-2)+\alpha(\beta+1)

Therefore, the natural form (
4
) does not automatically imply
that
\tau(\pm 1)\neq 0
. However, as we now show, the condition that

\tau(\pm 1)\neq 0
does not entail a loss of generality.

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

By Lemma
8.1
every exceptional Jacobi operator is
gauge equivalent to some
{T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(\eta,\mu,\alpha,\beta)
where

\eta(\pm 1)\neq 0
and
\mu(\pm 1)\neq 0
. Hence, by
(
160
),

(163)

\mathcal{M}(\mu)\circ{T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(\eta,\mu;\alpha,\beta)\circ\mathcal{M}(\mu^{-1})={T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\mu\eta;\alpha,\beta)

By inspection,
\tau(x)=\eta(x)\mu(x)
does not vanish at

x=\pm 1
. Moreover, by (
163
), if
P(x)
is an
eigenpolynomial of
{T_{\mathrm{gg}}}(\eta,\mu,\alpha,\beta)
, then
\mu(x)P(x)
is
an eigenpolynomial of
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\mu\eta;\alpha,\beta)
. Therefore, the
latter is itself an exceptional operator.
∎

8.2.
Proofs of Propositions
2.8
,
2.18
and

2.22

Next, we prove Propositions
2.18
and

2.22
. The former classifies the qr-eigenfunctions of an
exceptional Jacobi operator into four classes according to the asymptotic
type. The latter, classifies the possible qr-eigenvalues. The proofs
are based on the following lemmas, which establish the fundamental
characterization of qr-eigenvalues and eigenfunctions. Proposition

2.8
then follows as a straightforward corollary.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\tau\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
be an exceptional operator
and
\phi(x)
a qr-eigenfunction. Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3},\mu_{4}
be
as per Table
1
. Then, necessarily

\phi(x)=\mu_{\imath}(x)\hat{\tau}(x)/\tau(x)
for some

\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
\hat{\tau}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
.

Proof.

Set
u=\tau^{\prime}/\tau,w=\phi^{\prime}/\phi
. By (
5
),
(
7
), (
17
), (
25
) and
(
147
) ,

(164)

(x^{2}-1)\left(w^{\prime}+w^{2}\right)+(\alpha-\beta+(2+\alpha+\beta)x)w+2(x^{2}-1)u^{\prime}+2xu=\lambda.

By construction,

\deg\big(2(x^{2}-1)u^{\prime}+2xu\big)\leq 0.

Hence,
\deg w<0
. If not, then by inspection we would have

\deg\left((x^{2}-1)\left(w^{\prime}+w^{2}\right)+(\alpha-\beta+(2+\alpha+\beta)x)w\right)=2+2\deg w>0,

which would contradict (
164
).
Also by construction, the poles of
2(x^{2}-1)u^{\prime}+2xu
have order 2 at
most. This implies that
w(x)
has at most simple poles. If
w(x)

were to have a non-simple pole, then by inspection,

(x^{2}-1)\left(w^{\prime}+w^{2}\right)+(\alpha-\beta+(2+\alpha+\beta)x)w
would have a pole
of order 3 or higher. This would, once again, contradict
(
164
).

Thus,
\phi(x)=(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}\hat{\tau}(x)/\tau(x)
, where
a,b
are
the residues of
w(x)
at
x=+1,-1
, respectively, and where
\hat{\tau}(x)

is a qr-function such that
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}(x)/\hat{\tau}(x)
is regular at
x=\pm 1
.
Recall that, by assumption,
u(x)
has no poles at
x=\pm 1
.
Hence, by a straight-forward calculation,

\underset{x=1}{\operatorname{Res}}\;(x^{2}-1)\left(w^{\prime}+w^{2}\right)+(\alpha-\beta+(2+\alpha+\beta)x)w=-2a(a+\alpha)=0.

Hence, either
a=0
or

a=-\alpha
. Similarly, either
b=0
or
b=-\beta
. Therefore
(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}=\mu_{\imath}(x)
for some
\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.

By (
16
),
\xi(x):=(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}\hat{\tau}(x)
is a qr-
eigenfunction of
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
. In
[
24
]
(see Remark
5.22 and Lemma 5.16 of that reference) it was shown that such

\xi(x)
is regular at every
x\neq\pm 1
. Hence,
\hat{\tau}(x)
is
everywhere regular. Since
\deg w<0
we must have

\deg\hat{\tau}^{\prime}/\hat{\tau}<0
also. Therefore,
\hat{\tau}(x)
is a polynomial
that does not vanish at
x=\pm 1
.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})
be a quasi-polynomial eigenfunction
of degree
i
of an exceptional
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
. Then,
necessarily the corresponding eigenvalue has the form

\lambda=i(i+\alpha+\beta+1)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)},\;k\in\mathbb{Z}
be the subspace of rational functions of
degree less or equal than
k
. Set
w=\pi_{i}^{\prime}/\pi_{i},\;u=\tau^{\prime}/\tau
and

N=\deg\tau
. Observe that

\displaystyle((x^{2}-1)u^{\prime}+xu)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{x^{2}\tau^{\prime\prime}}{\tau}-\left(\frac{x\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}\right)^{2}+\frac{x\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}\mod\mathcal{Q}^{(-1)}

\displaystyle\equiv N(N-1)-N^{2}+N\equiv 0\mod\mathcal{Q}^{(-1)}

As above, (
164
) holds by assumption. Working mod

\mathcal{Q}^{(-1)}
, a direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle(x^{2}-1)\left(w^{\prime}+w^{2}+\eta(x;\alpha,\beta)\,w\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{x^{2}\pi_{i}^{\prime\prime}}{\pi_{i}}+\left(\alpha+\beta+2\right)\frac{x\pi_{i}^{\prime}}{\pi_{i}}

\displaystyle\equiv i(i-1)+(\alpha+\beta+2)i=\lambda

The desired conclusion follows immediately.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.18
.

The first assertion is a consequence of Lemma
8.4
.
The case
\imath=1
of (
28
) is covered by Lemma

8.5
. We turn to the case of
\imath=2
. Consider a
type 2 eigenfunction

\phi_{2,k}(x)=\mu_{2}(x)\pi_{2,k}(x),\;k\in I_{2}
. By Proposition

2.19
,
\pi_{2,k}
is an eigenfunction of

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,-\beta)
with

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,-\beta)\pi_{2,k}-(\alpha+\beta)\pi_{2,k}=\lambda\pi_{2,k}.

Hence, by Lemma
8.5
,

\lambda=(-k-1)(-k-1-\alpha-\beta+1)-\alpha-\beta=k(k+\alpha+\beta+1)=d(d+1+\alpha+\beta),

where

d=\deg\phi=-k-1-\alpha-\beta.

The cases

\imath=3,4
are argued similarly.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.22
.

Consider a type 2 eigenfunction,

\phi_{2,k}=\mu_{2}\pi_{2,k},\;k\in I_{2}
. By definition
(
31
) and by (
30
),
\pi_{2,k}(x)
is a
rational eigenfunction of
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;-\alpha,-\beta)
with

\deg\pi_{2,k}=k
. Recall that

\deg\phi_{2,k}=k-\alpha-\beta
. Hence, the corresponding eigenvalue
is

\lambda=(k-\alpha-\beta)(k+1)=\lambda_{2}(k;\alpha,\beta).

Similarly, if
\phi_{3,j},\;j\in I_{3}
is a type 3 eigenfunction,
then by the convention of (
31
) and (
32
),

\phi_{3,j}=\mu_{3}\pi_{3,j}
where the latter is a rational function
of degree
j
. Hence,
d=\deg\phi_{3,j}=j-\alpha
. It follows that

d(d+\alpha+\beta+1)=(j-\alpha)(j+\beta+1)=\lambda_{3}(j;\alpha,\beta).

Similarly, if
\phi_{4,j}=\mu_{4}(x)\pi_{j},\;j\in I_{4}
is a
type 4 eigenfunction, then
d=\deg\phi_{4,j}=j-\beta
. Hence, in
this case also,

d(d+\alpha+\beta+1)=(j-\beta)(j+\alpha+1)=\lambda_{4}(j;\alpha,\beta).

∎

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

The first assertion follows by the more general Proposition

2.18
. We turn to the second
assertion. Suppose that
\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
. Let
P(x)
be an
eigenpolynomial of
{T_{\mathrm{ng}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
. We claim that
P(\pm 1)\neq 0
. By
(
16
),
\xi(x)=P(x)/\tau(x)
is a rational
eigenfunction of
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
. By Proposition
2.18
,

\xi(x)=\mu_{\imath}(x)\tilde{P}(x)/\tau(x)
for some

\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
\tilde{P}(x)\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
. Since
\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
we must have

\imath=1
. Since
\mu_{1}(x)=1
, we conclude that
P=\tilde{P}
.
Hence,
I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})\supseteq I({T_{\mathrm{ng}}})-N
. Again, by Proposition

2.18
, an quasi-polynomial eigenfunction of
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}
must
have the form
\xi(x)=P(x)/\tau(x)
where
P(x)\in\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
. Hence,

I_{1}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}})\subseteq I({T_{\mathrm{ng}}})-N
.
∎

8.3.
Proofs of Proposition
2.23
and Theorem
2.35

Finally, we prove two results concerning the qr-spectrum and the spectral diagram of exceptional Jacobi operators.

Lemma 8.6
.

Setting
s=(i+j)/2
, we have

(165)

\lambda_{1}(z;\alpha,\beta)-\lambda_{1}(z-s;\alpha+i,\beta+j)=\lambda_{1}(s;\alpha,\beta).

Proof.

Applying the definition (
37
) and calculating, gives

(166)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(z;\alpha,\beta)-\lambda_{1}(z-s;\alpha+i,\beta+j)=z(z+\alpha+\beta+1)-(z-s)(z+s+\alpha+\beta+1)

\displaystyle=s(s+\alpha+\beta+1)=\lambda_{1}(s;\alpha,\beta)

∎

Proof of Proposition
2.23
.

The claim is true for classical Jacobi operators by Proposition

6.8
. By Theorem
2.34
, it suffices to
show that if (
39
) is true for a given exceptional
operator
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
, then it is true for the
exceptional operators related to
T
by a 1-step RDT.

We prove this for the case of a type 1 RDT; the other cases are
argued similarly. By (
166
),
the set
\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
as defined in (
38
) enjoys the following transformation
law:

(167)

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha,\beta)=\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha+i,\beta+j)+\lambda_{1}(s;\alpha,\beta),\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{Z},\;s=\frac{i+j}{2},

The spectral shifts defined in (
140
) and utilized in
(
141
) match (
167
). By (
140
)
and by Proposition
7.13
,

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta))=\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{1k};\alpha+1,\beta+1))+\lambda_{1}(1;\alpha,\beta).

Therefore,

\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}({T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{1k};\alpha+1,\beta+1))=\sigma_{\tiny\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}}(\alpha+1,\beta+1)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.35
.

The argument is essentially the same as the induction proof given
above, with Proposition
7.13
providing the inductive step.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

Consider a Jacobi RDT

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\imath,k}}}\xrightharpoonup{\imath,k}T_{1},\;\imath\in\{1,2,3,4\}
with factorization
function
\phi_{\imath,k},\;k\in I_{\imath}(T_{0})
of degree

j=\deg\phi_{\imath k}=\deg\mu_{\imath}+k
, intertwiner
A_{\imath}
as
per (
142
), and
{\hat{\alpha}}={\hat{\alpha}}_{\imath},{\hat{\beta}}={\hat{\beta}}_{\imath}
as
per (
140
). For
i\in I_{0}(T_{0})\setminus\{j\}
, set

\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}}=\frac{A_{\imath}\pi_{i}}{i-j},

where

{\hat{\imath}}=i-1,i+1,i,i
respectively, according to whether

\imath=1,2,3,4
. Then,
\hat{\pi}_{\hat{\imath}}
is a rational eigenfunction of

T_{1}
satisfying

(168)

\displaystyle\deg\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}}={\hat{\imath}},\quad\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}(\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}})=\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}(\pi_{i}),

(169)

\displaystyle(i-j)\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{{\hat{\alpha}}}(1+x)^{{\hat{\beta}}}-(i-j^{\star})\pi_{i}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}=

\displaystyle\qquad D_{x}\left[\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}}(x)\pi_{i}(x)\,(1-x)^{{\tilde{\alpha}}}(1+x)^{{\tilde{\beta}}}\right],

where

{\tilde{\alpha}}=\max\{\alpha,{\hat{\alpha}}\},\;{\tilde{\beta}}=\max\{\alpha,{\hat{\alpha}}\},\;j^{\star}=-j-1-\alpha-\beta
.

Proof.

We give the proof for the case
\imath=1
, where
j=k
. The other three
cases are proved analogously. By (
142
),

A_{\imath}\pi_{i}=\pi_{k}^{-1}\operatorname{Wr}[\pi_{k},\pi_{i}].

The first
assertions now follow
by (
114
) (
115
) of Lemma
4.22
. We
now prove (
169
). Dropping some indices and
simplifying the notation in (
142
) (
143
),
write

\displaystyle\pi(x)=\pi_{i}(x),\;\hat{\pi}(x)=\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}}(x),

\displaystyle\phi(x)=\pi_{k}(x),\;\hat{\phi}(x)=\pi_{k}(x)^{-1}(1-x)^{-\alpha-1}(1+x)^{-\beta-1},

\displaystyle A=A_{1k},\;\hat{A}=\hat{A}_{2\hat{k}},

and recall that

\displaystyle T_{0}

\displaystyle=\hat{A}A+k(k+1+\alpha+\beta),

\displaystyle A[y]

\displaystyle=\phi D_{x}\left[y\phi^{-1}\right],

\displaystyle\hat{A}[y]

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)\hat{\phi}D_{x}\left[y\hat{\phi}^{-1}\right],

\displaystyle\hat{A}[\hat{\pi}]

\displaystyle=(i(i+1+\alpha+\beta)-k(k+1+\alpha+\beta))/(i-k)\pi=(i-k^{\star})\pi.

Hence, by direct calculation,

\displaystyle D_{x}\left[\hat{\pi}(x)\pi(x)(1-x)^{\alpha+1}(1+x)^{\beta+1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=D_{x}\left[\hat{\pi}(x)\pi(x)(\hat{\phi}(x)\phi(x))^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\pi(x)\phi(x)^{-1}D_{x}\left[\hat{\pi}(x)\hat{\phi}(x)^{-1}\right]+\hat{\pi}(x)\hat{\phi}(x)^{-1}D_{x}\left[\pi(x)\phi(x)^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\left(\pi(x)\hat{\phi}(x)D_{x}\left[\hat{\pi}(x)\hat{\phi}(x)^{-1}\right]+\hat{\pi}(x)\phi(x)D_{x}\left[\pi(x)\phi(x)^{-1}\right]\right)(1-x)^{\alpha+1}(1+x)^{\beta+1}

\displaystyle\quad=-\pi(x)\hat{A}[\hat{\pi}(x)](1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}+A[\pi(x)]\hat{\pi}(x)(1-x)^{\alpha+1}(1+x)^{\beta+1}

\displaystyle\quad=-(i-k^{\star})\pi(x)^{2}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}+(i-k)\hat{\pi}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{\alpha+1}(1+x)^{\beta+1}.

∎

8.4.
Proofs of results on the generic class

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Since
a,b,a\pm b\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the eigenvalue set

\{\lambda_{1}(k;a,b):k\in\tilde{K}\}
consists of
p_{1}+p_{3}+p_{4}
distinct
eigenvalues. Hence, we can apply Propositions
4.11

and
7.1
to construct an RDT chain connecting the
classical
T(a,b)
to the exceptional

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)+p_{1}(1+a+b+p_{1})
.

The formula (
47
) for the corresponding Jacobi
quasi-polynomials is justified by Proposition
4.13
,
Lemma
4.22
, and Proposition
7.15
.
Proposition
4.13
accounts for the ratio of the
Wronskians, while Proposition
7.15
accounts for the
degree shift between
\pi_{i}
and
{\check{\pi}}_{i+p_{1}}
. The factorization
gauge at each step of the chain is determined by (
142
).
The factor
(x-1)^{p_{3}}(x+1)^{p_{4}}
in (
47
) is just a
product of the factorization gauge functions in the chain: the type
3 Jacobi RDTs utilize a gauge factor of
b(x)=x-1
, the type 4
Jacobi RDTs utilize a gauge factor of
b(x)=x+1
, and the type 1
Jacobi RDTs utilize a gauge factor of
b(x)=1
. Lemma
4.22

accounts for the form of the normalizing factor

\prod_{k\in\tilde{K}}(i+p_{1}-k)
. The presence of this product in the
denominator ensures that
\pi_{i}
is monic.

Next, we justify the form of
\tau(x)
given in (
46
).
Having established the validity of (
47
), Proposition

2.18
and, in particular, formula (
27
)
indicate that the
\tau(x)
in question is the polynomial part of
the Wronskian
\operatorname{Wr}[\phi(K_{1},K_{3},K_{4};a,b)]
. Write

\operatorname{Wr}[\phi(K_{1},K_{3},K_{4};a,b)]=(1-x)^{A}(1+x)^{B}\tau(x),

where

\tau(x)
is a polynomial with no zeros at
x=\pm 1
. The forms of

A
and
B
shown in (
46
) are a consequence of Lemma

4.20
. The degree formula (
48
) is a
consequence of Lemma
4.22
and of the definition
(
46
). The form of
\alpha,\beta
in (
42
) is
justified by repeated application of Proposition
7.1

and the table (
140
). Basically, the RDT chain that
connects
T(a,b)
and
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
contains
p_{1}
type 1
RDTs,
p_{3}
type 3 RDTS and
p_{4}
type 4 RDTS. Thus,

(\alpha,\beta)-(a,b)=p_{1}(+1,+1)+p_{3}(-1,1)+p_{4}(1,-1).

The form
of the index sets (
41
) is a consequence of Proposition

7.15
and of the type G flip alphabet
(
151
) in Proposition
7.18
. Since the RDT
chain contains
p_{1}
type 1 steps and since all the other steps are
type 3 and type 4, the relationship between
I_{12}
, the combined
index set of
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
and
{\check{I}}_{12}
, the combined
index set of
T(a,b)
is simply
I_{12}={\check{I}}_{12}-p_{1}
. We have
equality because the type G qr-eigenvalues are all simple. This is
a consequence of Proposition
7.18
. A further consequence
of Proposition
7.18
is that the labels at the eigenvalues

\lambda_{1}(k;a,b),\;k\in K_{1}
undergo the flips

\boxcirc\mapsto\boxtimes
. Hence, when we break up
I_{12}
into

I_{1}
and
-I_{2}-1
we have to (i) remove the indices
k,\;k\in K_{1}

from
{\check{I}}_{1}
, (ii) add the indices
-k,\;k\in K_{1}
to
{\check{I}}_{2}
and
then shift the former by
-p_{1}
and the latter by
+p_{1}
. Similar
reasoning serves to justify the forms of
I_{3}
and
I_{4}
.

By (
110
) of Lemma
4.17
, we have

\int\pi_{i}(x)\pi_{j}(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1},\quad i\neq j\in I_{1}(T_{0}).

Let

\kappa(z;K,a,b)
be as in (
50
). Repeated application
of Lemma
8.7
shows that the anti-derivative

\int\pi_{i}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}-\kappa(i;K,a,b){\check{\pi}}_{i+p_{1}}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b},\quad i\in I_{1}(T)

is
quasi-rational. By Proposition
6.13
and by
(
130
), the anti-derivatives

\int\left({\check{\pi}}_{i+p_{1}}(x)^{2}-\frac{\nu(i+p_{1};a,b)}{\nu(0;a,b)}\right)(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b},\quad\int\frac{(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}}{\nu(0;\alpha,\beta)}-\frac{(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}}{\nu(0;a,b)}

are also quasi-rational. Hence,

\int\left(\pi_{i}(x)^{2}-\kappa(i;K,a,b)\frac{\nu(i;a,b)}{\nu(0;\alpha,\beta)}\right)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}

is quasi-rational. Since
\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the
above anti-derivative must be an element of
\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1}
by
Lemma
6.9
. This justifies the form of the norm formula
given in (
49
) and (
50
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

By Theorem
2.35
, Proposition
6.1
and
Proposition
7.18
, the spectral diagram of a type G
exceptional operator has the representation described in Remark

3.4
. The same remark indicates how to uniquely recover the
parameters
K_{1},K_{3},a,b
from the spectral diagram. The fact that

\tau(x)
must have the form shown in (
46
) is justified
above, in the proof of Proposition
3.2
.
∎

8.5.
Proofs of results on the semi-degenerated classes

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}
be a class A
exceptional operator with
\pi_{i}(x)=\pi_{1,i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
the
corresponding quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions, relative to some
choice of normalization. If
i\neq j
, then the incomplete
inner-products

(170)

\rho_{ij}(x):=\int\pi_{i}(x)\pi_{j}(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta},\quad i,j\in I_{1}(T),

define quasi-rational functions in
\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of (
110
) of Lemma

4.17
.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
T,\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
, be as above and

\rho_{ij}(x),\;i,j\in I_{1}(T)
be as defined in (
170
).
Suppose that the indefinite norms
\rho_{ii}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
are
quasi-rational functions. Then, the corresponding norms are given
by
\nu_{i}=\rho_{ii}(1),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
.

Proof.

For each
i\in I_{1}(T)
, define the quasi-rational anti-derivatives

\hat{\rho}_{ii}(x):=\int\left(\pi_{i}(x)^{2}-\frac{\rho_{ii}(1)}{\nu(\alpha,\beta)}\right)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}dx.

Such anti-derivatives are uniquely defined because
\rho_{ii}(x)
is
assumed to be quasi-rational, and because
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. By
Proposition
6.15
and by construction
\hat{\rho}_{ii}(1)=0
.
Hence, by Lemma
6.9
,
\hat{\rho}_{ii}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1}
.
Therefore, following the criterion shown in (
21
),

\nu_{i}=\rho_{ii}(1)
, as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
T,\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T),\;\rho_{ij}(x),\;i,j\in I_{1}(T)
be
as in Lemma
8.8
, and suppose that the indefinite norms

\rho_{ii}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
are quasi-rational functions such that

\rho_{ii}(1)\neq 0
. Fix an
\ell\in I_{1}(T)
and set

(171)

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}(x)

\displaystyle=\tau(x)\pi_{\ell}(x),

(172)

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}(x)

\displaystyle=\tau(x)\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)(1+x)^{-\beta-1},

(173)

\displaystyle\tilde{T}

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tilde{\tau},\alpha+1,\beta+1)+\alpha+\beta+2,

(174)

\displaystyle\hat{T}

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau},\alpha,\beta+2)+\alpha+\beta+2.

Then,
\tilde{\tau}(x),\hat{\tau}(x)
are polynomials and

T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\tilde{T}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\hat{T},\;\lambda=\lambda_{1}(\ell;\alpha,\beta)
is a
type A CDT, as described in Lemma
7.30
. Moreover,

(175)

\deg\hat{\tau}=\deg\tau+2\ell+\alpha.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{T}
be defined as above. By Proposition
7.1
,

T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\tilde{T},\;\lambda=\lambda_{1}(\ell;\alpha,\beta)
is a type 1 RDT. By
inspection,
\deg\tilde{\tau}=\deg\tau+\ell
. Hence by
(
143
), the seed eigenfunction for the inverse
transformation
\tilde{T}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}T
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}_{2}(x)=\frac{\tau(x)}{\tilde{\tau}(x)}(1-x)^{-\alpha-1}(1+x)^{-\beta-1},

\displaystyle\lambda=\lambda_{2}(-\ell;\alpha+1,\beta+1)+\alpha+\beta+2

By assumption,
\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{0,\beta+1}
is
quasi-rational. Hence, By Proposition
4.25
and since

\rho_{\ell\ell}(1)\neq 0
, the quasi-rational

\tilde{\phi}_{3}:=\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)\tilde{\phi}_{2}(x)
is a type 3
eigenfunction of
\tilde{T}
. By (
170
),

\deg\rho_{\ell\ell}=2\ell+\alpha+\beta+1.

Hence, (
175
) holds, and

\deg\phi_{3}=\ell-1,\quad\lambda=\lambda_{3}(\ell-1+\alpha;\alpha+1,\beta+1)+\alpha+\beta+2.

It also follows that

\tau(x)/\tilde{\tau}(x)\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)(1+x)^{-\beta-1}
is a
quasi-polynomial. Hence, by Proposition
2.18
,

\hat{\tau}
is the polynomial numerator of that rational function. Let

\hat{T}
be as defined above. By Proposition
7.1
,

\tilde{T}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\hat{T}
is a type 3 RDT, as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
T,\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T),\;\rho_{ij}(x),\;i,j\in I_{1}(T)
be
as in Lemma
8.8
, and suppose that the indefinite norms

\rho_{ii}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
are quasi-rational functions such that

\rho_{ii}(1)\neq 0
. Fix an
\ell\in I_{1}(T)
and let

\tilde{\tau}(x),\hat{\tau}(x),\hat{T}
be as in Lemma
8.10
. Set

(176)

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=(1+x)^{-1}\left(\pi_{i}(x)-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}(x)}{\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)}\pi_{\ell}(x)\right),\quad i\in I_{1}(T)\setminus\{\ell\},

(177)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{ij}(x)

\displaystyle=\rho_{ij}-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}(x)\rho_{j\ell}(x)}{\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)},\quad i,j\in I_{1}(T)\setminus\{\ell\}.

The degrees and leading coefficients of these
eigenfunctions are given by

(178)

\deg\hat{\Pi}_{i}=i-1,\qquad\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}(\hat{\Pi}_{i})=\left(\frac{i-\ell}{i-\ell^{\star}}\right)\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}(\pi_{i}),\quad i\in I_{1}(T)\setminus\{\ell\},

where
\ell^{\star}=-\ell-1-\alpha-\beta
.
The
\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{ij}(x)
are the corresponding incomplete inner products;
i.e.,

(179)

\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{ij}(x)=\int\hat{\Pi}_{i}(x)\hat{\Pi}_{j}(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta+2},\quad i,j\in I_{1}(T)\setminus\{\ell\}.

These quasi-rational functions satisfy

\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{ij}(1)=\rho_{ij}(1)
. Moreover,

I_{1}(\hat{T})=(I_{1}(T)\setminus\{\ell\})-1
and

(180)

\hat{T}\hat{\Pi}_{i+1}=\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta+2)\hat{\Pi}_{i+1},\quad i\in I_{1}(\hat{T}).

Proof.

Let
T,\tilde{T},\hat{T},\tau(x),\tilde{\tau}(x),\hat{\tau}(x),\lambda
be as in the
preceding lemma. Set

\displaystyle w_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\tau}}-\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau},

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\tau}}+\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}-\frac{\alpha+1}{x-1}-\frac{\beta+1}{x+1},

\displaystyle w_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{\tau}^{\prime}}{\hat{\tau}}-\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\tau}}-\frac{\alpha+1}{x-1}

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hat{\tau}^{\prime}}{\hat{\tau}}+\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\tau}}-\frac{\beta+2}{x+1},

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=D_{x}-w_{1},

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)(D_{x}-\hat{w}_{1}),

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=(x-1)(D_{x}-w_{2}),

\displaystyle B_{2}

\displaystyle=(x+1)(D_{x}-\hat{w}_{2}).

Then, by Proposition
7.1
,

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=B_{1}A_{1}+\lambda,

\displaystyle\tilde{T}

\displaystyle=A_{1}B_{1}+\lambda,

\displaystyle\tilde{T}

\displaystyle=B_{2}A_{2}+\lambda,

\displaystyle\hat{T}

\displaystyle=A_{2}B_{2}+\lambda.

By (
172
),

\frac{\hat{\tau}^{\prime}}{\hat{\tau}}=\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}-\frac{\beta+1}{x+1}+\frac{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{-1}\left(B_{1}-\frac{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}\right),

\displaystyle A_{2}A_{1}

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{-1}\left(B_{1}A_{1}-\frac{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}A_{1}\right)=(x+1)^{-1}\left(T-\lambda-\frac{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}A_{1}\right)

\displaystyle A_{2}A_{1}\pi_{i}

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{-1}\left((\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)-\lambda_{1}(\ell;\alpha,\beta))\pi_{i}-\frac{\pi_{\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}\operatorname{Wr}[\pi_{\ell},\pi_{i}](1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}\right)\quad\text{by
~\eqref{eq:rhodefA}},

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{-1}(i-\ell)(i+\ell+\alpha+\beta+1)\left(\pi_{i}-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}\pi_{\ell}\right)\quad\text{by
~\eqref{eq:phi12W}}

\displaystyle=(i-\ell)(i+\ell+\alpha+\beta+1)\hat{\Pi}_{i}.

Eigenvalue relation (
180
) follows by
(
144
). Relations (
178
) follow by Lemma

8.7
. Relation (
179
) follows by the
following calculation:

\displaystyle(1-x)^{-\alpha}(1+x)^{-\beta}\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{ij}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(1-x)^{-\alpha}(1+x)^{-\beta}\left(\rho_{ij}^{\prime}-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}^{\prime}\rho_{j\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}^{\prime}\rho_{j\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}+\frac{\rho_{i\ell}\rho_{j\ell}\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}\pi_{j}-\frac{\pi_{i}\pi_{\ell}\rho_{j\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}-\frac{\pi_{j}\pi_{\ell}\rho_{i\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}+\frac{\pi_{\ell}\pi_{\ell}\rho_{i\ell}\rho_{j\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{i}-\frac{\pi_{\ell}\rho_{i\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}\right)\left(\pi_{j}-\frac{\pi_{\ell}\rho_{j\ell}}{\rho_{\ell\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle=(1+x)^{2}\hat{\Pi}_{i}\hat{\Pi}_{j}.

The last assertion follows because, by Lemma
8.8
, we have

\rho_{il}(1)=0
if
i\neq\ell
.
∎

Lemma 8.12
.

The
\hat{\tau}(x)=\hat{\tau}(x;L,a,b)
, as defined in (
56
), is a
polynomial with

(181)

\deg\hat{\tau}=2\sum_{\ell\in L}\ell-q(q-1-a).

The
\hat{\pi}_{i}(x)=\hat{\pi}_{i}(x;L,a,b),\;i\in\hat{I}_{1}
defined in
(
57
) are monic rational functions of degree
i
that satisfy

(182)

{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau};a,b+2q)\hat{\pi}_{i}=\lambda_{1}(i;a,b+2q)\hat{\pi}_{i},\quad i\in\hat{I}_{1}.

The incomplete inner-products

\hat{\rho}_{ij}(x)=\int\hat{\pi}_{i}(x)\hat{\pi}_{j}(x)(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b+2q},\quad i,j\in\hat{I}_{1},

define quasi-rational functions, that satisfy

(183)

\hat{\rho}_{ij}(1)=\delta_{ij}\hat{\chi}(i+q;L,a,b)^{2}\nu(i+q;a,b),\quad i,j\in\hat{I}_{1},

with
\hat{\chi}
defined as in (
58
).

Proof.

Enumerate
L
as
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{q}
and
inductively define

\displaystyle\tilde{T}_{s}

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tilde{\tau}_{s},a+1,b+2s-1)+s(a+b+s+1),\quad s=1,\ldots,q,

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{s}

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau}_{s},a,b+2s)+s(a+b+s+1),

with
\tilde{\tau}_{s}(x),\hat{\tau}_{s}(x)
inductively defined using
(
172
) and (
176
) (
177
). For
the initial step, set
\hat{T}_{0}:=T(a,b)
, let

\pi_{0,i}(x)=\pi_{i}(x;a,b)
be the classical Jacobi polynomials
and let

\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{0;ij}(x)=\int\pi_{0,i}(x)\pi_{0,j}(x)(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b},\quad i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

be the classical incomplete inner
products. We have
\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{0;ij}(1)=0,\;i\neq j
by Lemma

4.17
. The corresponding indefinite norms

\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{0;ii}(x)
are quasi-rational by inspection. We then use
(
176
) to inductively construct the quasi-polynomial
eigenfunctions
\hat{\Pi}_{s;i}(x),\;i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\setminus\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{s}\}
and
use (
177
), to
inductively construct the corresponding incomplete inner products

\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{s;i,j}(x)
. We then adjust the degrees, and normalize these
quasi-polynomials to monic form by setting

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{s;i}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{s}\left(\frac{i-\ell_{j}^{*}}{i-\ell_{j}}\right)\hat{\Pi}_{i+s},

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{s;ij}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{s}\left(\frac{i-\ell_{j}^{*}}{i-\ell_{j}}\right)^{2}\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{s;i+s,j+s},\quad s=1,\ldots,q.

Relation (
183
) follows immediately. Relation
(
181
) follows by (
175
) and by
induction. Arrange these operators into the
2q
-step chain of type
1,3 CDTs

\hat{T}_{s-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{s}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{s}}\tilde{T}_{s}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{s}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{s}}\hat{T}_{s},\quad\lambda_{s}=\lambda_{1}(\ell_{s};a,b),\;s=1,\ldots,q,

that connects the classical

\hat{T}_{0}
with the exceptional
\hat{T}_{q}
. Relation
(
182
) follows by the preceding Lemmas and by
induction.

Observe that the definition of
\hat{\tau}(x)
in (
172
) and

\hat{\pi}_{i}(x)
in (
176
) is just the
q=1
case of the
definitions in (
56
) and (
57
). The general
determinantal formula (
56
) for
\hat{\tau}(x)
and
(
57
) for
\hat{\pi}_{i}(x)
are justified by Sylvester’s
identity. The full argument is related in Section 3 of

[
25
]
.
∎

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
\hat{T}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau},{\hat{\alpha}},{\hat{\beta}})
be an exceptional operator with
monic quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions
\hat{\pi}_{i}(x),\;i\in\hat{I}_{1}
.
Let
k_{1},\ldots,k_{p}\in\hat{I}_{1}
be distinct. Then,

\tau=\hat{\tau}\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{p}}]

is a polynomial such that

\deg\tau=\deg\hat{\tau}+\sum_{i}k_{i}-\binom{p}{2}

and such that the leading coefficient has the form

\operatorname{\mathbf{C}}(\tau)=\prod_{1\leq i<j<p}(k_{j}-k_{i}).

Proof.

The proof of the first assertion is by induction. The base case

p=1
follows by Proposition
2.18
. Consider the type
1 chain

T_{0}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{1}}T_{1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{2}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{2}}\cdots\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{p-1}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{p-1}}T_{p-1}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda_{p}}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda_{p}}T_{p}
where
\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{1}(k_{j};\alpha,\beta)
, where

T_{j}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau_{j};{\hat{\alpha}}+j,{\hat{\beta}}+j)+\epsilon_{j},\;j=1,\ldots,p
. By
Proposition
4.13
and by (
142
), the rational
function

{\tilde{\pi}}_{j}(x):=\frac{\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{j+1}}]}{\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{j}}]}=\frac{\tau_{j+1}}{\tau_{j}},\quad j=1,\ldots,p-1

is an eigenfunction of
T_{j}
whose numerator and denominator are the

\tau
-functions of
T_{j+1}
and
T_{j}
, respectively. Having
established that
\tau_{j}=\hat{\tau}\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{j}}]
, it then follows that

\frac{\tau_{j+1}}{\tau_{j}}=\frac{\hat{\tau}\operatorname{Wr}[\hat{\pi}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{k_{j+1}}]}{\tau_{j}}.

This is the inductive step of the
argument. The assertions regarding degree and the leading
coefficient follow by Lemma
4.22
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

The classical
T(0,b)
is Darboux-connected to the exceptional

\hat{T}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau};0,b+2q)+q(q+b+1)
by a
2q
-step chain consisting
of type A CDTs as described in Lemma
8.12
. The form of

\hat{\tau}
in (
56
) and the quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions

\hat{\pi}_{i}(x),\;i\in\hat{I}_{1}
is justified by that Lemma. This
intermediate
\hat{T}
is then Darboux connected to

T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)+(p+q)(p+q+b+1)
by a
p
-step type 1 chain.
The formula for the quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions

\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}
of
T
given in (
60
) is justified
by Proposition
4.13
. The formula for
\tau
in
(
59
) follows by Lemma
8.13
. The same Lemma
justifies the degree formula (
62
) and the form of the
normalization coefficient in (
61
). Let

\hat{\rho}_{ij}(x),\rho_{ij}(x)
be the incomplete inner products
corresponding to the quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions of
\hat{T}
and

T
respectively. By (
183
),

\hat{\rho}_{ii}(1)=\hat{\chi}(i+q;L,a,b)^{2}\nu(i+q;a,b),\quad i\in I_{1}(\hat{T}).

Hence, by (
169
) of Lemma
8.7
,

\displaystyle\rho_{ii}(1)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k\in K}\left(\frac{i+p+q-k^{*}}{i+p+q-k}\right)\hat{\chi}(i+p+q;L,a,b)^{2}\nu(i+p+q;a,b),\quad i\in I_{1}(T),

\displaystyle=\kappa(i+p+q;K,L,a,b)\nu(i+p+q;a,b).

Define
\nu_{i},\;i\in I_{1}
as per (
63
). Hence, by
Proposition
6.11
and Proposition
6.13
,
the anti-derivative

\int\left(\pi_{i}(x)^{2}-\frac{\nu_{i}}{\nu(\alpha,\beta)}\right)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta},\quad\alpha=a+p,\;\beta+b+p+2q,

defines a
quasi-rational function in
\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{0,\beta+1}
that vanishes at

x=1
. In order to obtain the
(1+x)^{\alpha}
factor the asymptotic
behaviour of the antiderivative at
x=1
must be

C+O\left((1-x)^{\alpha+1}\right),
where
C
is a constant. Since this
anti-derivative vanishes at
x=1
, we must have
C=0
. Hence the
above anti-derivative is an element of
\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1}
, and
hence
\nu_{i}
, as defined above, are the norms of the
\pi_{i}
.
∎

.

.

7.9

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta\notin\mathbb{Z},\alpha+\beta+1\in\mathbb{Z}
be
a class C or CB exceptional operator and consider the type 2 RDT

T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{2,k}}}\xrightharpoonup{2,k}\hat{T}
where
k\in I_{2+}(T)
and

\hat{T}={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\pi_{2,k};\alpha-1,\beta-1)-\alpha-\beta
. Let
A_{2k}
be the
corresponding intertwiner as defined in (
142
), and

\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}}=\frac{A_{2}\pi_{i}}{i-j},\quad j=k-\alpha-\beta,\;i=j^{\star}=-j-1-\alpha-\beta=-k-1,\;

the indicated monic
eigenpolynomial of
\hat{T}
. Then,
\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}}
has zero norm, in the
sense that

\int\hat{\pi}_{{\hat{\imath}}}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{\alpha+1}(1+x)^{\beta+1}

defines a
quasi-rational function.

Proof.

This follows by (
169
) of Lemma
8.7
,
because in
this case,
i-j^{\star}=0
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.14
.

Since
a,b\notin\mathbb{Z}
, we have
\tilde{K}_{12}\cap\tilde{K}_{34}=\emptyset
.
Moreover, by the assumption that
\tilde{K}_{12}
and
\tilde{K}_{34}
are
disjoint unions, the eigenvalue set
\{\lambda_{1}(k;a,b):k\in\tilde{K}\}

consists of
p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}+p_{4}
distinct eigenvalues. Hence, we can
apply Propositions
4.11
and
7.1
to
construct an RDT chain connecting the classical
T(a,b)
to the
exceptional
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)+(p_{1}-p_{2})(1+a+b+p_{1}-p_{2})
. In the
class C case, the chain consists of
p_{1}
type 1 RDTs,
p_{2}
type 2
RDTs, and
p_{3}
type 3 RDTS. By Proposition
7.18
, the
corresponding flips are

\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\to\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\to\boxcirc,\boxplus\to\boxminus
, respectively. In the most general CB case, the chain
consists of
p_{1}
type 1 RDTs,
p_{2}
type 2 RDTs,
p_{3}
type 3 RDTS,
and
p_{4}
type 4 RDTS. The corresponding flips are

\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\to\boxtimes,\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\to\boxcirc,\boxdiv\to\boxminus,\boxdiv\to\boxplus
, respectively. The form of
\alpha,\beta
in
(
42
) is justified by repeated application of Proposition

7.1
and the table (
140
). In a nutshell:

(\alpha,\beta)-(a,b)=p_{1}(+1,+1)+p_{2}(-1,-1)+p_{3}(-1,1)+p_{4}(1,-1).

By Proposition
7.15
and because the target labels of
the above flips are all simple, the relationship between
I_{12}
,
the combined index set of
{T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta)
and
{\check{I}}_{12}
, the
combined index set of
T(a,b)
is

I_{12}\supset{\check{I}}_{12}-p_{1}+p_{2}
. The relation isn’t a simple
shift because the flip
\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\to\boxtimes
destroys a

\boxtimes
label and moves a
\boxcirc
label from
I_{1+}
to

I_{1-}
. This justifies the forms of
I_{1-}
and
I_{1+}
in
(
76
). The other index transformations detailed in
(
76
) are justified in a similar manner.

The formula (
78
) for the corresponding Jacobi
quasi-polynomials is justified by Proposition
4.13
,
Lemma
4.22
, and Proposition
7.15
.
Proposition
4.13
accounts for the ratio of the
Wronskians, while Proposition
7.15
accounts for the
degree shift between
\pi_{i}
and
{\check{\pi}}_{i+p_{1}-p_{2}}
. The factorization
gauge at each step of the chain is determined by (
142
).
The factor
(x-1)^{p_{2}+p_{3}}(x+1)^{p_{2}+p_{4}}
in (
47
) is just a
product of the factorization gauge functions in the chain: the type
3 Jacobi RDTs utilize a gauge factor of
b(x)=x-1
, the type 4
Jacobi RDTs utilize a gauge factor of
b(x)=x+1
, the type 2 RDT
has a gauge factor of
b(x)=(x-1)(x+1)
, and the type 1
Jacobi RDTs utilize a gauge factor of
b(x)=1
. Lemma
4.22

accounts for the form of the normalizing factor

\prod_{k\in\tilde{K}}(i+p_{1}-p_{2}-k)
. The presence of this product in the
denominator ensures that
\pi_{i}
is monic. Observe that

(i+p_{1}-p_{2})^{*}=-i-1-p_{1}+p_{2}-a-b=-i-1-\alpha-\beta+p_{1}-p_{2}=i^{\star}+p_{1}+p_{2}.

Hence,

\mathfrak{u}(i+p_{1}-p_{2})=\begin{cases}i+p_{1}-p_{2}&\text{ if }i\in I_{1+},\\
i^{\star}+p_{1}-p_{2}&\text{ if }i\in I_{1-}.\end{cases}

This justifies the
\mathfrak{u}(i+p_{1}-p_{2})
index in the
Wronskian formula (
78
). The mapping

{\check{\pi}}_{(i+p_{1}-p_{2})^{*}}\to\pi_{i},\;i\in{\rm{I}}_{1-}
describe the
state-adding transformations
\mathrlap{\boxcirc}\boxtimes\to\boxcirc
indexed by
K_{2}
.

Next, we justify the form of
\tau(x)
given in (
77
).
Having established the validity of (
78
), Proposition

2.18
and, in particular, formula (
27
)
indicate that the
\tau(x)
in question is the polynomial part of
the Wronskian
\operatorname{Wr}[\phi(K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},K_{4};a,b)]
. Write

\operatorname{Wr}[\phi(K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},K_{4};a,b)]=(1-x)^{A}(1+x)^{B}\tau(x),

where

\tau(x)
is a polynomial with no zeros at
x=\pm 1
. The forms of

A
and
B
shown in (
77
) are a consequence of Lemma

4.20
. The degree formula (
48
) is a
consequence of Lemma
4.22
.

By (
110
) of Lemma
4.17
, we have

\int\pi_{i}(x)\pi_{j}(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1},\quad i\neq j\in I_{1}(T_{0}).

Let
\kappa(z;K,a,b)
be as in
(
50
). For
i\in I_{1+}
, repeated application of Lemma

8.7
shows that the anti-derivative

\int\pi_{i}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}-\kappa(i+p_{1}-p_{2};K,a,b){\check{\pi}}_{i+p_{1}-p_{2}}(x)^{2}(1-x)^{a}(1+x)^{b}

is quasi-rational. The
desired conclusion now follows by Proposition
6.14
.
If
i\in I_{1-}
, then the desired conclusion follows by Lemma

8.14
and repeated application of Lemma

8.7
.
∎

Proof of Propositions
3.15
and
3.16
.

By Theorem
2.34
, by Propositions
7.8

7.9
and by Proposition
7.18
, the given
T
is
Darboux-connected to a classical

T(a,b),\;a,b\notin\mathbb{Z},\;a-b\in\{-1,0,1\}
. Hence, by
Proposition
7.18
,
\Lambda_{T}
is a finite modification of
the corresponding
\Lambda(a,b)
, as described in Remark
3.8
.
The construction and uniqueness of the parameters
K,a,b
is
spelled out in that same remark. The form of
\tau
then follows by
the argument detailed about in the proof of Proposition

3.2
.
∎

8.6.
Proofs of results on the degenerate class

Lemma 8.15
.

Let
T={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tau;\alpha,\beta),\;\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be a class D exceptional
operator with
\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
the corresponding
quasi-polynomial eigenfunctions, relative to some choice of
normalization. Then, for
i\neq j
, the incomplete inner products

(184)

\rho_{ij}(x):=\int_{-1}^{x}\pi_{i}(x)\pi_{j}(x)(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta},\quad i,j\in I_{1}(T),

are quasi-rational functions that vanish at
x=-1
. Indeed,

(185)

\rho_{ij}(x)=\frac{\operatorname{Wr}_{x}[\pi_{i}(x),\pi_{j}(x)](1-x)^{\alpha+1}(1+x)^{\beta+1}}{(j-i)(i+j+\alpha+\beta+1)}\in\mathrm{q}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha+1,\beta+1}.

Proof.

This follows by (
110
) of Lemma
4.17
.
∎

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
T,\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T),\;\rho_{ij}(x),\;i,j\in I_{1}(T)
be
as in Lemma
8.15
, and suppose that the indefinite norms

\rho_{ii}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
define quasi-rational functions that
vanish at
x=-1
. Then, the corresponding norms are given by

\nu_{i}=\rho_{ii}(1),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
.

Proof.

See the proof of Lemma
8.9
.
∎

Lemma 8.17
.

Let
T,\pi_{i}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
be as in Lemma
8.15
and

\rho_{ij}(x),\;i,j\in I_{1}(T)
as in (
184
). Suppose
that the indefinite norms
\rho_{ii}(x),\;i\in I_{1}(T)
define
rational functions that vanish at
x=-1
. Fix an
\ell\in I_{1}(T)
and set

(186)

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}(x)

\displaystyle=\tau(x)\pi_{\ell}(x),

\displaystyle\tilde{T}

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\tilde{\tau};\alpha+1,\beta+1)+\alpha+\beta+2,

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}(x)

\displaystyle=\tau(x)(t+\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)),\quad t\notin\{0,-\rho_{\ell\ell}(1)\}

\displaystyle\hat{T}

\displaystyle={T_{\mathrm{rg}}}(\hat{\tau};\alpha,\beta).

Then,
\tilde{\tau}(x),\hat{\tau}(x)
are polynomials and

T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{1,\ell}}}\xrightharpoonup{1,\ell}\tilde{T}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{1,\ell}}}\xrightharpoonup{1,\ell}\hat{T}
is a type D CDT with label
transformations
\boxcirc\to\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\to\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
, as described in
Lemma
7.30
. Moreover,

(187)

\deg\hat{\tau}=\deg\tau+2\ell+\alpha+\beta+1.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.1
and by (
32
),

T\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\tilde{T},\;\lambda=\lambda_{1}(\ell;\alpha,\beta)
is a type 1 RDT. By
inspection,
\deg\tilde{\tau}=\deg\tau+\ell
. By
(
143
), the inverse seed eigenfunction is

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}_{2-}(x)=\frac{\tau(x)}{\tilde{\tau}(x)}(1-x)^{-\alpha-1}(1+x)^{-\beta-1},

By assumption, by Lemma
6.9
, and by the restrictions
imposed on
t
, we have
t+\rho_{\ell\ell}(\pm 1)\neq 0
. Hence, by
Proposition
4.25
, the rational

\tilde{\phi}_{2+}:=(t+\rho_{\ell\ell}(x))\tilde{\phi}_{2-}(x)
is also a type 2
eigenfunction of
\tilde{T}
. By construction,

\deg\rho_{\ell\ell}=2\ell+\alpha+\beta+1
. Hence,

\deg\tilde{\phi}_{2+}=\ell-1
; this implies (
187
). By
Proposition
7.1
,
\tilde{\tau}/\hat{\tau}
is a
quasi-polynomial eigenfunction of
\hat{T}
with eigenvalue

\lambda
. Since
\deg(\tilde{\tau}/\hat{\tau})=-\ell-\alpha-\beta-1=\ell^{\star}
,
the type 2 RDT
\tilde{T}\mathrlap{\xleftharpoondown{\phantom{\lambda}}}\xrightharpoonup{\lambda}\hat{T}
corresponds to the label
transformation
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\sqbullet$}\crcr}}}\to\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\Box$\cr\hbox{$\mkern 2.5mu\smalltriangledown$}\crcr}}}
, as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma 8.18
.

. Set

(188)

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}_{i}(x;t)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}(x)-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}(x)}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)}\pi_{\ell}(x),\quad i\in I_{1}(T)

(189)

\displaystyle=\rho_{ij}-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}(x)\rho_{j\ell}(x)}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}(x)},\quad i,j\in I_{1}(T)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{t\rho_{\ell\ell}(1)}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}(1)}&\text{ if }i=j=\ell,\\
\rho_{ij}(1)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

\displaystyle w_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\tau}}-\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau},

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hat{\tau}^{\prime}}{\hat{\tau}}+\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\tau}},

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=D_{x}-w_{1},

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-1)(D_{x}-\hat{w}_{1}),

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle B_{2}

Then, by Proposition

,

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=B_{1}A_{1}+\lambda,

\displaystyle\tilde{T}

\displaystyle=A_{1}B_{1}+\lambda,

\displaystyle\tilde{T}

\displaystyle\hat{T}

\displaystyle=A_{2}B_{2}+\lambda.

By (

),

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\tau}^{\prime}}{\hat{\tau}}=\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}+\frac{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}

\displaystyle A_{2}A_{1}

\displaystyle=B_{1}A_{1}-\frac{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}A_{1}=T-\lambda-\frac{\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}A_{1}

If
i\neq\ell
, then

\displaystyle A_{2}A_{1}\pi_{i}

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{1}(i;\alpha,\beta)-\lambda_{1}(\ell;\alpha,\beta))\pi_{i}-\frac{\pi_{\ell}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}\operatorname{Wr}[\pi_{\ell},\pi_{i}](1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}\quad\text{by
~\eqref{eq:rhointD}},

\displaystyle=(i-\ell)(i+\ell+\alpha+\beta+1)\left(\pi_{i}-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}\pi_{\ell}\right)\quad\text{by

\displaystyle=(i-\ell)(i+\ell+\alpha+\beta+1)\hat{\pi}_{i}.

If

(

). For

(196)

\hat{\Pi}_{\ell}=\frac{t\pi_{\ell}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}.

, and because

, as
defined above. Relations (

)
and (

. by

25
25

25

The derivative is with respect to

\displaystyle(1-x)^{-\alpha}(1+x)^{-\beta}\hat{\mathrm{P}}_{ij}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(1-x)^{-\alpha}(1+x)^{-\beta}\left(\rho_{ij}^{\prime}-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}^{\prime}\rho_{j\ell}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}-\frac{\rho_{i\ell}^{\prime}\rho_{j\ell}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}+\frac{\rho_{i\ell}\rho_{j\ell}\rho_{\ell\ell}^{\prime}}{(t+\rho_{\ell\ell})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}\pi_{j}-\frac{\pi_{i}\pi_{\ell}\rho_{j\ell}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}-\frac{\pi_{j}\pi_{\ell}\rho_{i\ell}}{t+\rho_{\ell\ell}}+\frac{\pi_{\ell}\pi_{\ell}\rho_{i\ell}\rho_{j\ell}}{(t+\rho_{\ell\ell})^{2}}

Lemma 8.23
.

let

, let
